Her face moved slowly from one decided expression to another equally determined, lacking the fluidity of emotion that characterized the softer sex. Those who looked at her once generally looked twice; even passersby in the street halted to gaze an instant longer at the firm, severe, dignified woman who never yielded in courtesy nor paused in her straight-onward course toward the clearly defined end she proposed.

She was handsomely dressed in stout black silk, not a thread worn or discolored, a testament to a discipline that bordered on the ascetic. She mended a large, long tablecloth of the finest texture, holding it up against the light to discover thin places requiring her delicate care. There was not a book in the room save for Matthew Henry's Bible commentaries, six volumes lying in the center of the massive sideboard, flanked by a tea-urn and a lamp. In a remote apartment, the clatter of a piano filled the air. Someone was practicing a *morceau de salon*, playing it with reckless speed, every third note on an average either indistinct or wholly missed, the loud chords at the end half of them false, yet perfectly satisfactory to the performer.

Mrs. Thornton heard a step, decisive and familiar, pass the dining-room door. *"John! Is that you?"*

Her son opened the door, revealing himself. *"What has brought you home so early? I thought you were going to tea with that friend of Mr. Bell's; that Mr. Hale?"*

*"Dress! Humph! When I was a girl, young men were satisfied with dressing once in a day. Why should you dress to go and take a cup of tea with an old parson?"*

*"I am not easily caught, mother, as I think you know. But I must not have Miss Hale spoken of in that way, which, you know, is offensive to me. I never was aware of any young lady trying to catch me yet, nor do I believe that any one has ever given themselves that useless trouble."*

Mrs. Thornton did not choose to yield the point to her son; pride, that ancient armor of her sex, held her fast. *"Well! I only say, take care. Perhaps our Milton girls have too much spirit and good feeling to go angling after husbands; but this Miss Hale comes out of the aristocratic counties, where, if all tales be true, rich husbands are reckoned prizes."*

Mr. Thornton's brow contracted, and he came a step forward into the room. *"Mother"* (with a short, scornful laugh), *"you will make me confess. The only time I saw Miss Hale, she treated me with a haughty civility which had a strong flavour of contempt in it. She held herself aloof from me as if she had been a queen, and I her humble, unwashed vassal. Be easy, mother."*

*"No! I am not easy nor content either. What business had she, a renegade clergyman's daughter, to turn up her nose at you! I would dress for none of them—a saucy set! if I were you."*

As he was leaving the room, he added, *"Mr. Hale is good, and gentle, and learned. He is not saucy. As for Mrs. Hale, I will

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of the industrial age—thoroughly indifferent to the quiet suffocations of its women. She stood by the tea-table in a light-coloured muslin gown, the fabric stained faintly with the pink of her own flushed cheeks, her round ivory hands moving with a dainty, noiseless precision among the cups. A bracelet encircled one tapering arm, threatening to slide down over her wrist; she pushed it up impatiently until it tightened her soft flesh, then marked the loosening, the inevitable fall. It was a futile gesture, a pantomime of control in a world that demanded submission.

Mr. Thornton watched the replacement of this troublesome ornament with far more attention than he listened to her father. His gaze was fixed on the mechanics of her constraint, fascinated by the way she fought the weight of gold against the fluidity of her skin. He could almost have exclaimed, "There it goes again!" The sight of her struggling with her own adornment mirrored the struggle he perceived in the very air of the room: a tension between the raw, crushing power of industry and the fragile, enduring humanity trying to survive within it.

He observed the contrast between the two men with a clarity that cut through the dim light. Mr. Hale was slight, appearing taller than he was due to the economy of his frame, his face a map of soft, wavering lines that trembled with every fluctuating emotion. His eyelids were large and arched, lending his eyes a peculiar, almost feminine languor. In stark opposition stood Mr. Thornton, a man of massive, solid frame whose straight brows fell low over clear, deep-set eyes that seemed intent on penetrating the very core of truth. Where Hale's features were carved from marble yet softened by dreams, Thornton's were defined by firm, uncompromising lines, particularly about the lips which were slightly compressed. When a rare, bright smile broke through the severity of his expression, it transformed his face instantly from a mask of resolve into a beacon of honest, fearless joy—a light Margaret found herself drawn to, inexplicable and magnetic.

"And this imagination of power, this practical realisation of a gigantic thought, came out of one man's brain in our good town," Mr. Thornton declared, his voice resonant with the confidence of someone who believed in the inevitability of progress. "That very man has it within him to mount, step by step, on each wonder he achieves to higher marvels still. And I'll be bound to say, we have many among us who, if he were gone, could spring into the breach and carry on the war which compels, and shall compel, all material power to yield to science."

His words hung in the air, heavy with the arrogance of triumph. Margaret looked up, her eyes wide with inquiring wonder as he quoted the old ballad of Chevy Chase, bridging the gap between the cog-wheels of the factory floor and the romanticized chivalry of the past.

"It is no boast of mine," replied Mr. Thornton, dismissing the notion of pride with a wave of his hand. "It is plain matter-of-fact. I won't deny that I am proud of belonging to a town—or perhaps I should rather say a district—the necessities of which give birth to such grandeur of conception. I would rather be a man toiling, suffering—nay, failing and successless—here, than lead a dull prosperous life in the old worn grooves of what you call more aristocratic society down in the South, with their slow days of careless ease. One may be clogged with honey and unable to rise and fly."

"You are mistaken," said Margaret, the defense of her homeland rising in her throat with a vehemence that brought the color into her cheeks and angry tears to her eyes. "You do not know anything about the South. If there is less adventure or less progress—from the gambling spirit of trade, which seems requisite to force out these wonderful inventions—there is less suffering also. I see men here going about in the streets who look ground down by some pinching sorrow or care—who are not only sufferers but haters. Now, in the South we have our poor, but there is not that terrible expression in their countenances of a sullen sense of injustice which I see here. You do not know the South, Mr. Thornton," she concluded, collapsing into a determined silence, furious with herself for having spoken so freely, exposing the wounds she tried so hard to hide.

"And may I say you do not know the North?" asked he, his tone shifting to an inexpressible gentleness as he sensed the pain he had caused. She remained resolutely silent, yearning after the lovely haunts of Hampshire with a passion that made her feel her voice would be unsteady and trembling if she dared to speak.

"At any rate, Mr. Thornton," said Mrs. Hale, attempting to steer the conversation toward safer ground, "you will allow that Milton is a much more smoky, dirty town than you will ever meet with in the South."

"I'm afraid I must give up its cleanliness," said Mr. Thornton, flashing a quick, gleaming smile that belied the seriousness of his stance. "But we are bidden by parliament to burn our own smoke; so I suppose, like good little children, we shall do as we are bid—some time."

"But I think you told me you had altered your chimneys so as to consume the smoke, did you not?" asked Mr. Hale.

"Mine were altered by my own will, before parliament meddled with the affair. It was an immediate outlay, but it repays me in the saving of coal. I'm not sure whether I should have done it, if I had waited until the act was passed. At any rate, I should have waited to be informed against and fined, and given all the trouble in yielding that I legally could. But all laws which depend for their enforcement upon informers and fines, become inert from the odiousness of the machinery. I doubt if there has been a chimney in Milton informed against for five years past, although some are constantly sending out one-third of their coal in what is called here unparliamentary smoke."

"I only know it is impossible to keep the muslin blinds clean here above a week together; and at Helstone we have had them up for a month or more, and they have not looked dirty at the end of that time. And as for hands—Margaret, how many times did you say you had washed your hands this morning before twelve o'clock? Three times, was it not?"

"Yes, mamma."

"You seem to have a strong objection to acts of parliament and all legislation affecting your mode of management down here at Milton," said Mr. Hale.

"Yes, I have; and many others have as well. And with justice, I think. The whole machinery—I don't mean the wood and iron machinery now—of the cotton trade is so new that it is no wonder if it does not work well in every part all at once. Seventy years ago what was it? And now what it is not? Raw, crude materials came together; men of the same level, as regarded education and station, took suddenly the different position of masters and men, owing to the motherwit, as regarded opportunities and probabilities, which distinguished some, and made them far-seeing as to what great future lay concealed in that rude model of Sir Richard Arkwright's. The rapid development of what might be called a new trade, gave those early masters enormous power of wealth and command. I don't mean merely over the workmen; I mean over purchasers—over the whole world's market. Why, I may give you, as an instance, an advertisement, inserted not fifty years ago in a Milton paper, that so-and-so (one of the half-dozen calico-printers of the time) would close his warehouse at noon each day; therefore, that all purchasers must come before that hour. Fancy a man dictating in this manner the time when he would sell and when he would not sell. Now, I believe, if a good customer chose to come at midnight, I should get up, and stand hat in hand to receive his orders."

Margaret's lip curled, a flash of defiance in the dim light, but she was compelled to listen; she could no longer abstract herself in her own thoughts. The sheer audacity of the statement, the casual dismissal of human dignity in favor of commercial efficiency, gnawed at her.

"I only name such things to show what almost unlimited power the manufacturers had about the beginning of this century. The men were rendered dizzy by it. Because a man was successful in his ventures, there was no reason that in all other things his mind should be well-balanced. On the contrary, his sense of justice, and his simplicity, were often utterly smothered under the glut of wealth that came down upon him; and they tell strange tales of the wild extravagance of living indulged in on gala-days by those early cotton-lords. There can be no doubt, too, of the tyranny they exercised over their work-people. You know the proverb, Mr. Hale, 'Set a beggar on horseback, and he'll ride to the devil,'—well, some of these early manufacturers did ride to the devil in a magnificent style—crushing human bone and flesh under their horses' hoofs without remorse. But by-and-by came a reaction; there were more factories, more masters; more men were wanted. The power of masters and men became more evenly balanced; and now the battle is pretty fairly waged between us. We will hardly submit to the decision of an umpire, much less to the interference of a medd

"There is iron in all our blood, and a grain or two perhaps is good; but his, he makes me harshly feel, has got a little too much of steel."

"Margaret!" Mr. Hale called out, returning from the stairs where he had shown his guest below. "I couldn't help watching your face with some anxiety when Mr. Thornton confessed to being a shop-boy. I knew the truth from Mr. Bell, so I anticipated the moment; yet I half expected you to rise and flee the room."

"Oh, Papa! Do you truly think me so foolish?" Margaret retorted, her voice brightening. "I preferred that admission above anything else he said. Every other word revolted me for its hardness, but speaking of himself so simply—stripped of the pretense that usually masks the vulgarity of shop-folk—and with such tender reverence for his mother... it made me stay. Not when he boasted of Milton as if no other place existed, nor when he claimed to despise those who squandered their lives without considering it his duty to lift them up, despite the very training his mother gave him. No, his honesty about being a shop-boy was the most compelling thing he ever said."

"I am surprised at you, Margaret," her mother interjected, smoothing her apron. "You who constantly accused people of being 'shoppy' at Helstone! I don't think, Mr. Hale, you were wise to introduce someone without revealing his background. I was terrified of showing how shocked I was by his words. His father 'dying in miserable circumstances.' Why, it might have been in the workhouse."

"It may have been worse than the workhouse," her husband replied gently. "Mr. Bell told me much of his past before we arrived. He gambled wildly, failed, and then took his own life, unable to bear the disgrace. All his former friends vanished when the truth of his dishonest dealings emerged—those wild, desperate struggles using other people's money to reclaim a modest fortune. No one stepped forward to aid the mother and this boy. There was another child, a girl, too young to work, yet she had to be fed. Friends did not appear immediately, and Mrs. Thornton is certainly not one to wait for tardy kindness. So they fled Milton. I knew he had taken a job in a shop, and that his wages, combined with a small inheritance, sustained them for years. Mr. Bell said they subsisted on water-porridge for a long stretch—how, he could not say. Long after creditors had abandoned hope of repayment following the father's suicide, this young man returned to Milton. He went quietly to each creditor, paying the first installment owed. There was no drama, no assembly of debtors; it was done in silence until every penny was settled. This was aided significantly by one of the creditors, a crusty old man, who took Mr. Thornton in as a partner."

"That truly is fine," Margaret murmured. "What a pity such a noble nature should be tarnished by his position as a Milton manufacturer."

"How tarnished?" her father asked.

"Oh, Papa, by measuring everything against the standard of wealth. When he spoke of mechanical powers, he viewed them solely as new methods to expand trade and accumulate money. And the poor men around him? They were poor because they were vicious—cast aside from his sympathies because they lacked his 'iron nature,' the specific capabilities that allowed him to become rich."

"Not vicious; he never used that word. He called them improvident and self-indulgent."

Meanwhile, Margaret began gathering her mother's sewing materials, preparing for bed. Just as she reached the door, she paused. She felt compelled to offer an acknowledgment that would please her father, yet to be fully truthful, it required admitting a slight irritation. Out it came.

"Papa, I do think Mr. Thornton a very remarkable man; but personally, I don't like him at all."

"And I do!" her father laughed warmly. "Personally, as you call it, and all. I don't elevate him to a hero, or anything of that sort. Good-night, child. Your mother looks dreadingly tired tonight, Margaret."

Margaret had noted her mother's exhausted countenance with growing alarm for some time. Her father's comment sent a dim, heavy fear settling onto her heart. Life in Milton was a stark departure from the perpetual freshness of Helstone, where the air seemed alive with revitalizing principles. Here, the atmosphere felt stagnant, stripped of renewal. Domestic worries pressed upon the women of the household in a new, sordid intensity, raising genuine fears for her mother's health. There were other troubling signs: mysterious consultations between Mrs. Hale and Dixon in the bedroom, followed by Dixon emerging weeping and irritable whenever her mistress suffered. Once, Margaret had found her mother on her knees shortly after Dixon left, whispering prayers for strength to endure severe physical pain. Margaret yearned to restore the intimate bond fractured by her long absence at Aunt Shaw's, striving with gentle touches and soft words to reach the deepest corner of her mother's heart. Yet, though she received affection in abundance, she sensed a secret withheld—one bearing serious implications for her mother's condition. She lay awake long into the night, plotting how to mitigate the toxic influence of their Milton existence. She considered hiring a servant to take Dixon permanently off her hands, ensuring her mother received the constant personal attention she had always known.

For several days, visiting registration offices and encountering improbable souls consumed Margaret's time and thoughts. One afternoon, she encountered Bessy Higgins in the street and stopped to speak.

"Well, Bessy, how are you? Better, I hope, now the wind has changed."

"Better and not better, if you know what that means," Bessy replied, her voice thin.

"Not exactly," Margaret smiled.

"I'm better in that I'm not torn apart by coughing all night, but I'm weary and tired of Milton, longing for the Land of Beulah. And when I realize I'm drifting further away, my heart sinks, and I'm worse."

Margaret turned to walk beside the girl, whose progress was feeble. For a moment, neither spoke. Finally, Margaret whispered, "Bessy, do you wish to die?" She recoiled from the thought herself, clinging to life with the instinctive fervor of youth.

Bessy remained silent for a moment. Then, "If you'd led the life I have, grown as weary of it as I have, and thought at times, 'Maybe it'll last for fifty or sixty years—it does with some,' and felt dizzy and dazed as each of those sixty years spun around me, mocking me with its endless hours and minutes... oh, wench! I tell thee, thou'd be glad enough when the doctor said he feared thou'd never see another winter."

"Why, Bessy, what kind of a life has yours been?"

"Nought worse than many others', I reckon. Only I fretted against it, and they didn't."

"But what was it? You know, I'm a stranger here, so perhaps I'm not quick enough to understand what you mean."

"If you'd come to our house when you promised, I could maybe have told you. But Father says you're just like the rest of them; it's out of sight, out of mind with you."

"I don't know who the rest are; and I've been very busy. To tell the truth, I had forgotten my promise—"

"You offered it; we asked for nothing," Bessy insisted.

"I forgot what I said for the time," Margaret continued calmly. "I would have remembered when I was less busy. May I go with you now?"

Bessy glanced sharply at Margaret's face, searching for sincerity. The hardness in her eyes softened into a wistful longing under Margaret's gentle gaze.

"I have none so many to care for me; if you care, you may come."

They walked on in silence. Turning up a narrow court from a squalid street, Bessy spoke. "You won't be daunted if Father is home and speaks a bit gruffly at first. He took a liking to you, you see, and thought a lot of your coming to see us. Just because he likes you, he was vexed and upset."

"Don't fear, Bessy."

But Nicholas was not home when they entered. A large, unkempt girl, older than Bessy but taller and stronger, was busy at the wash-tub, knocking about the furniture with rough capability, creating such a din that Margaret shrank back in sympathy for poor Bessy, who collapsed onto the nearest chair, utterly spent. Margaret asked the sister for water. While the girl ran to fetch it—clattering fire-irons and tripping over chairs—Margaret loosened Bessy's bonnet strings to ease her labored breathing.

"Do you think such a life as this is worth caring for?" Bessy gasped finally. Margaret said nothing, holding the water to her lips. Bessy drank greedily, then fell back, closing her eyes. Margaret heard her muttering, "They shall hunger no more, neither thirst any more: neither shall the sun light on them, nor any heat."

Margaret leaned forward. "Bessy, don't be impatient with your life, whatever it is—or may have been. Remember who gave it you, and made it what it is!"

She was startled by Nicholas's voice behind her. He had entered unnoticed.

"Now, I'll not have my wench preached to. She's bad enough, as it is, with her Methodist fancies and her visions of cities

The morning light filtered through the high windows of the Thornton parlor, illuminating dust motes that danced in the stagnant air, much like the unresolved tension between mother and son. Mr. Thornton had spent considerable effort attempting to elevate his mother to a plane of mere civility, a task akin to polishing a stone that naturally preferred to remain rough. Mrs. Thornton rarely ventured beyond the sanctuary of her own home, and when she did, she approached social obligations with the gravity of a military campaign. Her son had provided a carriage, yet she had steadfastly refused to allow him to maintain horses for it; for solemn occasions alone warranted the hiring of vehicles, ensuring that every step of her departure and arrival was marked by a deliberate, temporary expenditure.

For three days, indeed nearly a fortnight prior, she had ridden out in borrowed steeds, systematically exhausting her circle of acquaintances until they were left breathless, forcing them to incur similar expenses in their attempts to reciprocate. Yet, Crampton lay too distant for her weary feet, prompting repeated inquiries from her regarding whether her desire to visit the Hales was robust enough to justify the cost of a cab. She harbored a secret relief that it might not be; as she reasoned aloud, "Why should I cultivate friendships and intimacies with every teacher and master in Milton? Next, he will expect me to call upon Fanny's dancing-master's wife!"

"And so I would, mother," John countered gently, "were Mr. Mason and his wife friendless in this strange land, much as the Hales are."

"Oh! Do not speak so hastily," she retorted, her composure cracking slightly. "I am departing tomorrow. My sole intention was to ensure you understood the logistics."

"If you are going tomorrow, I shall order the horses."

"Nonsense, John. One would think you were carved from pure gold."

"Not quite, yet. However, regarding the horses, I am resolved. Recall the last time you rode in a cab; you returned home suffering from a severe headache caused by the violent jolting."

"I never complained of it, I assure you."

"No! My mother is not a creature of complaint," he declared, a flicker of pride brightening his features. "Precisely because you do not complain, I have all the greater responsibility to safeguard your health. Consider Fanny there; a measure of hardship would prove beneficial to her constitution."

"She is not forged from the same iron as you, John. She could not withstand such rigors."

Mrs. Thornton fell silent, her previous words striking a chord that mortified her. She possessed an unconscious disdain for weakness, particularly in those areas where both she and her son displayed formidable strength. Mrs. Thornton was not a woman inclined toward prolonged introspection or debate; her rapid judgment and unwavering resolve substituted for endless internal deliberation. Instinctively, she perceived that nothing could fortify Fanny to endure trials with patience or confront obstacles with courage. Though she winced inwardly at this admission concerning her daughter, it transformed into a specific, pitying tenderness in her demeanor—the exact manner in which mothers treat their frail, sickly offspring.

To a casual observer, Mrs. Thornton's behavior might have suggested a far deeper reservoir of love for Fanny than for John. Such a conclusion, however, would have been profoundly erroneous. The boldness with which mother and son articulated unpalatable truths to one another demonstrated a reliance on the solid bedrock of their shared souls. In contrast, the uneasy tenderness Mrs. Thornton displayed toward her daughter, coupled with the shameful attempt to conceal Fanny's poverty beneath the grandeur of her own innate qualities—which she held in such high esteem in others—betrayed a lack of a secure foundation for her affection. She never addressed her son by any name other than "John"; terms of endearment like "love" and "dear" were exclusively reserved for Fanny. Yet, her heart gave thanks for her son day and night, and she strode proudly among the women of Milton solely for his sake.

"Fanny dear! I shall arrange for horses to the carriage today to visit these Hales. Pray, go and see Nurse; it lies in the same direction, and she is invariably delighted to see you. You might proceed there while I attend to Mrs. Hale."

"Oh! Mamma, it is such a long way, and I am so exhausted."

"With what?" Mrs. Thornton queried, her brow furrowing slightly.

"I do not know—the weather, I suppose. It feels so... relaxing."

"Could you not request that Nurse come here? The carriage could fetch her, allowing her to spend the remainder of the day here, which I am certain she would appreciate."

Mrs. Thornton remained silent, laying her embroidery down upon the table as if contemplating a difficult equation. "It will be a considerable distance for her to walk back at night," she finally remarked.

"Oh, but I shall send her home in a cab. I never considered her walking."

At that precise moment, Mr. Thornton entered, just before his departure for the mill. "Mother! I scarcely need remind you that if there is any trivial matter that could serve Mrs. Hale as an invalid, you will certainly offer it, I am sure."

"If I can ascertain what it is, I will. However, having never been ill myself, I am not particularly versed in the whims of invalids."

"Well! Here stands Fanny, who is seldom free from some ailment. She ought to be able to suggest something, perhaps—will you not, Fan?"

"I do not always suffer from ailments," Fanny replied pettishly. "And I am not accompanying Mother. I have a headache today, and I shall not go out."

Mr. Thornton appeared annoyed. His mother's gaze was fixed intently on her needlework, where she stitched away with industrious fervor. "Fanny! I require you to go," he stated authoritatively. "It will benefit you rather than harm you. You will oblige me by complying, without further discussion."

He departed abruptly from the room immediately after issuing this command. Had he lingered even a moment longer, Fanny would have burst into tears at his tone of authority, even when he utilized the phrase, "You will oblige me." As it stood, she merely grumbled. "John always speaks as though I imagine I am ill, and I am certain I never indulge in such fantasies. Who are these Hales that he makes such a fuss about?"

"Fanny, do not speak disparagingly of your brother. He possesses valid reasons of some kind, or he would not wish us to visit. Hasten and prepare your things."

Yet, the brief altercation between her son and her daughter failed to soften Mrs. Thornton's disposition toward "these Hales." Her jealous heart replayed her daughter's query, "Who are they, that he is so eager for us to pay them such attention?" It resurfaced like a persistent refrain in a song, long after Fanny had forgotten the incident amidst the pleasant excitement of admiring a new bonnet in the mirror.

Mrs. Thornton was inherently shy. Only in recent years had she possessed sufficient leisure to engage with society, and even then, she found little joy in it. She derived satisfaction from hosting dinners and critiquing the culinary efforts of others. However, the act of initiating acquaintance with strangers was a vastly different endeavor. She felt profoundly ill-at-ease, appearing more stern and forbidding than usual as she crossed the threshold into the Hales' modest drawing-room.

Margaret was diligently embroidering a small piece of cambric intended for an article of dress for Edith's anticipated baby—"Flimsy, useless work," Mrs. Thornton chided silently. She preferred Mrs. Hale's double knitting far more; that craft possessed a sensible utility. The room was cluttered with knick-knacks that demanded extensive dusting, and for people of limited means, time was synonymous with currency. These observations swirled through her mind as she engaged in her stately discourse with Mrs. Hale, uttering the stereotypical platitudes that most people recite with their senses effectively blindfolded.

Mrs. Hale, however, exerted considerably more energy in her responses, captivated by a genuine piece of antique lace Mrs. Thornton wore. "Lace," she later noted to Dixon, "of that old English point which has not been manufactured for seventy years, nor can it be purchased. It must be an heirloom, proving she possessed ancestors." Consequently, the owner of this ancestral treasure deemed herself worthy of more than the languid exertion required to be agreeable to a visitor, which would otherwise have been the limit of Mrs. Hale's conversational efforts.

Presently, Margaret, straining her brain to converse with Fanny, overheard her mother and Mrs. Hale descending into the interminable topic of domestic service.

"I presume you are not musical," Fanny remarked, noting the absence of a piano.

"I have a fondness for hearing fine music; I cannot play well myself. Furthermore, Papa and Mama do not hold it in high regard, so we sold our old instrument upon arriving here."

"I wonder how you manage to exist without one. It almost seems a necessity of life."

"Fifteen shillings a week, with three saved from the proceeds!" Margaret thought bitterly. "She must have been very young. She likely has forgotten her own personal experience, yet she must recall those days."

A distinct chill permeated Margaret's manner when she responded next. "You host excellent concerts here, I believe."

"Oh, yes! Delightful! Though too crowded—that is the greatest drawback. The directors admit guests indiscriminately. Nevertheless, one is assured

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the Hale household windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of the industrial city itself. She stood in the hallway, the heavy scent of coal smoke and damp wool clinging to her clothes, her gaze fixed upon the staircase leading upward. Her visitors had departed, leaving behind a silence that pressed against her ears, and now she ascended, driven by a sudden, fierce need to inquire after Betsy Higgins. It was a journey she undertook with a purpose that felt both urgent and sacred; she intended to sit with the dying girl as long as possible before the summons of dinner.

As she navigated the crowded, narrow streets of the lower district, Margaret felt a profound shift within her. The mere act of caring for a resident of this grim quarter had imbued the alleyways with a new significance, transforming them from mere thoroughfares into spaces of moral weight. In the Higgins home, the atmosphere was stifling. Mary Higgins, the slatternly younger sister, had endeavored to tidy the space for the expected visit, but the effort was a desperate patchwork against decay. Rough-stoning marred the center of the floor, while the flags beneath the chairs and tables retained their dark, unwashed appearance, absorbing the gloom of the room. Though the day outside was sultry, a large fire burned aggressively in the grate, turning the interior into an oven. Margaret, steeped in the logic of the North, failed to interpret the lavish consumption of coal as a gesture of hospitable welcome; instead, she perceived the oppressive heat as a necessity for Bessy's fragile constitution, a cruel irony given the lack of ventilation.

Bessy lay upon a squab, a short sofa positioned precariously under the window. She appeared far more feeble than the previous day, exhausted by the exertion of raising herself at every passing moment to peer through the glass, searching for Margaret's arrival. Now that the visitor had entered and claimed a chair beside her, Bessy collapsed back into silence, her eyes fixed on Margaret's face with a childlike, starving admiration. She reached out to trace the textures of Margaret's garments, marveling at their fineness.

"I never knew why folk in the Bible cared for soft raiment afore," Bessy whispered, her voice thin and reedy. "But it must be nice to go dressed as yo' do. It's different fro' common. Most fine folk tire my eyes out wi' their colours; but some how yours rest me. Where did ye get this frock?"

"In London," Margaret replied, a flicker of amusement touching her lips despite the gravity of the scene.

"London!" Bessy gasped. "Have yo' been in London?"

"Yes! I lived there for some years. But my home was in a forest; in the country."

"Tell me about it," Bessy urged, leaning back and shutting her eyes, crossing her hands over her breast as if to create a barrier against the world. "I like to hear speak of the country, and trees, and such like things."

Margaret spoke, her voice carrying the echo of memories she had tried to suppress. She described Helstone not as a lost place, but as a living dream, more vivid than reality. She painted pictures of great trees with branches stretching level to create deep shades of rest, of turfs soft as velvet or lush with the moisture of hidden brooks, and of billowy ferns catching streaks of golden sunlight like the sea. She spoke of wide commons rising above the tree line, places of silence broken only by the distant, sharp voices of farmers or the solitary song of a lark.

"I am glad of that," Bessy murmured, her breathing shallow. "I felt smothered like down below. When I have gone for an out, I've always wanted to get high up and see far away... I get smothered enough in Milton, and I think the sound yo' speak of among the trees, going on for ever and ever, would send me dazed; it's that made my head ache so in the mill."

The conversation drifted inevitably to the source of her suffering. Bessy confessed that she feared the end of all things, terrified of a life spent solely in toil and sickness without the promise of divine reward. Her fear curdled into a violent outburst when she realized the finality of her condition. Sitting up abruptly, she clutched Margaret's hand with a ferocity that bordered on madness.

"If this life is th' end, and there's no God to wipe away all tears from all eyes—yo' wench, yo'!" she cried, her voice cracking. "I could go mad, and kill yo, I could."

She fell back, completely worn out by the intensity of her passion. Margaret knelt immediately, placing a steadying hand on the girl's shoulder.

"Bessy—we have a Father in Heaven."

"I know it! I know it," Bessy moaned, turning her head uneasily. "I'm very wicked. I've spoken very wickedly. Oh! don't be frightened by me and never come again. I would not harm a hair of your head." Opening her eyes, she looked at Margaret with a terrifying clarity. "And I believe, perhaps, more than yo' do o' what's to come. I read the book o' Revelations until I know it off by heart, and I never doubt when I'm waking, and in my senses, of all the glory I'm to come to."

"Do not let us talk of these fancies that arise in your feverish state," Margaret pleaded gently. "I would rather hear of what you used to do when you were well."

Bessy recounted her early employment in a carding-room, describing the "fluff"—tiny bits of cotton that filled the air like white dust, winding around the lungs and tightening them. She explained the economic paralysis of the industry: the expensive wheels designed to remove the dust brought no profit, and workers, accustomed to the toxic environment, refused to accept lower wages required to operate them. Thus, the machines remained unbuilt, and the poison continued.

"How old are you?" Margaret asked softly.

"Nineteen, come July."

"And I too am nineteen," Margaret answered, a wave of sorrow washing over her as she considered the stark contrast between their lives. She struggled to maintain composure, the emotion threatening to break her resolve.

Turning to the topic of Mary, Bessy implored Margaret to be a friend to her sister, unsure of what future Mary was fit for. Margaret hesitated, glancing at the uncleaned corners of the room, acknowledging the impossibility of taking Mary in as a servant given her current state. Yet, she promised friendship nonetheless, a bond forged in the shared tragedy of their circumstances.

"I'll know yo' won't forget me again," Bessy insisted, her grip tightening. "I'll not mistrust yo' no more. But remember, in a week or a fortnight I may be dead and buried!"

"I'll come again as soon as I can, Bessy," Margaret said, squeezing her hand tightly. "But you'll let me know if you get worse."

"Ay, that will I," Bessy returned, her strength fading.

From that day forward, Mrs. Hale descended further into the role of a suffering invalid. As the anniversary of Edith's marriage approached, Margaret reflected on the year's accumulated heap of troubles, wondering how they had been endured. If she could have foreseen them, she would have fled, hiding from the inevitable. Yet, day by day, small, keen, bright spots of positive enjoyment had pierced the darkness, rendering the burden bearable. A year prior, the prospect of a long illness in a strange, noisy, desolate place would have caused her to groan bitterly. But now, amidst the increasing grounds for complaint, a new, stoic patience had taken root in her mother's mind.

Mr. Hale, however, remained trapped in a stage of apprehensive denial. Irritated by his daughter's anxiety, he dismissed her concerns as fanciful delusions. He pointed to his wife's rosy cheeks, insisting they were signs of health rather than the flush of pain. When Margaret persisted, suggesting the color was symptomatic, he grew agitated, pushing her away gently but firmly, as if her words were an intrusion he wished to expel. He paced the room, muttering to himself about the impossibility of doing right without sacrificing others, expressing a hatred for the town and himself alike.

When Margaret finally questioned him about his wife talking of Helstone, he seized upon the silence as proof of her mental stability. "Then, you see, she can't be fretting after them, eh?" he reasoned, seeking comfort in the simplicity of his wife's ignorance. He dismissed her fears as "foolish morbid ideas," urging her to kiss him and retire to bed. But as Margaret lay in her own bed later that night, listening to the rhythmic, anxious pacing of her husband's footsteps—a sound she and Edith had once called *racooning*—she knew better. The truth was not in the silence of the sickroom, nor in the flushed cheeks of the invalid, but in the relentless, grinding march of the city outside, and the quiet, terrible cost of survival within it.

The wind howled down the chimney that night, a frantic, ghostly intruder that shattered the fragile peace of the Hale household. It was a sound that had haunted Mrs. Hale since her son Frederick had cast his lot with the sea, transforming the familiar roar of the elements into a specific, terrifying prophecy of storms. Yet, amidst this tempest of memory, a profound shift occurred within the domestic sphere. Margaret found herself drawn closer to her mother than ever before, bridging the chasm of years with a tenderness that felt like a rediscovered kinship. Her mother, usually reserved, treated her not as a child, but as a confidante, pouring out her sorrows with a raw vulnerability that Margaret had longed to witness. In responding to these calls for sympathy, even over trivialities that would have passed unnoticed by anyone else, Margaret unknowingly stepped into the role she had secretly coveted—the position of emotional anchor that Dixon had previously held.

One evening, with Mr. Hale away, the dam broke. Mrs. Hale spoke of Frederick, the very subject that paralyzed Margaret's tongue despite her burning curiosity. The narrative unfolded through the lens of maternal anxiety: dreams of glass-green walls of water towering over a tiny ship, the cruel white foam curling like serpents, and the desperate hope that the wind harming her son was merely a metaphor for the unseen dangers of his exile. "He is on land now," her mother insisted, trying to rationalize the terror, noting the change in his name to Dickenson, a deliberate shield against recognition. But the truth lay hidden in the yellow, sea-stained letters tucked inside a Japanese cabinet, their scent carrying the brine of distant oceans.

As Margaret deciphered the faded script, the story of Frederick's descent into rebellion crystallized. He had been a boy of gentle temper until the shadow of Captain Reid fell upon him. The letters revealed a slow-burning fuse ignited by petty tyrannies and the humiliation of men forced to race down rigging under threat of the cat-o'-nine-tails. A single moment of desperation—a sailor falling from the spars to avoid a flogging—had sparked a mutiny that spiraled out of control. By the time the news reached England, the *Russell* was a phantom vessel, its crew mutinous, and Captain Reid abandoned in a boat, his fate sealed by the very men he commanded.

When the official lists of the mutineers arrived, bearing the name of Carr instead of Hale, a flicker of false hope ignited. Was it a clerical error? Could the mighty Frederick Hale have been mistaken for someone else? This fragile optimism evaporated the moment Mr. Hale returned from Southampton, his posture broken, his spirit crushed by the grim reality of the press reports. Mrs. Hale recounted the scene with visceral horror: her husband stumbling under the weight of grief, unable to speak, forcing her to read the vitriolic articles that branded her son a traitor of the "blackest dye." The rage that consumed her was not tears, but a searing heat, a physical burning that left her speechless as she tore the paper apart, her hands shaking with the fury of a woman whose world had inverted.

In the aftermath, a fierce pride emerged from the ashes of their despair. Mrs. Hale confessed that she admired Frederick's defiance of arbitrary power more than his blind obedience ever could. "It is still finer to defy unjust authority," Margaret affirmed, her voice steady, recognizing the moral courage in her mother's lost son. Yet, beneath this admiration lay a crushing finality. The court-martial on the *Amicia* had condemned the mutineers to hang at the yard-arm, declaring they had been led astray by their superiors. The sentence was absolute. Frederick was safe in South America, or perhaps drifting toward Spain, but the law awaited him if he ever returned to British soil.

"There is no comfort to be given," Mrs. Hale whispered, turning her face to the wall, refusing to acknowledge the living presence of her daughter. She retreated into the static of her grief, a statue of despair. As Mr. Hale entered the room, bringing with him the heavy atmosphere of defeat, Margaret fled. She walked out into the hallway, the silence pressing against her ears, seeing no dawn on the horizon, only the enduring, dark silhouette of a tragedy that had already claimed its victim, leaving behind only the echo of a name spoken in whispers and the bitter taste of salt on the lips of a grieving family.

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a city that refused to acknowledge the quiet suffocation of its visitors. She sat beside her father, Mr. Hale, her gaze fixed not on the book he held, but on the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post, now folded neatly on the table. They were from Mr. Thornton, his script confident and sprawling, detailing his latest thoughts on industry and labor.

Margaret traced the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener, her mind drifting to the task ahead. Tomorrow, they must return Mrs. Thornton's call. Her mother was bedridden, a victim of a fever that had drained the color from her cheeks, leaving behind a pallor that seemed to echo the grey skies outside. Yet, Mr. Hale's voice remained steady, masking a deep, veiled anxiety that Margaret recognized instantly.

"Did you consult the doctor, Margaret?" he asked, his tone shifting from casual inquiry to urgent concern. "Did you send for him?"

"No, papa," Margaret replied, her voice clear and firm. "You spoke of his coming to see me when I was well. But if I only knew of a good physician, I would go this afternoon and ask him to come. I am sure Mamma is seriously indisposed."

She spoke the truth plainly, driven by a sudden realization that her father had previously shut his mind against the severity of her fears. Now, the landscape of their conversation had shifted. He answered in a despondent whisper, haunted by a terrifying possibility.

"Do you think she has any hidden complaint? Do you think she is really very ill? Has Dixon said anything? Oh, Margaret! I am haunted by the fear that our coming to Milton has killed her. My poor Maria!"

"Oh, papa! Don't imagine such things," Margaret said, shocked by the gravity of his despair. "She is not well, that is all. Many a one is not well for a time; and with good advice, gets better and stronger than ever."

"But has Dixon said anything about her?"

"No! You know Dixon enjoys making a mystery out of trifles; and she has been a little mysterious about Mamma's health, which has alarmed me rather—that is all. Without any reason, I dare say. You know, papa, you said the other day I was getting fanciful."

"I hope and trust you are," Mr. Hale said, his eyes lighting up with relief. "But don't think of what I said then. I like you to be fanciful about your mother's health! Don't be afraid of telling me your fancies. I like to hear them, though I dare say I spoke as if I was annoyed. But we will ask Mrs. Thornton if she can tell us of a good doctor. We won't throw away our money on anyone but someone first-rate. Stay, we turn up this street."

The street did not look as if it could contain a house large enough for Mrs. Thornton's habitation. Her son's presence never gave any impression as to the kind of house he lived in; but, unconsciously, Margaret had imagined that tall, massive, handsomely dressed Mrs. Thornton must reside in a mansion of equal grandeur. Now, Marlborough Street revealed itself as a long row of small, cramped dwellings, punctuated by blank walls.

"He told me he lived in Marlborough Street, I'm sure," said Mr. Hale, his brow furrowed in perplexity.

"Perhaps it is one of the economies he still practices, to live in a very small house. But here are plenty of people about; let me ask."

She accordingly inquired of a passer-by, who informed them that Mr. Thornton lived close to the mill, pointing out the factory lodge-door at the end of a long, dead wall they had noticed earlier.

The lodge-door was unremarkable, resembling a common garden gate, flanked by great closed iron gates designed for the ingress and egress of lorries and wagons. The lodge-keeper admitted them into a vast, oblong yard. On one side stood offices for the transaction of business; on the opposite, an immense, multi-windowed mill from which proceeded the continual, deafening clank of machinery and the long, groaning roar of the steam-engine. Opposite the wall, along the narrow side of the yard, stood a handsome stone-coped house. Blackened, to be sure, by the perpetual smoke, its paint, windows, and steps were kept with scrupulous cleanliness. It was evident that the house had been built some fifty or sixty years prior. The stone facings, the long narrow windows, the multiple flights of steps ascending from either side and guarded by railings—all testified to its age. Margaret wondered why people who could afford to live in such a substantial house, maintaining it in such pristine order, did not prefer a smaller dwelling in the countryside or a quieter suburb, escaping the continual whirl and din of the factory.

Her unaccustomed ears could hardly catch her father's voice as they stood on the steps awaiting the opening of the door. The yard, with the great doors in the dead wall serving as a boundary, was but a dismal outlook for the sitting-rooms of the house—a fact Margaret confirmed when they had mounted the old-fashioned stairs and been ushered into the drawing-room. The room featured three windows overlooking the front door and the passage on the right. There was no one inside. It seemed as though no one had entered since the day the furniture was bagged up with the meticulous care of someone preparing for a volcanic eruption, intending to discover the house a thousand years hence.

The walls were pink and gold; the pattern on the carpet depicted bunches of flowers on a light ground, yet it was carefully covered in the center by a linen drugget, glazed and colorless. The window curtains were lace; each chair and sofa wore its own particular veil of netting or knitting. Great alabaster groups occupied every flat surface, protected from dust beneath glass shades. In the middle of the room, directly under the bagged-up chandelier, stood a large circular table with smartly bound books arranged at regular intervals around its polished surface, like gaily colored spokes of a wheel. Everything reflected light; nothing absorbed it. The whole room possessed a painfully spotted, spangled, speckled appearance that impressed Margaret so unpleasantly that she was hardly conscious of the peculiar cleanliness required to keep everything so white and pure in such an atmosphere, or of the trouble that must be willingly expended to secure that effect of icy, snowy discomfort. Everywhere there was evidence of care and labor, but not care and labor to procure ease or to foster habits of tranquil home employment; solely to ornament and then to preserve ornament from dirt or destruction.

They had leisure to observe and to speak to each other in low voices before Mrs. Thornton appeared. They were discussing matters that the entire world might eventually hear, yet the room itself seemed to demand silence, as if unwilling to awaken its unused echoes.

Finally, Mrs. Thornton entered, rustling in handsome black silk, as was her wont. Her muslins and laces rivaled, though did not excel, the pure whiteness of the room's decor. Margaret explained how her mother could not accompany them to return the call, but in her anxiety not to revive her father's fears too vividly, she gave a bungling account. She left the impression on Mrs. Thornton's mind that Mrs. Hale's condition was some temporary or fanciful fine-ladyish indisposition, resolvable with a strong enough motive, or that if it was too severe to allow her to come out that day, the call might simply be deferred. Remembering the horses to her carriage, hired specifically for her own visit to the Hales, and recalling how Fanny had been ordered to go by Mr. Thornton to pay every respect to them, Mrs. Thornton drew up slightly offended. She gave Margaret no sympathy—indeed, hardly any credit for the statement of her mother's indisposition.

"How is Mr. Thornton?" asked Mr. Hale. "I was afraid he was not well, from his hurried note yesterday."

"My son is rarely ill; and when he is, he never speaks about it, or makes it an excuse for not doing anything. He told me he could not get leisure to read with you last night, sir. He regretted it, I am sure; he values the hours spent with you."

"I am sure they are equally agreeable to me," said Mr. Hale. "It makes me feel young again to see his enjoyment and appreciation of all that is fine in classical literature."

"I have no doubt that classics are very desirable for people who have leisure. But I confess, it was against my judgment that my son renewed his study of them. The time and place in which he lives seem to me to require all his energy and attention. Classics may do very well for men who loiter away their lives in the country or in colleges; but Milton men ought to have their thoughts and powers absorbed in the work of today. At least, that is my opinion." She delivered this final clause with the pride that apes humility.

"But, surely, if the mind is too long directed to one object only, it will get stiff and rigid, and unable to take in many interests," said Margaret.

"I do not quite understand what you mean by a mind getting stiff and rigid. Nor do I admire those whirligig characters that are full of this thing today, to be utterly forgetful of it in their new interest tomorrow. Having many interests does not suit the life of a Milton manufacturer. It is or ought to be enough for him to have one great desire, and to bring

The afternoon sun cast long, skeletal shadows across the floorboards of the Hale residence, a visual prelude to the shadow of death that now settled over the household. Dr. Donaldson arrived to conduct his first formal consultation with Mrs. Hale, a visit that immediately reinstated the barrier of professional secrecy Margaret had hoped to dismantle through their previous intimacy. While the physician entered the sickroom, Margaret was relegated to the periphery, confined to the adjacent chamber.

She paced the narrow confines of her mother's bedroom, the rhythm of her footsteps a frantic counterpoint to the silence. Every pause was seized by her ear, straining to catch the faintest tremor of pain—a moan that seemed to vibrate through the walls. Clenching her fists until her nails dug into her palms, she held her breath, convinced she had heard the sound of suffering. When the room finally fell silent, followed by the rustle of chairs and the murmur of departure, she rushed out, her heart hammering against her ribs.

In the hallway, she intercepted Dr. Donaldson, her demeanor shifting instantly from anxious daughter to authoritative mistress of the house. She bypassed the old servant, Dixon, with a dignified stride that silenced the man's usual officious interjections. This sudden adoption of regal authority provided her with a fleeting respite from her terror, a momentary oblivion granted by the ridiculous grandeur of her posture. Yet, as she descended the stairs, the weight of the task crashed back upon her, stealing the air from her lungs.

Confronting the doctor in the hall, she demanded the truth with an imperious tone. "What is the matter with mamma?" she commanded. "You will oblige me by telling the simple truth." When the physician hesitated, citing her mother's explicit wishes and the presence of a devoted servant, Margaret cut him down. "I am her daughter, sir," she declared, her voice steady despite the turmoil within. "I am not good or patient enough to submit to prohibition. Besides, you are too wise to have promised to keep a secret that must inevitably surface."

Dr. Donaldson, a man whose diagnostic acumen relied on piercing the soul rather than just the flesh, observed her reaction with clinical precision. He saw the dilation of her pupils, the flash of black horror in her eyes, and the livid pallor spreading across her cheeks. He understood that withholding the truth would inflict a torture more agonizing than the knowledge itself. With a low, grave voice, he delivered the diagnosis in two short sentences.

The effect was instantaneous. Margaret's color drained completely, her lips compressing into a line of steel. "I thank you most truly, sir, for your confidence," she whispered, her voice breaking. "That dread has haunted me for many weeks. It is a true, real agony. My poor, poor mother!" Tears welled, unchecked for a brief moment, before her formidable will reclaimed control. She pressed further, asking about suffering and prognosis. The doctor assured her that while the outcome depended on countless variables, modern medicine offered significant means of alleviation.

When Margaret trembled, asking about her father, the doctor advised caution. He suggested bearing the burden alone until the shock wore off, noting that Mr. Thornton's recent sacrifices proved his devotion, even if his judgment was flawed. He spoke of future visits, framing his role not merely as a healer but as a confidant worthy of years of social calling. As he departed, Margaret wrung his hand with such desperate force that he later marveled at the strength of her grip, remarking on the "immense power" of her well-set bones. He admired her resilience, noting how she had forced him to speak the truth with her head thrown back, only to bend eagerly forward to receive it. "Such a girl as that would win my heart," he mused, though age rendered the sentiment moot. "Another, who had gone that deadly colour, could never have come round without either fainting or hysterics. But she wouldn't do either—not she! And the very force of her will brought her round."

Back in the study, Margaret collapsed inwardly, praying for strength before ascending to her mother. "Oh, my God, my God! but this is terrible," she sobbed silently. "How shall I bear it? Such a deadly disease! no hope!" She lamented her absence during her childhood, wishing she had remained by her mother's side, and prayed that her mother's final moments would not be marked by unbearable agony. She resolved to shield her father from the immediate blow, fearing it would kill him, yet she refused to waste another second of her mother's remaining time.

Upon entering the sickroom, she found Mrs. Hale reclining in an easy chair, draped in a soft white shawl and wearing a neat cap. Despite the gravity of the situation, her mother appeared remarkably calm, her face flushed with a faint, peaceful color. Margaret approached, startled by the tranquility. Her mother, sensing the unasked questions, grew displeased, accusing Margaret of avoiding the doctor.

"I made him," Margaret confessed, kneeling beside her mother's chair and seizing her hand. "It was I—blame me." She kissed the hand fervently, her hot tears soaking the fabric. Mrs. Hale protested, claiming it was wrong for Margaret to know, but her resistance crumbled under the weight of her daughter's affection. Exhausted by the emotional contest, she allowed her hand to remain in Margaret's grasp.

"Let me be your nurse," Margaret pleaded, her voice thick with emotion. "I will learn anything Dixon can teach me... I do think I have a right to do everything for you." Mrs. Hale shuddered, warning her of the magnitude of the request, but Margaret insisted, promising a dedication surpassing anyone else's.

"You don't know what you are asking," Mrs. Hale warned, referencing Dixon's belief that Margaret would shrink from the reality of illness. Margaret scoffed at the notion, defending her capacity for love against the servant's patronizing assumptions about weak-willed women. "Don't let Dixon's fancies come any more between you and me, mamma," she implored.

Mrs. Hale softened, urging peace regarding Dixon, and Margaret agreed to humility and learning. They shared memories of their time at Helstone, contrasting the makeshift comforts of their youth with the polished luxury of Harley Street. Mrs. Hale tearfully declared she would never see Helstone again, viewing her impending death as a just punishment for leaving it. Suddenly, the mention of "Frederick" shattered her composure. A wild, passionate cry erupted from her—"Frederick! Frederick! Come to me. I am dying!"—as the thought of her husband overwhelmed her exhaustion, triggering violent hysteria.

Margaret summoned Dixon in terror. The servant entered, scolding Margaret for over-excitement, but the young woman bore the rebuke meekly, hoping her father's return might be delayed. Following Dixon's instructions with prompt obedience, she smoothed her mother's distress. Once Mrs. Hale slept, Dixon led Margaret to the drawing-room, forcing her to drink a cup of coffee while standing over her with a sour, commanding air.

"You shouldn't have been so curious," Dixon chided, predicting that Margaret's curiosity would only hasten her grief. "And now, I suppose, you'll tell master..."

"No, Dixon," Margaret replied sorrowfully, bursting into fresh tears to prove her fortitude. "I will not tell papa. He could not bear it as I can."

Dixon, moved by the display of spirit, revealed the depth of her own hidden love for Mrs. Hale, recounting stories of the Beresford family's fierce loyalty and justice. She praised Margaret's resemblance to her father, Frederick, noting how her temper mirrored his stormy looks. As Margaret prepared to leave, she lingered, then suddenly kissed the old servant, exiting the room with a light step. Dixon watched her go, blessing her sweetness and listing the three people she loved above all others: her mistress, Master Frederick, and the young lady. She reflected bitterly on Frederick's obsession with reading and thinking, believing it had cost him his wife, and watched Margaret disappear into the night, her shabby clothes a stark contrast to the finery she had worn a year prior.

The air of Milton Street possessed a peculiar alchemy; it was heavy with the scent of coal dust and wet wool, yet within its narrow confines, it carried a strange, electric vitality that lifted Margaret's spirits. She stepped out of the mill gates with a heaviness in her heart, but as the cobblestones gave way to the bustling thoroughfare, her step lightened, her lips curving upward. Here, the world was not confined to the silent rows of machines and the stoic faces of workers. She observed a tableau of human energy: men with hands buried deep in pockets, striding with a swagger that suggested ownership of the pavement; clusters of girls, their voices rising in raucous laughter, radiating a boisterous independence that felt both thrilling and dangerous. Yet, lurking in the shadows of the beer-houses and gin-pubs were the discreditable minority—men whose faces bore the marks of excess, smoking and casting judgmental glances at every passerby. Margaret recoiled slightly from this underbelly of the city, eager to escape the long walk through these streets and reach the fields she had envisioned, but a sudden impulse redirected her path. She would not seek the solitude of the countryside today; instead, she would find Bessy Higgins. It lacked the serene refreshment of a rural stroll, but it promised a kinship of shared burden.

Inside the Higgins household, the atmosphere was thick with the haze of tobacco smoke. Nicholas Higgins sat by the fire, the ember glowing steadily in his pipe, while Bessy rocked rhythmically on the opposite side of the room, her movements a physical manifestation of her unrest. When Margaret entered, Nicholas extinguished his pipe and stood, pushing his chair aside to lean casually against the chimney-piece. His posture was relaxed, yet his eyes held a steeliness that spoke of suppressed tension.

"How fares the spirit?" Margaret asked gently, turning her attention to Bessy.

"Hoo's rather down in the mouth regarding spirits," Bessy replied, her voice flat, "but hoo's better in health. Hoo doesn't like this strike. Hoo's a deal too much set on peace and quietness at any price."

"This is the third strike I've seen," she continued, letting out a weary sigh that seemed to carry the weight of years.

"Well, third time pays for all," Nicholas declared, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial rumble. "See if we don't damn the masters this time. See if they don't come begging us to return at our own terms. That's the game. We missed our mark before, I grant you; but this time, we've laid our plans desperate deep."

"Why do you strike?" Margaret pressed, her curiosity sharpening. "Striking is simply ceasing work until you secure your desired wage, isn't it? I must confess my ignorance; in my homeland, such a concept was unknown to me."

"I wish I were there," Bessy murmured, her gaze drifting to the window. "But it is not for me to grow sick and tired of strikes. This shall be the final one I witness. Before it concludes, I shall be in the Great City—the Holy Jerusalem."

"Hoo's so consumed with the life to come, hoo cannot comprehend the present," Nicholas countered, resuming his pipe. "Now, you see, I am bound to do the best I can here. A bird in the hand is worth two in the bush. Thus, our differing perspectives on the strike question arise."

"But," Margaret interjected, "if the people struck, as they do in my land among the field laborers, the seeds would rot unsown, the hay remain uncut, and the corn unreaped."

"Well?" Nicholas challenged, puffing a cloud of gray smoke.

"Why," Margaret persisted, "what becomes of the farmers?"

He exhaled slowly. "I reckon they'd either abandon their farms or offer a fair rate of wages."

"Suppose neither option presents itself?" Margaret argued. "They cannot relinquish their lands instantly, regardless of their desire. Without hay or corn to sell that year, where would the capital originate to pay the laborers' wages the following season?"

Still puffing, Nicholas finally spoke. "I know naught of your customs down South. I've heard they are a breed of spiritless, downtrodden men, utterly cowed, too numbed by oppression to recognize when they are being exploited. Here, it is not so. We know precisely when we are put upon; and we possess enough blood in our veins to endure no more. We simply remove our hands from our looms and declare, 'You may cower us, but you shall not oppress us, my masters!' And be damned to them, they shall not succeed this time!"

"I wished I lived down South," Bessy whispered.

"There is much to endure there," Margaret admitted. "Sorrows abound everywhere. There is grueling physical toil performed with scant sustenance to fuel the body."

"But it is outdoors," Bessy insisted. "Away from the endless, deafening noise and the sickening heat of the factories."

"It is sometimes in torrential rain, and sometimes in biting cold," Margaret corrected. "A young person might withstand it, but an old man is ravaged by rheumatism, bent and withered prematurely. Yet, he must continue working, or face the grim alternative of the workhouse."

"I thought you were so enamored with the ways of the Southern country," Nicholas remarked abruptly.

"So I am," Margaret smiled faintly, realizing she had walked into a trap of her own making. "I only mean, Bessy, that there is good and evil in all things. Since you perceive the darkness here, I deemed it fair that you should also understand the shadow cast by the South."

"And you claim they never strike down there?" Nicholas asked sharply.

"No!" Margaret answered firmly. "I believe they possess too much sense."

"And I contend," Nicholas retorted, dashing the ashes from his pipe with such violent force that the stem snapped, "that it is not that they lack sense, but that they lack spirit."

"Oh, father!" Bessy cried out. "What have you gained by striking? Recall that first strike when Mother passed—how we were forced to cease work, you suffering most of all. Yet, countless others returned weekly at the same wage until employment vanished entirely, leaving some destitute for the remainder of their lives."

"Aye," Nicholas conceded. "That strike was poorly managed. Fools and traitors seized control. You shall see; this time shall be different."

"But throughout this discourse, you have not revealed what you are striking for," Margaret demanded again.

"Why, you see," Nicholas explained, his voice gaining intensity, "there are five or six masters who have doubled down on the wages they paid over the past two years, prospering and growing wealthier. Now, they approach us demanding a reduction. We refuse. We shall grind them to a halt first, and observe who remains willing to employ us. They intend to kill the goose that lays the golden eggs, I wager."

"And so you plan to perish in order to exact revenge upon them!" Margaret exclaimed, horrified.

"No," he denied vehemently. "I merely anticipate the possibility of dying at my post sooner than yielding. That is what men call noble and honorable in a soldier, and why not in a poor weaver? But listen closely."

"But," Margaret protested, "a soldier dies for the Nation—for the sake of others."

He laughed, a grim, dry sound. "My lass," he said, "you are but a young woman, yet do you not realize I must sustain three lives—Bessy, Mary, and myself—on sixteen shillings a week? Do you think I strike for my own sake? It is just as much for the cause of others as that soldier you praise. Perhaps the difference lies in the fact that the soldier perishes for strangers he has never met, whereas I champion the cause of John Boucher, who resides next door, burdened by a sickly wife and eight children, none of whom are of factory age. I do not advocate solely for him, though he is a poor soul capable of managing only two looms simultaneously; I fight for the cause of justice. Why should we accept lower wages now than we did two years ago?"

"Do not ask me," Margaret deflected, sensing the danger of further inquiry. "I am profoundly ignorant. Inquire of your masters. Surely they will provide a rationale. It cannot be merely an arbitrary decree issued without cause."

"You are merely a foreigner, and nothing more," Nicholas sneered, his contempt palpable. "What wisdom do you possess? Ask the masters! They would bid us mind our own business while they mind theirs. Our duty is to accept the reduced wage and express gratitude; their business is to drive us down to the brink of starvation to inflate their profits. That is the essence of it."

"But," Margaret insisted, refusing to retreat despite seeing the irritation flare in his eyes, "the state of trade may be such that they are unable to maintain previous remuneration levels."

"State of trade!" Nicholas spat the words. "That is mere humbug concocted by the masters. I was speaking of the rate of wages. The masters monopolize the state of trade; they wield it like a specter, a black bug-a-boo, to frighten naughty children into submission. Let me enlighten you: it is their role, their cue, to crush us and swell their fortunes; it is ours to rise and fight hard—not solely for ourselves, but for those surrounding us, for justice and fair play. We contribute to their profits,

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the Hale parlor windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a city determined to crush the quiet suffocation of its inhabitants. She sat at the mahogany table, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool clinging to the room, her gaze fixed upon the stack of correspondence that had arrived with the morning post. One was a hurried scrawl for her mother; the other, crisp and silvery, bore the foreign postmarks of a journey interrupted by heat, clearly from Aunt Shaw.

Margaret lifted the letter from Naples, her fingers tracing the thin paper. It was then that her father burst into the room, his movements jerky, his face already etched with a gravity that betrayed the turmoil beneath his composure.

"So your mother is tired, and gone to bed early!" he exclaimed, his voice tight. "I'm afraid such a thundery day was not the best in the world for the doctor to see her. What did he say? Dixon tells me he spoke to you about her."

Margaret hesitated, the weight of the unspoken pressing down on her chest. Her father's eyes narrowed, searching hers for confirmation of a catastrophe he refused to acknowledge.

"He does not think her seriously ill?"

"Not at present," Margaret replied, her voice steady despite the ache in her throat. "He says she needs care. He was very kind, and he promised to call again to see how his medicines worked."

"Only care?" Mr. Hale leaned forward, his knuckles white as he gripped the back of his chair. "He did not recommend a change of air? Did he not say this smoky town is doing her any harm, Margaret?"

"No! Not a word," she lied, the falsehood tasting like ash. "He was anxious, I think."

"Anxious," he muttered, dismissing the sentiment as mere professional affectation. "Doctors have that manner. It's business."

Yet, Margaret watched him, seeing the first tremor of genuine terror take root in his mind. He could not let the subject slide; he could not walk away from the shadow of illness that loomed over his wife. Throughout the evening, he returned to the topic with an unwillingness to entertain even the slightest unfavorable idea, a desperate denial that left Margaret feeling profoundly isolated in her sadness.

"This letter is from Aunt Shaw, papa," she finally said, breaking the silence. "She has reached Naples, found it too hot, and has taken apartments at Sorrento. But I don't think she likes Italy."

"He did not mention diet, did he?"

"It was to be nourishing and digestible. Mamma's appetite is pretty good, I think."

"Yes! And that makes it all the more strange he should have spoken of diet."

"I asked him, papa." Another pause, filled with the sound of rain against the glass. Then, softer, "Aunt Shaw says she has sent me some coral ornaments. But," she added, a faint, forced smile touching her lips, "she fears the Milton dissenters won't appreciate them. She has formed all her ideas of Dissenters from the Quakers, hasn't she?"

"If ever you hear or notice that your mother wishes for anything, be sure you let me know immediately. I am so afraid she does not tell me always what she would like. Pray, see after that girl Mrs. Thornton named. If we had a good, efficient house-servant, Dixon could be constantly with her, and I'd answer for it we'd soon set her up amongst us if care will do it. She's been terribly tired lately, with the heat and the difficulty of finding help. A little rest will put her quite to rights—eh, Margaret?"

"I hope so," Margaret said, but her tone was so laden with sorrow that her father noticed instantly. He reached out, pinching her cheek with a rough, affectionate gesture.

"Come, if you look so pale, I must rouge you up a little. Take care of yourself, child, or you'll be needing the doctor next."

But he could not settle. He moved restlessly, pacing on laborious tiptoes, peering into the bedroom to check if his wife was still asleep. Margaret's heart ached at his agitation—his futile attempt to strangle the hideous fear rising from the dark corners of his soul.

Finally, he returned, slightly relieved.

"She's awake now, Margaret. She smiled as she saw me standing by her. Just her old smile. She says she feels refreshed and ready for tea. Where's the note for her? She wants to see it. I'll read it to her while you make tea."

The note was a formal invitation from Mrs. Thornton, extending hospitality to Mr., Mrs., and Miss Hale for dinner on the twenty-first. Margaret was surprised to find an acceptance contemplated after the grim probabilities of the day, yet so it was. The prospect of her husband and daughter attending had captivated Mrs. Hale's imagination, an event to diversify the monotony of her invalid confinement. She clung to the idea with fretful pertinacity, refusing to listen to Margaret's objections.

"Nay, Margaret: if she wishes it, I'm sure we'll both go willingly. She never would wish it unless she felt herself really stronger—really better than we thought, eh, Margaret?" Mr. Hale asked anxiously as Margaret prepared to draft the response.

"Eh, Margaret?" he questioned, his hands making a nervous fluttering motion. It seemed cruel to deny him the comfort he craved. Moreover, his passionate refusal to admit fear almost inspired Margaret with a flicker of hope.

"I do think she is better since last night," she said softly. "Her eyes look brighter, and her complexion clearer."

"God bless you," her father whispered earnestly. "But is it true? Yesterday was so sultry everyone felt ill. It was a most unlucky day for Mr. Donaldson to visit on."

With that, he departed to his duties, which had expanded to include preparing lectures for the working people at a neighboring Lyceum. He had chosen Ecclesiastical Architecture, a subject closer to his own tastes and knowledge than the crude desires of his audience, hoping to offer a gratis course to an institution drowning in debt.

Later that night, the atmosphere shifted as Mr. Thornton entered the dining room.

"Well, mother," he asked, "who have accepted your invitations for the twenty-first?"

"Fanny, where are the notes?" Mrs. Thornton snapped, her voice sharp with fatigue. "The Slicksons accept, Collingbrooks accept, Stephenses accept, Browns decline. The Hales—father and daughter come, though mother is too great an invalid. Macphersons come, and Mr. Horsfall, and Mr. Young. I was thinking of asking the Porters since the Browns can't come."

"Very good. Do you know, I'm really afraid Mrs. Hale is very far from well, based on what Dr. Donaldson said."

"It's strange of them to accept a dinner invitation if she's very ill," Fanny remarked, her voice small and nervous.

"I didn't say *very* ill," her brother replied sharply. "I said far from well. They may not know it either." Then, abruptly, he remembered that Margaret, at least, knew the exact state of affairs from the doctor's previous words.

"Very probably they are quite aware of what you said yesterday, John—of the great advantage it would be to them, to Mr. Hale specifically, to be introduced to people like the Stephenses and Collingbrooks."

"I'm sure that motive would not influence them. No! I think I understand how it is."

"John!" Fanny laughed, a weak, brittle sound. "How you profess to understand these Hales, and how you never allow that we can know anything about them. Are they really so very different to most people one meets?"

She did not intend to vex him, but if she had, she could not have succeeded more thoroughly. He chafed in silence, refusing to dignify her question with a reply.

"They do not seem to me out of the ordinary," Mrs. Thornton interjected. "He appears a worthy kind of man, though rather too simple for trade—it's perhaps as well he was a clergyman first and now a teacher. She's a bit of a fine lady, with her invalidism. As for the girl... she's the only one who puzzles me. She seems to have a great notion of giving herself airs. I can't make out why. I could almost fancy she thinks herself too good for her company at times. And yet they're not rich; from all I can hear, they never have been."

"And she's not accomplished, mamma. She can't play."

"Go on, Fanny. What else does she want to bring her up to your standard?"

"Nay! John," his mother said, turning to him. "That speech of Fanny's did no harm. I myself heard Miss Hale say she could not play. If you would let us alone, we could perhaps like her and see her merits."

"I'm sure I never could!" murmured Fanny, retreating behind her mother's shield. Mr. Thornton heard her but cared not to respond.
