He walked up and down the dining room, wishing his mother would order candles so he could retreat to reading or writing, escaping the conversation. But he never interfered with the small domestic regulations Mrs. Thornton observed, born of her old economies.

"Mother," he stopped, bracing himself to speak the truth, "I wish you would like

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a city that refused to acknowledge the quiet suffocation of its women. Yet within this confined sanctuary, where the walls and curtains seemed to expand until they swallowed the horizon, a peculiar alchemy was taking place. Mrs. Hale watched her daughter with a mixture of amusement and maternal fascination, observing Margaret's anticipation of the Thornton dinner party with the wide-eyed simplicity of a child awaiting a promised treat. In the monotonous stagnation of illness, both mother and daughter had lost their sense of proportion; the domestic sphere felt vast and impenetrable, while the world beyond remained a terrifying abstraction. For Mrs. Hale, whose youthful vanities had been smothered by the crushing weight of poverty and clerical modesty, the prospect of seeing Margaret dressed for society was a thrilling resurrection of a dead self. She hovered anxiously over the wardrobe, her questions sharp with insecurity.

"Then you think you shall wear your white silk?" she pressed, her voice tight. "Are you sure it will fit? It's nearly a year since Edith was married!"

"Oh, yes, mamma!" Margaret replied, her tone lightening the tension. "Mrs. Murray made it, and it's sure to be right; it may be a straw's breadth shorter or longer-waisted, according to my having grown fat or thin. But I don't think I've altered in the least."

"Perhaps you'd better let Dixon see it," Mrs. Hale suggested, her eyes darting to the moth-eaten corners of the room. "It may have gone yellow with lying by."

"If you like, mamma. But if the worst comes to the worst, I've a very nice pink gauze which Aunt Shaw gave me, only two or three months before Edith was married. That can't have gone yellow."

"No! but it may have faded."

"Well! then I've a green silk. I feel more as if it was the embarrassment of riches."

"I wish I knew what you ought to wear," Mrs. Hale confessed, her nerves fraying.

Margaret's demeanor shifted instantly, the gravity of the moment settling over her. "Shall I go and put them on one after another, mamma, and then you could see which you liked best?"

"But—yes! perhaps that will be best."

Off went Margaret. She was tempted to indulge in the theatricality of the occasion—to make her rich white silk balloon out into a grotesque cheese, or to retreat backward from her mother as if she were a sovereign queen—but when she sensed that such whims were viewed as interruptions to the serious business of survival, she suppressed them, becoming grave and sedate. What drove the world to fuss so relentlessly over her attire, she could not comprehend. Yet later that afternoon, when she named her engagement to Bessy Higgins, the servant girl's reaction was immediate and electric.

"Dear! and are you going to dine at Thornton's at Marlborough Mills?" Bessy exclaimed, her voice rising.

"Yes, Bessy. Why are you so surprised?"

"Oh, I dunno. But they visit wi' a' th' first folk in Milton."

"And you don't think we're quite the first folk in Milton, eh, Bessy?" Margaret teased gently.

Bessy's cheeks flushed, the color returning to her pale face as her thought was effortlessly read. "Well," she said, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper, "yo' see, they thinken a deal o' money here; and I reckon yo've not getten much."

"No," said Margaret, her expression softening. "That's very true. But we are educated people, and have lived amongst educated people. Is there anything so wonderful, in our being asked out to dinner by a man who owns himself inferior to my father by coming to him to be instructed? I don't mean to blame Mr. Thornton. Few drapers' assistants, as he was once, could have made themselves what he is."

"But can yo' give dinners back, in yo're small house? Thornton's house is three times as big."

"Well, I think we could manage to give Mr. Thornton a dinner back, as you call it. Perhaps not in such a large room, nor with so many people. But I don't think we've thought about it at all in that way."

"I never thought yo'd be dining with Thorntons," Bessy repeated, awe coloring her features. "Why, the mayor hissel' dines there; and the members of Parliament and all."

"I think, I could support the honour of meeting the mayor of Milton."

"But them ladies dress so grand!" Bessy added, casting an anxious glance at Margaret's plain print gown, which her Milton-trained eyes appraised at a mere sevenpence a yard.

Margaret's face dimpled into a merry laugh. "Thank you, Bessy, for thinking so kindly about my looking nice among all the smart people. But I've plenty of grand gowns,—a week ago, I should have said they were far too grand for anything I should ever want here. But as I'm to dine at Mr. Thornton's, and perhaps to meet the mayor, I shall put on my very best gown, you may be sure."

"What win yo' wear?" Bessy asked, a note of relief entering her voice.

"White silk," said Margaret. "A gown I had for a cousin's wedding, a year ago."

"That'll do!" Bessy declared, sinking back into her chair. "I should be loth to have yo' looked down upon."

"Oh! I'll be fine enough, if that will save me from being looked down upon in Milton."

"I wish I could see you dressed up," Bessy whispered, her gaze drifting upward. "I reckon, yo're not what folk would ca' pretty; yo've not red and white enough for that. But dun yo' know, I ha' dreamt of yo', long afore ever I seed yo'."

"Nonsense, Bessy!"

"Ay, but I did. Yo'r very face,—looking wi' yo'r clear steadfast eyes out o' th' darkness, wi' yo'r hair blown off from yo'r brow, and going out like rays round yo'r forehead, which was just as smooth and straight as it is now,—and yo' always came to give me strength, which I seemed to gather out o' yo'r deep comforting eyes,—and yo' was drest in shining raiment—just as yo'r going to be drest. So, yo' see, it was yo'!"

"Nay, Bessy," said Margaret, gently, "it was but a dream."

"And why might na I dream a dream in my affliction as well as others? Did not many a one i' the Bible? Ay, and see visions too! Why, even my father thinks a deal o' dreams! I tell yo' again, I saw yo' as plainly, coming swiftly towards me, wi' yo'r hair blown back wi' the very swiftness o' the motion, just like the way it grows, a little standing off like; and the white shining dress on yo've getten to wear. Let me come and see yo' in it. I want to see yo' and touch yo' as in very deed yo' were in my dream."

"My dear Bessy, it is quite a fancy of yours."

"Fancy or no fancy,—yo've come, as I knew yo' would, when I saw yo'r movement in my dream,—and when yo're here about me, I reckon I feel easier in my mind, and comforted, just as a fire comforts one on a dree day. Yo' said it were on th' twenty-first; please God, I'll come and see yo'."

"Oh Bessy! you may come and welcome; but don't talk so—it really makes me sorry. It does indeed."

"Then I'll keep it to mysel', if I bite my tongue out. Not but what it's true for all that."

Margaret fell silent. Finally, she spoke. "Let us talk about it sometimes, if you think it true. But not now. Tell me, has your father turned out?"

"Ay!" Bessy answered heavily, her spirit collapsing under the weight of recent news. "He and many another,—all Hamper's men,—and many a one besides. Th' women are as bad as th' men, in their savageness, this time. Food is high,—and they mun have food for their childer, I reckon. Suppose Thorntons sent 'em their dinner out,—th' same money, spent on potatoes and meal, would keep many a crying baby quiet, and hush up its mother's heart for a bit!"

"Don't speak so!" Margaret cried, horror striking her. "You'll make me feel wicked and guilty in going to this dinner."

"No!" Bessy insisted, her voice gaining a fierce, defiant edge. "Some's pre-elected to sumptuous feasts, and purple and fine linen,—may be yo're one on 'em. Others toil and moil all their lives long—and the very dogs are not pitiful in our days, as they were in the days of Lazarus. But if yo' ask me to cool yo're tongue wi' th' tip of my finger, I'll come across the great gulf to yo' just for th' thought o' what yo've been to me here."

"Bessy; you

The Duke of Normandy's decree echoed in Margaret's mind, a grim reminder of the stakes: *"Let 'em all eat... Let 'em have ten tire of teeth a-piece, I care not."* Yet, as she stepped through the threshold of her home, the weight of that indifference felt less like royal resolve and more like a crushing inevitability. Her mind was a chaotic storm, replaying the scenes from Higgins's cottage with such visceral intensity that the domestic duties awaiting her seemed to dissolve into mist. Her mother, Mrs. Hale, sat confined to her chair, her usual reliance on Margaret's cheerful reports now tinged with a desperate hunger for news from the outside world.

"And can your factory friend come on Thursday to see you dressed?" Mrs. Hale asked, her voice tight with anticipation.

"She was so ill I never thought of asking her," Margaret replied, the word *ill* tasting like bile.

"Dear! Everybody is ill now, I think," Mrs. Hale muttered, a flicker of envy crossing her features—the possessive jealousy of the sick fearing the sickness of others. "But it must be very sad to be ill in one of those little back streets." Her tone softened, the old Helstone compassion surfacing beneath the irritation. "It's bad enough here. What could you do for her, Margaret? Mr. Thornton has sent me some of his old port wine since you went out. Would a bottle of that do her good, think you?"

"No, mamma!" Margaret protested, her voice rising in agitation. "I don't believe they are very poor—at least, they don't speak as if they were; and, at any rate, Bessy's illness is consumption—she won't want wine. Perhaps, I might take her a little preserve, made of our dear Helstone fruit. No! there's another family to whom I should like to give—" She broke off, overwhelmed by a surge of emotion that shattered the composure she had meticulously constructed before entering the house. "Oh mamma, mamma! How am I to dress up in my finery, and go off and away to smart parties, after the sorrow I've seen to-day?"

The revelation hit Mrs. Hale with the force of a physical blow. Restlessness seized her, transforming into a frantic need to act. She barked orders at Margaret to pack a basket in the drawing-room, demanding it be dispatched immediately to the suffering families. She was nearly furious when Margaret suggested it didn't matter if it waited until morning, citing Higgins's provisions and the money already left with Bessy. To Mrs. Hale, such pragmatism was cold-blooded cruelty. She scolded her daughter for being unfeeling, refusing to draw breath until the basket was gone, carried out into the gray afternoon.

Only then, exhaling sharply, did she allow herself a moment of reflection. "After all, we may have been doing wrong," she whispered, the guilt settling heavy in her chest. "It was only the last time Mr. Thornton was here that he said, those were no true friends who helped to prolong the struggle by assisting the turn-outs. And this Boucher-man was a turn-out, was he not?"

When Mr. Hale descended the stairs, fresh from a lesson with Mr. Thornton that had devolved into heated debate, his wife posed the question to him. Margaret stood aside, her mind too reeling with recent horrors to engage in the abstract moral calculus of political strategy.

Mr. Hale listened, striving for the detached impartiality of a judge. He recalled the clarity of Mr. Thornton's arguments from moments prior, yet found himself forced into an uneasy compromise. In this specific instance, he conceded that his wife and daughter had acted rightly; he could not envision any other path. Yet, as a general principle, he acknowledged the validity of Mr. Thornton's warning: a prolonged strike inevitably drove masters to import distant hands or invent machines to render labor obsolete. Therefore, the kindest act was often to withhold aid that merely propped up folly. However, regarding Boucher specifically, he resolved to visit him first thing in the morning to ascertain what could truly be done.

True to his word, Mr. Hale visited the following dawn. Boucher was absent, but his wife remained. He secured an order for the Infirmary and, observing the abundance Mrs. Hale had provided—which the children, acting as temporary masters in their father's absence, consumed with lavish disregard—he returned with a report far more consoling than Margaret had dared to hope. Indeed, her previous fears had primed his imagination to expect catastrophe; the reaction of his relief colored his description, making the situation appear significantly better than it objectively was.

"I will go again, and see the man himself," Mr. Hale declared, his brow furrowed in contemplation. "I hardly know as yet how to compare one of these houses with our Helstone cottages. I see furniture here which our labourers would never have thought of buying, and food commonly used which they would consider luxuries; yet for these very families there seems no other resource now that their weekly wages are stopped, but the pawn-shop. One had need to learn a different standard, up here in Milton."

By evening, Bessy showed signs of improvement, though her weakness was profound. She seemed to have entirely forgotten her earlier feverish desire to witness Margaret's transformation, or perhaps that impulse had been a hallucination born of delirium. Margaret, meanwhile, found herself trapped in a surreal ritual of preparation. Dressed for a destination she dreaded, her heart burdened by the anxieties of the day, she could not help but contrast this strange toilette with the joyous, girlish experiments she and Edith had shared barely a year ago. Her only motivation now was the knowledge that her mother would derive pleasure from seeing her adorned.

Dixon flung open the drawing-room door, breaking her reverie with an appeal for approval. "Miss Hale looks well, ma'am,—doesn't she? Mrs. Shaw's coral couldn't have come in better. It just gives the right touch of colour, ma'am. Otherwise, Miss Margaret, you would have been too pale."

Margaret's hair, too thick for plaits, was coiled tightly into massive spirals that crowned her head, secured by two large coral pins resembling small arrows. Her white silk sleeves were bound with matching cords, and heavy coral beads rested against the curve of her milk-white throat.

"Oh, Margaret! how I should like to be going with you to one of the old Barrington assemblies,—taking you as Lady Beresford used to take me," her mother exclaimed, a burst of vanity escaping her.

Margaret kissed her cheek, suppressing a smile that felt foreign on her lips. "I would rather stay at home with you,—much rather, mamma."

"Nonsense, darling! Be sure you notice the dinner well. I shall like to hear how they manage these things in Milton. Particularly the second course, dear. Look what they have instead of game."

Had Mrs. Hale witnessed the sumptuous array of the dining table, she would have been astounded. To Margaret, accustomed to the refined restraint of London tastes, the profusion of delicacies felt oppressive; half the quantity would have sufficed for a tighter, more elegant presentation. Yet, this excess was dictated by Mrs. Thornton's rigid code of hospitality: every guest must have access to every dainty, regardless of inclination. For Mrs. Thornton, frugal in private, setting a feast was a point of immense pride. Her son shared this sentiment. Though he denied himself the trivial expense of a sixpence and harbored regrets over sending the invitations, he nonetheless welcomed the opportunity to display the old magnificence of preparation.

Margaret and her father arrived first. Mr. Hale was punctual to the minute. The drawing-room was empty save for Mrs. Thornton and Fanny. Every cover had been removed, revealing a space ablaze with yellow silk damask and a carpet of brilliant flowers. Ornamentation crowded every corner until the eye grew weary, creating a stark, jarring contrast to the bald ugliness visible through the great folding doors leading to the mill-yard, where gates stood open for carriages. The mill loomed high on the left, casting a premature shadow over the summer evening.

"My son was engaged up to the last moment on business. He will be here directly, Mr. Hale. May I beg you to take a seat?"

Mr. Hale stood by a window as she spoke. Turning away, he queried, "Don't you find such close neighbourhood to the mill rather unpleasant at times?"

Mrs. Thornton drew herself up, her posture rigid. "Never. I am not become so fine as to desire to forget the source of my son's wealth and power. Besides, there is not such another factory in Milton. One room alone is two hundred and twenty square yards."

"I meant that the smoke and the noise—the constant going out and coming in of the workpeople, might be annoying!"

"I agree with you, Mr. Hale!" Fanny interjected quickly. "There is a continual smell of steam and oily machinery—and the noise is perfectly deafening."

"I have heard noise that was called music far more deafening," Mr. Hale countered calmly. "The engine-room is at the street-end of the factory; we hardly hear it, except in summer weather, when all the windows are open; and as for the continual murmur of the workpeople, it disturbs me no more than the humming of a hive of bees. If I think of it at all, I connect it with my son, and feel how all belongs to him, and that his is the head that directs it. Just now, there are no sounds to come from the mill; the hands have been ungrateful enough to turn out

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of the city itself. She stood in the doorway of the drawing-room, her white silk dress, usually a symbol of her gentle femininity, feeling suddenly stark against the gloom. Outside, the streets were clean, the night fine, yet inside, the air was thick with the weight of impending doom.

"You look pale, Margaret," her father said, his voice trembling slightly as he steadied himself against the doorframe. "And you've thrown off your gown?"

"I couldn't stay in it, Papa," she replied, her voice barely audible over the storm. "It feels like a costume for a party I'm no longer invited to."

Earlier that evening, walking home from the ball, they had discussed Mr. Thornton. Margaret recalled his cool demeanor, the way he had spoken with detached precision while the workers' anger simmered beneath the surface. "He seemed very anxious tonight," she had told her father then. Now, looking at him, she realized her father was right; his anxiety was a physical ache, a tension that pulled at his shoulders. He was a man of immense strength of character, forged in the fires of practical necessity, yet he carried the burden of being perceived as a 'hard man,' a title that felt heavier than any stone.

"He is proud," her father had argued earlier, defending Thornton's stoicism. "Too proud to show his feelings. A man of unusual intellect, despite his lack of advantage."

"And yet," Margaret had countered softly, "he stands upon his rights as no human being ought to, considering what we and all our petty rights are in the sight of the Almighty."

Now, as the doctor's carriage rattled away, leaving them alone with the silence of the house, the reality crashed down. The servant Dixon burst into the room, her face a mask of terror. "Oh, Master! Oh, Miss Margaret! Thank God you are come! Dr. Donaldson is here. The charwoman is gone home. She's better now; but, oh sir! I thought she'd have died an hour ago!"

Mr. Hale caught Margaret's arm, his knees buckling under the shock. He looked at her, expecting the same calm composure she had shown throughout the day, but found instead an expression of supreme sorrow mixed with a terrifying clarity. She knew more than he did, yet she listened with that hopeless, awed apprehension that only comes when one realizes the finality of loss.

"Oh! I should not have left her—wicked daughter that I am!" Margaret moaned, supporting her father's stumbling steps as they ascended the stairs.

Dr. Donaldson met them on the landing, his face grim. "She is better now," he whispered urgently. "The opiate has taken effect. The spasms were very bad... but she'll rally this time."

"This time!" The word hung in the air, a cruel echo. In that instant, Mr. Hale aged decades. His sight dimmed, his senses wavered, his walk tottered as if he were seventy years old. Dr. Donaldson took his arm, leading him into the bedroom where his wife lay. She slept, but the look on her face was unmistakable; Death had signed her for his own, and it was clear he would return soon to take possession.

Mr. Hale stared at her for a long time without a word. Then, he began to shake all over. Turning away from the doctor's anxious care, he groped blindly for the door, unable to see it despite the flaring candles. He staggered into the drawing-room and collapsed onto a chair.

"Speak to him, Miss Hale. We must rouse him."

"Papa!" Margaret cried, her voice wild with pain. "Papa! Speak to me!"

The speculation returned to his eyes, clouding his reason. "Margaret, did you know of this? Oh, it was cruel of you!"

"No, sir, it was not cruel!" Dr. Donaldson interjected with quick decision. "Miss Hale acted under my directions. There may have been a mistake, but it was not cruel. Your wife will be a different creature tomorrow, I trust. She has had spasms, as I anticipated... she will have a good long sleep; and tomorrow that look which has alarmed you so much will have passed away."

"But not the disease?"

Dr. Donaldson glanced at Margaret. Her bent head, her face raised with no appeal for a temporary reprieve, showed that quick observer of human nature that she understood the gravity of the situation.

"Not the disease. We cannot touch the disease, with all our poor vaunted skill. We can only delay its progress—alleviate the pain it causes. Be a man, sir—a Christian. Have faith in the immortality of the soul, which no pain, no mortal disease, can assail or touch!"

But all the reply he got was in the choked words, "You have never been married, Dr. Donaldson; you don't know what it is," and in the deep, manly sobs that went through the stillness of the night like heavy pulses of agony.

Margaret knelt beside him, caressing him with tearful touches. Time lost its meaning. Mr. Hale, realizing the necessity of the present moment, asked, "What must we do? Tell us both. Margaret is my staff—my right hand."

Dr. Donaldson gave his clear, sensible directions. No fear for tonight, nay, even peace for tomorrow and for many days yet. But no enduring hope of recovery. He advised Mr. Hale to go to bed, leaving only one to watch the slumber. He promised to return early in the morning. With a warm, kindly shake of the hand, he left them.

They spoke but few words; they were too exhausted by their terror to do more than decide upon the immediate course of action. Mr. Hale was resolved to sit up through the night, and all Margaret could do was to prevail upon him to rest on the drawing-room sofa. Dixon stoutly and bluntly refused to go to bed; as for Margaret, it was simply impossible that she should leave her mother, let all the doctors in the world speak of "husbanding resources," and "one watcher only being required."

So, Dixon sat, staring, winking, drooping, picking herself up again with a jerk, and finally giving up the battle, fairly snoring. Margaret had taken off her gown and tossed it aside with a sort of impatient disgust, putting on her dressing-gown instead. She felt as if she could never sleep again; her whole senses were acutely vital, endowed with double keenness for the purpose of watching. Every sight and sound—even every thought—touched some nerve to the very quick.

For more than two hours, she heard her father's restless movements in the next room. He came perpetually to the door of her mother's chamber, pausing there to listen, until she went and opened it to tell him how all went on, in reply to the questions his baked lips could hardly form. At last, he too fell asleep, and all the house was still.

Margaret sat behind the curtain, thinking. Far away in time, far away in space, seemed all the interests of past days. Not more than thirty-six hours ago, she had cared for Bessy Higgins and her father, and her heart had been wrung for Boucher; now, that was all like a dreaming memory of some former life. Everything that had passed outdoors seemed dissevered from her mother, and therefore unreal. Even Harley Street appeared more distinct; there she remembered, as if it were yesterday, how she had pleased herself with tracing out her mother's features in her Aunt Shaw's face—and how letters had come, making her dwell on the thoughts of home with all the longing of love. Helstone itself was in the dim past. The dull gray days of the preceding winter and spring, so uneventful and monotonous, seemed more associated with what she cared for now above all price. She would fain have caught at the skirts of that departing time, praying it to return, and give her back what she had too little valued while it was yet in her possession.

What a vain show Life seemed! How unsubstantial, and flickering, and flitting! It was as if from some aerial belfry, high up above the stir and jar of the earth, there was a bell continually tolling, "All are shadows!—all are passing!—all is past!"

When the morning dawned, cool and gray, like many a happier morning before, Margaret looked one by one at the sleepers. It seemed as if the terrible night were unreal as a dream; it, too, was a shadow. It, too, was past.

Mrs. Hale herself was not aware when she awoke how ill she had been the night before. She was rather surprised at Dr. Donaldson's early visit, and perplexed by the anxious faces of husband and child. She consented to remain in bed that day, saying she certainly was tired; but the next, she insisted on getting up; and Dr. Donaldson gave his consent to her returning into the drawing-room. She was restless and uncomfortable in every position, and before night she became very feverish. Mr. Hale was utterly listless, and incapable of deciding on anything.

"What can we do to spare mamma such another night?" Margaret asked on the third day.

"It is to a certain degree, the reaction after the powerful opiates I have been obliged to use. It is more painful for you to see than for her to bear, I believe

The air in the drawing-room tasted of dust and suppressed panic. Though the windows hung half-open to combat the stifling heat, the Venetian blinds cast a gray, distorted gloom across the floorboards, reflecting the chaotic energy of the street below. This unnatural light, mingled with the sickly green hue filtering from above, rendered even Margaret Hale's own reflection in the mirror ghostly and wan. She sat, waiting, the silence of the room pressing against her ears until it became deafening. Occasionally, a phantom gust seemed to carry the distant, multitudinous roar closer, only to die away into a profound, terrifying stillness.

Finally, Fanny burst in, her face a mask of hysteria.

"Mamma will be with you directly, Miss Hale," she gasped, her voice trembling. "She begged me to apologize for the delay. You must understand, my brother has imported hands from Ireland, and it has inflamed the Milton populace to a frenzy—as if he lacked the right to secure labor wherever he could! These fools refused to work for him, and now, terrified by their own threats, they dare not let the poor Irish starvelings out. You can see them huddled in the attic of the mill, kept there for safety from the brutes who will neither work nor allow them to. Mamma is arranging their food, and John is attempting to calm the women, some of whom are weeping to return. Oh! Here comes Mamma!"

Mrs. Thornton entered, her expression carved from black stone, a look that made Margaret feel acutely guilty for intruding during such a crisis. Yet, she remained, compelled by Mrs. Thornton's earlier instruction to attend to her mother's needs. Mrs. Thornton's brow furrowed deeper, her mouth tightening into a rigid line as Margaret spoke softly of her mother's restlessness and Dr. Donaldson's urgent recommendation for a water-bed. Silence stretched between them, taut and brittle.

Then, Mrs. Thornton sprang upright, her composure shattering.

"They're at the gates! Call John, Fanny—bring him in from the mill! They're battering the gates! They'll tear them down! Call John, I say!"

Simultaneously, the gathering thrum she had ignored while listening to Margaret erupted just outside the wall. An escalating cacophony of angry voices roared behind the wooden barrier, shaking it as if the unseen, maddened mob had transformed their bodies into living battering rams. They recoiled briefly, only to surge forward with renewed, unified momentum, their collective weight making the sturdy gates quiver like reeds before a hurricane.

Women flocked to the windows, transfixed by the terrifying spectacle. Mrs. Thornton, the maids, and Margaret stood frozen. Fanny had fled upstairs, screaming as if pursued by demons, collapsing onto the sofa in hysterical sobs. Mrs. Thornton scanned the room for her son, who remained in the mill. He emerged, looking up at the pale cluster of faces, offering a smile of grim encouragement before locking the factory door. He signaled to a servant to unlock his own entrance, which Fanny had hastily barred in her panic. Mrs. Thornton stepped forward herself.

The sound of Mr. Thornton's familiar, commanding voice seemed to strike the infuriated multitude like a physical blow. Until now, they had been voiceless, breath spent in their futile attempts to breach the defenses. Now, hearing him speak from within, they unleashed a fierce, unearthly groan that turned Mrs. Thornton's face deathly pale as she led him into the room. He appeared slightly flushed, but his eyes gleamed with the fire of a warrior answering a trumpet call. With a proud, defiant set to his jaw, he looked less like a man and more like a noble figure forged in conflict. Margaret, who had always feared her own courage might crumble in an emergency, found herself forgotten in the intensity of the moment. She felt only a piercing, painful sympathy for the unfolding tragedy.

Mr. Thornton stepped forward with frank authority.

"I am sorry, Miss Hale, that you have visited us at this ill-fated hour, when you may be drawn into our peril. Mother, would you not retire to the back rooms? I suspect they may have breached the path from Pinner's Lane into the stable-yard; if not, you will be safer there. Go, Jane!"

He commanded the upper servant, who obeyed, followed closely by the others.

"I remain here," Mr. Thornton declared firmly.

His mother refused to move. "Where you stand, I shall stay." Indeed, retreat was futile; the crowd had already surrounded the outbuildings at the rear, unleashing a terrifying roar from behind. The servants scrambled into the garrets, their cries and shrieks echoing in the chaos. Mr. Thornton watched them with a sneer of disdain. He glanced at Margaret, who stood alone by the window nearest the factory. Her eyes glittered, her cheeks flushed with a deep, feverish color. Sensing his gaze, she turned to him, voicing a question that had been brewing in her mind:

"Where are the poor imported workers? Are they still in the factory?"

"Yes," he replied. "I left them cowering in a small room at the head of the back staircase, instructing them to flee downward if they sensed an assault on the mill doors. But it is not them—they seek me."

"When can the soldiers arrive?" his mother asked, her voice low but steady.

He consulted his watch with the same composed precision he applied to all things, performing a quick mental calculation.

"If Williams departed immediately upon my command and avoided the crush, it will be twenty minutes yet."

"Twenty minutes!" his mother exclaimed, her terror finally breaking through in her tone.

"Close the windows instantly, Mother! The gates cannot withstand another shock. Close that window, Miss Hale."

Margaret hurried to shut hers, then moved to support Mrs. Thornton's trembling fingers. For several minutes, the unseen street held its breath. Mrs. Thornton stared wildly at her son's face, searching for an interpretation of the sudden silence. His features were set in rigid lines of contemptuous defiance; neither hope nor fear could be deciphered there.

Fanny stirred, raising herself weakly. "Are they gone?" she whispered.

"Gone!" he answered sharply. "Listen!"

They all heard it: the single, great strain of held breath, the slow creak of wood surrendering, the wrench of iron, and the thunderous crash of the ponderous gates falling inward. Fanny staggered, took a step toward her mother, and collapsed into her arms, overcome by faintness. Mrs. Thornton lifted her with a strength born of sheer will, carrying her away.

"Thank God!" Mr. Thornton breathed as he watched her exit. "Would you not prefer to go upstairs, Miss Hale?"

Margaret's lips formed a silent "No," but her words were lost beneath the tramp of innumerable footsteps pounding against the very foundation of the house and the fierce, low growl of angry voices that possessed a ferocious, satisfied murmur, far more dreadful than their frantic cries moments prior.

"Never mind!" he said, attempting to reassure her. "I regret that you were trapped in this alarm, but it cannot endure long. A few more minutes, and the soldiers will be here."

"Oh, God!" Margaret cried suddenly. "There is Boucher! I recognize his face, though he is livid with rage—he is fighting desperately to reach the front! Look! Look!"

"Who is Boucher?" Mr. Thornton asked coolly, stepping closer to the window to identify the man who captured Margaret's attention. Instantly, the crowd erupted in a yell so primal it bordered on the demonic—a ravenous hunger for the sustenance being denied them. Even Mr. Thornton drew back momentarily, dismayed by the intensity of the hatred radiating from the mob.

"Let them yell!" he shouted back. "In five minutes more... I only pray my poor Irishmen are not terrified out of their wits by such fiendish noise. Hold your courage for five minutes, Miss Hale."

"Do not fear for me," she said hastily. "But what happens in five minutes? Can you do nothing to soothe these poor creatures? It is horrifying to witness them."

"The soldiers will arrive directly, and that will restore their reason."

"To reason!" Margaret snapped, her voice rising. "What kind of reason?"

"The only reason that governs men who transform themselves into wild beasts. By heavens! They have turned toward the mill-door!"

"Mr. Thornton," Margaret pleaded, her whole body shaking with passion, "go down this instant, if you possess any shred of courage. Do not be a coward. Go down and face them like a man. Save these poor strangers whom you have lured here. Speak to your workmen as if they were human beings. Speak kindly. Do not wait for the soldiers to slaughter these mad creatures. I see one there who is already broken. If you have any nobility left in you, go out and address them, man to man!"

He turned to regard her as she spoke. A dark cloud settled over his face as he listened. He gritted his teeth, absorbing her words.

"I will go. Perhaps I may ask you to accompany me downstairs and bar the door behind me; my mother and sister require that protection."

"Oh! Mr. Thornton! I do not know—I may be mistaken—only—"

But he was already gone. Downstairs in the hall, he had unbolted the front door. All Margaret could do was follow him swiftly, latch it shut behind him, and climb the stairs again with a sick heart and a dizzy head

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the millmaster's dining room, a rhythmic drumming that felt less like weather and more like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of the industrial age. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of beeswax, damp wool, and the lingering, metallic tang of violence. Margaret Hale had not been gone five minutes when Mr. Thornton burst through the doorway, his face flushed with a heat that seemed to radiate from within, defying the chill of the storm.

"I could not come sooner," he panted, his voice tight with adrenaline, "the superintendent would—Where is she?"

His gaze swept the room, landing with almost fierce intensity on his mother, who was quietly rearranging the displaced chairs, her movements efficient and unflappable. She did not answer immediately.

"Where is Miss Hale?" he demanded again.

"Gone home," Mrs. Thornton replied, her tone clipped, cutting through the tension like a shears blade.

"Gone home!" The exclamation hung in the air, charged with disbelief.

"Yes. She was a great deal better. Indeed, I don't believe it was so very much of a hurt; only some people faint at the least thing."

"I am sorry she has gone home," Thornton muttered, pacing the rug with restless energy. "She could not have been fit for it."

"She said she was; and Mr. Lowe said she was. I went for him myself."

"Thank you, mother." He paused, extending a hand in a gesture of gratitude. She ignored it, her focus returning to the furniture.

"What have you done with your Irish people?" she asked, her voice devoid of warmth.

"Sent to the Dragon, for a good meal for them, poor wretches. And then, luckily, I caught Father Grady, and I've asked him in to speak to them, and dissuade them from going off in a body. How did Miss Hale go home? I'm sure she could not walk."

"She had a cab. Everything was done properly, even to the paying. Let us talk of something else. She has caused disturbance enough."

"I don't know where I should have been but for her."

"Are you become so helpless as to have to be defended by a girl?" Mrs. Thornton asked, her scorn dripping like acid.

Thornton flinched, the color rising higher in his cheeks. "Not many girls would have taken the blows on herself which were meant for me;—meant with right down goodwill, too."

"A girl in love will do a good deal," she replied, her voice hardening.

"Mother!"

He stepped forward, then halted, his chest heaving with the effort to suppress a surge of raw emotion. The atmosphere in the room shifted, becoming electric with an unspoken conflict. She was unsettled by the sheer force of his restraint, unsure of the tempest he was trying to dam. Was it anger? Joy? Pride? A panting mixture of desperate hope and terrifying doubt? She could not decipher the code written in his glowing eyes, yet the mere presence of such volatile feeling made her skin crawl.

She moved to the sideboard, opening a drawer to retrieve a duster, ostensibly to wipe a spot of eau de Cologne from the sofa arm. But she lingered with her back turned to her son far longer than necessity required. When she finally spoke, her voice sounded strained, artificial.

"You have taken some steps about the rioters, I suppose? You don't apprehend anymore violence, do you? Where were the police? Never at hand when they're wanted!"

"On the contrary," Thornton countered, his composure snapping back into place. "I saw three or four of them, when the gates gave way, struggling and beating about in fine fashion; and more came running up just when the yard was clearing. I might have given some of the fellows in charge then, if I had had my wits about me. But there will be no difficulty, plenty of people can identify them."

"But won't they come back to-night?"

"I'm going to see about a sufficient guard for the premises. I have appointed to meet Captain Hanbury in half an hour at the station."

"You must have some tea first."

"Tea! Yes, I suppose I must. It's half-past six, and I may be out for some time. Don't sit up for me, mother."

"You expect me to go to bed before I have seen you safe, do you?"

"Well, perhaps not." He hesitated. "But if I've time, I shall go round by Crampton, after I've arranged with the police and seen Hamper and Clarkson."

Their eyes locked. For a minute, the room seemed to hold its breath under the weight of their mutual scrutiny. Then she broke the silence.

"Why are you going round by Crampton?"

"To ask after Miss Hale."

"I will send. Williams must take the water-bed she came to ask for. He shall inquire how she is."

"I must go myself."

"Not merely to ask how Miss Hale is?"

"No, not merely for that. I want to thank her for the way in which she stood between me and the mob."

"What made you go down at all? It was putting your head into the lion's mouth!"

He glanced sharply at her, sensing the barrier she erected between them, knowing she knew nothing of the private exchange that had occurred in the drawing room. He deflected with another question.

"Shall you be afraid to be left without me, until I can get some of the police; or had we better send Williams for them now, and they could be here by the time we have done tea? There's no time to be lost. I must be off in a quarter of an hour."

Mrs. Thornton left the room. Her servants watched her departure with confusion; the woman whose commands were usually sharp as flint now moved with a strange uncertainty. Thornton remained in the dining room, ostensibly planning his route to the police office, but his mind was already drifting, tethered to the memory of Margaret. The future seemed dim and vague, overshadowed only by the phantom sensation of her arms around his neck, the soft clinging that had sent a flush racing across his cheeks.

The tea service was stiflingly silent, broken only by Fanny's incessant narration of her own terror—how she had been alarmed, then relieved, then sick with trembling dread.

"There, that's enough," Thornton said abruptly, rising from the table. "The reality was enough for me."

He reached for the door, but his mother's hand fell upon his arm, halting him.

"You will come back here before you go to the Hales'," she whispered, her voice low and fraught with anxiety.

Fanny, listening from the corner, pressed her lips together. *Why? Will it be too late to disturb them?*

"John, come back to me for this one evening. It will be late for Mrs. Hale. But that is not it. To-morrow, you will—— Come back to-night, John!"

It was rare for Mrs. Thornton to plead with her son; her pride was a fortress she rarely breached. Yet, she had never pleaded in vain.

"I will return straight here after I have done my business. You will be sure to enquire after them?—after her?"

Mrs. Thornton was not a talkative companion, nor a patient listener while her son was away. But upon his return, her senses sharpened, eager to dissect every detail of his actions, to verify the steps he had taken to fortify his position and protect those he deemed worthy. He saw his objective clearly now: punishment and suffering were the natural consequences for the rioters. Property must be protected, and the will of the proprietor enforced with the clean, sharp precision of a sword.

"Mother! You know what I have got to say to Miss Hale, to-morrow?"

The question struck her unexpectedly, breaking through her reverie. She looked up at him.

"Yes! I do. You can hardly do otherwise."

"Do otherwise! I don't understand you."

"I mean that, after allowing her feelings so to overcome her, I consider you bound in honour—"

"Bound in honour," he scoffed. "I'm afraid honour has nothing to do with it. 'Her feelings overcome her!' What feelings do you mean?"

"Nay, John, there is no need to be angry. Did she not rush down and cling to you to save you from danger?"

"She did!" he admitted.

"But, mother," he continued, stopping dead in his tracks directly before her, "I dare not hope. I never was faint-hearted before; but I cannot believe such a creature cares for me."

"Don't be foolish, John. Such a creature! Why, she might be a duke's daughter, to hear you speak. And what proof more would you have, I wonder, of her caring for you? I can believe she has had a struggle with her aristocratic way of viewing things; but I like her the better for seeing clearly at last. It is a good deal for me to say," Mrs. Thornton smiled slowly, tears welling in her eyes, "for after to-night, I stand second. It was to have you to myself, all to myself, a few hours longer, that I begged you not to go till to-morrow."

"Dearest mother!"

In that instant, love proved its inherent selfishness. He retreated into his own hopes and fears, casting a cold, creeping shadow over his mother's heart.

"But I know she does not care for me. I shall put myself at her feet—I must

The morning dawned with a brittle clarity, stripping away the fever dreams of the previous night. Margaret dragged herself upright, her limbs heavy with exhaustion yet strangely buoyant by the sheer relief that the ordeal was concluded. The house had remained a sanctuary of silence; her mother had stirred but once, a fleeting ripple in the otherwise still water. Outside, a faint breeze began to stir the stagnant heat, carrying with it the promise of movement. Though the garden offered no canopy of leaves to dance in the wind, Margaret's imagination conjured the scene anyway—somewhere beyond the walls, in the wilder spaces of copse or wood, the air must be filled with that familiar, murmuring rush, the chaotic joy of falling foliage. The mere thought of it ignited a distinct gladness within her chest, a warm counterpoint to the chill that still lingered in her bones.

Seated at her embroidery frame in Mrs. Hale's room, she constructed a fortress of routine. Once the morning slumber faded, she would assist her mother with dressing; after dinner, she would visit Bessy Higgins. Her plan was to excise the memory of the Thorntons entirely—to banish them until they physically manifested before her again. Yet, the paradox of suppression proved its own undoing; the harder she tried to push the image away, the more vividly it burned itself into her mind. Periodically, a hot flush would sweep across her pallid cheeks, spreading color as rapidly as a sunbeam breaks through watery clouds to strike the sea below.

Dixon entered silently, his footsteps swallowed by the carpet as he approached the shaded window where Margaret sat. "Mr. Thornton, Miss Margaret," he whispered, his voice barely audible. "He is in the drawing-room."

Margaret dropped her needle, the thread snapping with a tiny, sharp sound. "Did he ask for me?" she queried, her voice tight. "Has Papa returned?"

"He requested you, miss," Dixon replied, bowing slightly. "Master is currently out."

"Very well," Margaret said, her composure returning instantly, though her heart hammered against her ribs. "I will come."

Yet she hesitated, lingering in the doorway as if anchored by invisible chains. Inside the drawing-room, Mr. Thornton stood before a window, his back to the entrance, ostensibly studying the street. In truth, he was paralyzed by his own reflection in the glass—or rather, by the terrifying prospect of hers. His heart thundered, a frantic drumbeat echoing the memory of her arms wrapped around his neck earlier that day. That touch, once felt as an intrusion, now resonated through him with a devastating intimacy, melting his resolve like wax before a flame. He dreaded the approach, terrified that if he moved forward with arms outstretched in silent plea, she would retreat, or worse, accept him with the same unthinking devotion she had shown the day before. The possibility of her acceptance thrilled him, while the specter of her rejection withered his future into a barren wasteland.

Then, the subtle shift in the room's atmosphere alerted him. He turned. She had entered so softly that the distant clamor of the street had drowned out her arrival. Clad in soft muslin, she stood by the table, refusing to sit. Her eyelids were lowered, casting a shadow over her eyes; her lips were parted just enough to reveal the pale line beneath, breathing in slow, shallow rhythms that expanded her delicate nostrils. It was the only motion on her face. Her complexion was wan, stripped of its usual vitality, the heavy curtain of dark hair framing her temples serving to obscure any trace of the blow she had sustained. Despite the drooping eyes, her head was tilted back in that familiar posture of proud defiance. Her long arms hung limp at her sides. She resembled a prisoner awaiting judgment for a crime she despised, too indignant to offer a defense.

Thornton took a hurried step forward, then corrected himself, marching with quiet firmness to the open door to shut it out. Returning to the center of the room, he stood opposite her, absorbing the full weight of her presence before daring to disrupt it.

"Miss Hale," he began, his voice rough. "I was very ungrateful yesterday—"

"You had nothing to be grateful for," she interrupted, raising her eyes to meet his gaze with a gravity that silenced him. "You imply, I suppose, that I ought to thank you for what I did."

Despite her fury, a thick blush surged across her face, burning into her very eyes, yet she maintained her steady, unflinching stare. "It was merely a natural instinct," she said quickly, her hands clasped tightly together. "Any woman would have acted the same. We all feel the sanctity of our sex as a high privilege when faced with danger. I ought, rather, to apologize to you for those thoughtless words which drove you into peril."

"It was not your words; it was the truth they conveyed, expressed with such piercing force," he countered, stopping short of the confession that threatened to spill forth. He forced himself to pause, weighing each syllable, his will triumphing over his passion. "I do not seek to escape anything," she declared, her voice cool. "I simply state that you owe me no gratitude. Furthermore, any expression of it would be painful to me, for I do not feel deserving of it. Nevertheless, if speaking will alleviate even a phantom obligation, proceed."

"I do not wish to be relieved of any duty," he insisted, driven mad by her serene demeanor. "Whether fancied or real, I choose to believe I owe my very life to you—yes, smile if you deem it hyperbole. I believe it, because it imbues this life with value, doubling my joy, sharpening my pride, intensifying my sense of existence until I can scarcely distinguish pain from pleasure in knowing I owe it to one... nay, you must listen," he stepped forward, his voice dropping to a tender, terrifying intensity that made her shiver, "to one whom I love, as I do not believe man has ever loved woman before."

He seized her hand, gripping it tightly, waiting for her response. Instead, he recoiled in indignation as she spoke in an icy tone, her words faltering as if searching for purchase on solid ground. "Your manner shocks me. It is blasphemous. I cannot control my initial reaction. It might differ if I understood the sentiment you describe. I do not wish to vex you; moreover, we must speak gently, for my mother sleeps. Your entire demeanor offends me."

"How!" he exploded. "Offends you! I am truly most unfortunate."

"Yes!" she affirmed, reclaiming her dignity. "I feel offended, and I believe justly. You seem to imagine my actions yesterday were a personal transaction between us, inviting your thanks. Do you fail to perceive, as a gentleman would—that any woman worthy of the title would step forward to shield a man from the violence of the mob with her revered helplessness?"

"And the gentleman thus rescued is denied the relief of thanks!" he retorted, his voice dripping with contempt. "I am a man. I claim the right to express my feelings."

"And I yielded to that right," she replied, her chin lifted high. "Simply stating that you inflicted pain by insisting upon it. But you appear to have misconceived my motives. I was not merely guided by feminine instinct, but..." Passionate tears, suppressed for so long, welled in her eyes, choking her voice. "...but prompted by some particular feeling for *you*! Why, there was not a single man—not a desperate soul in that entire crowd—for whom I did not harbor greater sympathy, nor for whom I would not have done more heartily."

"You may continue, Miss Hale," he said, acknowledging the irony of her misplaced sympathies. "I now realize it was only your innate sense of oppression—yes, I, though a master, may also be oppressed—that compelled such noble action. I know you despise me; permit me to say, it is precisely because you do not understand me."

"I have no desire to understand," she snapped, clutching the table to steady herself. She perceived his cruelty, as he intended, and weakness overwhelmed her indignation. "No, I see clearly. You are unfair and unjust."

Margaret pressed her lips together, refusing to respond to such accusations. Yet, despite his savage words, he could have cast himself at her feet, kissing the hem of her garment. She remained motionless, silent. Hot tears of wounded pride streamed down her face. He waited, hoping for a taunt, a spark of engagement to which he could reply. Silence stretched between them, heavy and suffocating. Finally, he picked up his hat.

"One final word," he said. "You look as though being loved by me taints you. You cannot avoid it. Nor can I, if I wished, cleanse you of it. But I would not, if I could. I have never loved a woman before; my life was too busy, my thoughts consumed by other matters. Now I love, and I will love. But do not fear excessive expression on my part."

"I am not afraid," she replied, rising to her full height. "No one has ever dared to be impertinent to me, and no one ever shall. However, Mr. Thornton, you have been remarkably kind to my father." Her tone shifted, transforming from steel to a soft, vulnerable femininity. "Let us cease making each other angry. Please, do not!"

He ignored her plea, preoccupied for half a minute smoothing the nap of his hat with his sleeve. Rejecting her extended hand and dismissing her grave expression of regret, he turned abruptly and exited

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a city that had forgotten the quiet suffocation of its women. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Mr. Lennox, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest thoughts on literature and the boundaries of affection.

Margaret traced the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener, her movements slow and deliberate. Lennox was a man of undeniable erudition, yet his proposals were suffocated by the very patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of friendship sliding imperceptibly into love, positioning himself as the benevolent instructor to her "undefeated resolution to admire only the right things."

"The right things," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned mentally to the other letter, the one that burned brighter than any ink on paper. Mr. Thornton. In Lennox's case, there had been an intervening stage of friendship, a bridge crossed and immediately regretted. But with Thornton, there was no bridge. Their intercourse had been one continued series of opposition. His opinions clashed with hers, and indeed, she had never perceived that he had cared for her opinions as belonging to her, the individual. As far as they defied his rock-like power of character, his passion-strength, he seemed to throw them off from him with contempt, until she felt the weariness of the exertion of making useless protests.

Now, he had come, in this strange, wild, passionate way, to make known his love. Even before he left the room—and certainly not five minutes after—the clear conviction had dawned upon her, shining bright and terrible, that he did love her; that he had loved her; that he would love her. She shrank and shuddered as under the fascination of some great power, repugnant to her whole previous life. She crept away, hiding from his idea. But it was of no use. To parody a line out of Fairfax's *Tasso*:

"His strong idea wandered through her thought."

She disliked him the more for having mastered her inner will. How dared he say that he would love her still, even though she shook him off with contempt? She wished she had spoken more—stronger. Sharp, decisive speeches came thronging into her mind, now that it was too late to utter them. The deep impression made by the interview was like that of a horror in a dream; that will not leave the room although we waken up, and rub our eyes, and force a stiff, rigid smile upon our lips. It is there—there, cowering and gibbering, with fixed ghastly eyes, in some corner of the chamber, listening to hear whether we dare to breathe of its presence to any one. And we dare not; poor cowards that we are!

And so she shuddered away from the threat of his enduring love. What did he mean? Had she not the power to daunt him? She would see. It was more daring than became a man to threaten her so. Did he ground it upon the miserable yesterday? If need were, she would do the same tomorrow—by a crippled beggar, willingly and gladly—but by him, she would do it, just as bravely, in spite of his deductions, and the cold slime of woman's impertinence. She did it because it was right, and simple, and true to save where she could save; even to try to save. *Fais ce que dois, advienne que pourra.*

Hitherto she had not stirred from where he had left her; no outward circumstances had roused her out of the trance of thought in which she had been plunged by his last words, and by the look of his deep, intent, passionate eyes, as their flames had made her own fall before them. She went to the window, and threw it open, to dispel the oppression which hung around her. Then she went and opened the door, with a sort of impetuous wish to shake off the recollection of the past hour, in the company of others, or in active exertion. But all was profoundly hushed in the noonday stillness of a house, where an invalid catches the unrefreshing sleep that is denied to the night-hours. Margaret would not be alone. What should she do? "Go and see Bessy Higgins, of course," thought she, as the recollection of the message sent the night before flashed into her mind. And away she went.

When she got there, she found Bessy lying on the settle, moved close to the fire, though the day was sultry and oppressive. She was laid down quite flat, as if resting languidly after some paroxysm of pain. Margaret felt sure she ought to have the greater freedom of breathing which a more sitting posture would procure; and, without a word, she raised her up, and so arranged the pillows, that Bessy was more at ease, though very languid.

"I thought I should na' ha' seen yo' again," said she, at last, looking wistfully in Margaret's face.

"I'm afraid you're much worse. But I could not have come yesterday, my mother was so ill—for many reasons," said Margaret, colouring.

"You'd m'appen think I went beyond my place in sending Mary for yo'. But the wraglin' and the loud voices had just torn me to pieces, and I thought when father left, oh! if I could just hear her voice, reading me some words o' peace and promise, I could die away into the silence and rest o' God, just as a baby is hushed up to sleep by its mother's lullaby."

"Shall I read you a chapter, now?"

"Ay, do! M'appen I shan't listen to th' sense, at first; it will seem far away—but when yo' come to words I like—to th' comforting texts—it'll seem close in my ear, and going through me as it were."

Margaret began. Bessy tossed to and fro. If, by an effort, she attended for one moment, it seemed as though she were convulsed into double restlessness the next. At last, she burst out: "Don't go on reading. It's no use. I'm blaspheming all the time in my mind, wi' thinking angrily on what canna be helped.—Yo'd hear of th' riot, m'appen, yesterday at Marlborough Mills? Thornton's factory, yo' know."

"Your father was not there, was he?" said Margaret colouring deeply.

"Not he. He'd ha' given his right hand if it had never come to pass. It's that that's fretting me. He's fairly knocked down in his mind by it. It's no use telling him, fools will always break out o' bounds. Yo' never saw a man so downhearted as he is."

"But why?" asked Margaret. "I don't understand."

"Why, yo' see, he's a committee-man on this special strike. Th' Union appointed him because, though I say it as shouldn't say it, he's reckoned a deep chap, and true to th' back-bone. And he and t'other committee-men laid their plans. They were to hou'd together through thick and thin; what the major part thought, t'others were to think, whether they would or no. And above all there was to be no going again the law of the land. Folk would go with them if they saw them striving and starving wi' dumb patience; but if there was once any noise o' fighting and struggling—even wi' knobsticks—all was up, as they know by the experience of many, and many a time before. They would try and get speech o' th' knobsticks, and coax 'em, and reason wi' 'em, and m'appen warn 'em off; but whatever came, the Committee charged all members o' th' Union to lie down and die, if need were, without striking a blow; and then they reckoned they were sure o' carrying th' public with them. And besides all that, Committee knew they were right in their demand, and they didn't want to have right all mixed up wi' wrong, till folk can't separate it, no more nor I can the physic-powder from the jelly yo' gave me to mix it in; jelly is much the biggest, but powder tastes it all through. Well, I've told yo' at length about this'n, but I am tired out. Yo' just think for yor'sel, what it mun be for father to have a' his work undone, and by such a fool as Boucher, who must needs go right again the orders of Committee, and ruin th' strike, just as bad as if he meant to be a Judas. Eh! but father giv'd it him last night! He went so far as to say, he'd go and tell police where they might find th' ringleader o' th' riot; he'd give him up to th' mill-owners to do what they would wi' him. He'd show the world that th' real leaders o' the strike were not such as Boucher,

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Mrs. Thornton, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a city that refused to acknowledge the quiet suffocation of its women. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from her son, John, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest thoughts on industry and order.

Mrs. Thornton traced the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener. John was a man of undeniable erudition, yet his letters were suffocated by the very patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of wages, of strikes, of the "right things"—positioning himself as the benevolent instructor to her "undefeated resolution to admire only the right things."

"The right things," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned to his pages on the recent disturbances. John excused the unrest, arguing that men must be pardoned the "vices of young blood" so long as they were brave and generous. He painted a nostalgic picture of a "lusty old militant world," entirely blind to the fact that this merry England was built upon the bruised backs of women and the indentured silence of the poor. He dismissed the systemic inequalities that left women like Margaret Hale with no recourse but to forgive the betrayals of their husbands.

But today, the ink was not on paper; it was on her soul.

John had left the house that morning almost blinded by his baffled passion. He was as dizzy as if Margaret, instead of looking and speaking like a tender, graceful woman, had been a sturdy fish-wife giving him a sound blow with her fists. He had positive bodily pain—a violent headache, a throbbing intermittent pulse. He could not bear the noise, the garish light, the continued rumble and movement of the street. He called himself a fool for suffering so; and yet he could not, at the moment, recollect the cause of his suffering, and whether it was adequate to the consequences it had produced.

It would have been a relief to him, if he could have sat down and cried on a doorstep by a little child, who was raging and storming, through his passionate tears, at some injury he had received. He said to himself that he hated Margaret, but a wild, sharp sensation of love cleft his dull, thunderous feeling like lightning, even as he shaped the words expressive of hatred. His greatest comfort was in hugging his torment and in feeling, as he had indeed said to her, that though she might despise him, contemn him, treat him with her proud sovereign indifference, he did not change one whit. She could not make him change. He loved her, and would love her; and defy her, and this miserable bodily pain.

He had stood still for a moment to make this resolution firm and clear. There was an omnibus passing—going into the country; the conductor thought he was wishing for a place, and stopped near the pavement. It was too much trouble to apologize and explain; so he mounted upon it, and was borne away—past long rows of houses, then past detached villas with trim gardens, till they came to real country hedge-rows, and, by-and-by, to a small country town. Then everybody got down; and so did Mr. Thornton, and because they walked away he did so too.

He went into the fields, walking briskly, because the sharp motion relieved his mind. He could remember all about it now; the pitiful figure he must have cut; the absurd way in which he had gone and done the very thing he had so often agreed with himself in thinking would be the most foolish thing in the world; and had met with exactly the consequences which, in these wise moods, he had always foretold were certain to follow, if he ever did make such a fool of himself. Was he bewitched by those beautiful eyes, that soft, half-open, sighing mouth which lay so close upon his shoulder only yesterday? He could not even shake off the recollection that she had been there; that her arms had been round him, once—if never again. He only caught glimpses of her; he did not understand her altogether. At one time she was so brave, and at another so timid; now so tender, and then so haughty and regal proud. And then he thought over every time he had ever seen her once again, by way of finally forgetting her. He saw her in every dress, in every mood, and did not know which became her best. Even this morning how magnificent she had looked—her eyes flashing out upon him at the idea that, because she had shared his danger yesterday, she had cared for him the least!

If Mr. Thornton was a fool in the morning, as he assured himself at least twenty times he was, he did not grow much wiser in the afternoon. All that he gained for his sixpenny omnibus ride was a more vivid conviction that there never was, never could be, anyone like Margaret; that she did not love him and never would; but that she—no! nor the whole world—should never hinder him from loving her.

And so he returned to the little marketplace, and remounted the omnibus to return to Milton. It was late in the afternoon when he was set down, near his warehouse. The accustomed places brought back the accustomed habits and trains of thought. He knew how much he had to do—more than his usual work, owing to the commotion of the day before. He had to see his brother magistrates; he had to complete the arrangements, only half made in the morning, for the comfort and safety of his newly imported Irish hands; he had to secure them from all chance of communication with the discontented workpeople of Milton. Last of all, he had to go home and encounter his mother.

Mrs. Thornton had sat in the dining-room all day, every moment expecting the news of her son's acceptance by Miss Hale. She had braced herself up many and many a time, at some sudden noise in the house; had caught up the half-dropped work, and begun to ply her needle diligently, though through dimmed spectacles, and with an unsteady hand! and many times had the door opened, and some indifferent person entered on some insignificant errand. Then her rigid face unstiffened from its gray frost-bound expression, and the features dropped into the relaxed look of despondency, so unusual to their sternness.

She wrenched herself away from the contemplation of all the dreary changes that would be brought about to herself by her son's marriage; she forced her thoughts into the accustomed household grooves. The newly-married couple-to-be would need fresh household stocks of linen: and Mrs. Thornton had clothes-basket upon clothes-basket, full of table-cloths and napkins, brought in, and began to reckon up the store. There was some confusion between what was hers, and consequently marked G. H. T. (for George and Hannah Thornton), and what was her son's—bought with his money, marked with his initials. Some of those marked G. H. T. were Dutch damask of the old kind, exquisitely fine; none were like them now.

Mrs. Thornton stood looking at them long—they had been her pride when she was first married. Then she knit her brows, and pinched and compressed her lips tight, and carefully unpicked the G.H. She went so far as to search for the Turkey-red marking-thread to put in the new initials; but it was all used—and she had no heart to send for any more just yet. So she looked fixedly at vacancy; a series of visions passing before her, in all of which her son was the principal, the sole object—her son, her pride, her property.

Still he did not come. Doubtless he was with Miss Hale. The new love was displacing her already from her place as first in his heart. A terrible pain—a pang of vain jealousy—shot through her: she hardly knew whether it was more physical or mental; but it forced her to sit down. In a moment, she was up again as straight as ever—a grim smile upon her face for the first time that day, ready for the door opening, and the rejoicing triumphant one, who should never know the sore regret his mother felt at his marriage.

In all this, there was little thought enough of the future daughter-in-law as an individual. She was to be John's wife. To take Mrs. Thornton's place as mistress of the house, was only one of the rich consequences which decked out the supreme glory: all household plenty and comfort, all purple and fine linen, honour, love, obedience, troops of friends, would all come as naturally as jewels on a king's robe, and be as little thought of for their separate value. To be chosen by John, would separate a kitchen-wench from the rest of the world. And Miss Hale was not so bad. If she had been a Milton lass, Mrs. Thornton would have positively liked her. She was pungent, and had taste, and spirit, and flavour in her. True, she was sadly prejudiced, and very ignorant; but that was to be expected from her southern breeding. A strange sort of mortified comparison of Fanny with her, went on in Mrs. Thornton's mind; and for once she spoke harshly to her daughter; abused her

Mr. Thornton moved through the affairs of the day with a clarity that cut through the fog of his previous despair. He seized the slight demand for finished goods to drive hard bargains, sharpening his focus until the meeting of the magistrates. There, amidst older men whose wealth was sunk in land and whose influence was rooted in tradition, he stood as the arbiter of consequence. They looked to him for prompt wisdom; he was the one tasked with coordinating the police, leading the necessary steps against disorder. Yet, he cared for their unconscious deference no more than the south wind cares for the smoke rising from the great chimneys—it neither swerved nor lingered. He remained oblivious to the silent respect that surrounded him, viewing it only as potential friction against his singular objective. While the womenfolk whispered among themselves about how highly Mr. Thornton was held, how things would have faltered without his intervention, he swept aside these trivialities. The deep mortification of the prior day had burned away, clearing the mists from his intellect. He felt his power, reveling in it with a ferocity that bordered on defiance. If he had known it, he could have sung the Miller's song: *"I care for nobody—Nobody cares for me."*

Justice was swift in the borough court. The evidence against Boucher and his riotous followers was secured, though the charge of conspiracy against three others collapsed under scrutiny. Still, Mr. Thornton issued a stern charge to the constabulary: the law's right arm must remain poised, ready to strike the moment a new fault was proven. Stepping out of the hot, reeking courtroom, he emerged into the sultry street, feeling a sudden, overwhelming languor. His thoughts, unmoored, drifted inevitably to her—not the cold rejection of yesterday, but the fleeting warmth of the day before. He walked mechanically through the throng, a ghost in his own city, haunted by the memory of her heart beating against his chest.

"Why, Mr. Thornton! you're cutting me very coolly, I must say. And how is Mrs. Thornton? Brave weather this!" Dr. Donaldson hailed him, his voice bright against the gloom.

"I beg your pardon, Doctor. I didn't see you. My mother is well, thank you. It is a fine day, good for the harvest. If the wheat gets in well, we shall have a brisk trade next year, regardless of your medical prognostications."

"Ay, ay. Each man for himself. Your bad weather breeds sickness and prepares for death among you Milton men, unaware of the rot beneath the surface."

"Not with me, Doctor. I am made of iron. Even the news of my worst debt never varied my pulse. This strike, which weighs heavier on me than on anyone else in Milton—even more than on Hamper—does not touch my appetite. Go seek another patient."

Dr. Donaldson paused, his expression shifting. "By the way, you recommended a poor lady to me. Mrs. Hale, in Crampton House. I feared the worst, but today confirmed it. She has but a few weeks left."

Mr. Thornton fell silent. The vaunted steadiness of his composure cracked for an instant. "Can I do anything?" he asked, his voice altered.

"You know money is scarce. Are there comforts she requires?"

"No," the Doctor replied, shaking his head. "She craves fruit. A constant fever burns her, but jargonelle pears will suffice. There are plenty in the market."

"You will tell me if there is anything else," Mr. Thornton insisted. "I rely on you."

"Oh! Never fear! I shall not spare your purse; I know it is deep enough. Give me a *carte-blanche* for all my patients' wants."

But Mr. Thornton possessed no universal philanthropy, nor did he offer broad charity. Yet, driven by a specific, stubborn impulse, he marched straight to the first fruit shop in Milton. He selected a cluster of purple grapes with the most delicate bloom, the richest-hued peaches, and vines fresh with green leaves. Packed into a basket, the shopman waited for his destination.

"To Marlborough Mills, I suppose, sir?"

"No!" Mr. Thornton snapped. "Give the basket to me—I'll take it."

The weight filled both his hands, forcing him through the busiest thoroughfare, navigating the sea of feminine shoppers. Young ladies turned to stare, bewildered to see the formidable manufacturer carrying a load like a porter or an errand-boy. Inside his mind, he constructed a fortress of justification: *"I will not be daunted from doing as I choose by the thought of her. It is simply right to bring this fruit to the poor mother. She shall never scorn me for doing what pleases me. A pretty joke, indeed, if fear of a haughty girl prevents me from kindness to a man I like! I do it for Mr. Hale; I do it in defiance of her."*

At an unusual pace, he reached Crampton House. Ascending two steps at a time, he bypassed Dixon's announcement and entered the drawing-room. His face flushed, his eyes burning with a kindly earnestness, he found Mrs. Hale lying on the sofa, heated by fever. Mr. Hale read aloud nearby, while Margaret worked on a low stool beside her mother. Her heart fluttered, sensing the tension, though Mr. Thornton barely acknowledged either of them. With his basket in hand, he approached Mrs. Hale directly. In that subdued, gentle tone so striking when spoken by a robust man to a fragile invalid, he said, "I met Dr. Donaldson, ma'am. Since he said fruit would aid you, I took the liberty—the great liberty—of bringing you some that seemed fine."

Mrs. Hale trembled with eager delight. Mr. Hale, with fewer words, expressed a deeper gratitude. "Fetch a plate, Margaret—a basket—anything."

Margaret rose, paralyzed by the fear of movement, terrified of a conscious collision between her father and this stranger. She assumed that, having started on a low seat and now standing behind her father, he had overlooked her in his haste. Yet, she felt his consciousness of her presence radiating from him, even as his eyes never truly rested upon her.

"I must go," he declared abruptly. "I cannot stay. If you will forgive this liberty—and my rough ways, which seem too abrupt—I promise gentleness next time. You will allow me the pleasure of bringing fruit again if I find anything tempting. Good afternoon, Mr. Hale. Good-bye, ma'am."

And he was gone. Not a word, not a glance directed at Margaret. She believed he had not seen her. In silence, she fetched a plate, lifting the fruit tenderly with her delicate fingers. It was kind of him, especially after yesterday.

"Oh! It is so delicious!" Mrs. Hale whispered, her voice weak. "How kind of him to think of me! Margaret, love, taste these grapes! Was it not good of him?"

"Yes!" Margaret answered quietly.

"Margaret!" Mrs. Hale queried, her tone querulous. "You won't like anything Mr. Thornton does. I never saw anyone so prejudiced."

Mr. Hale peeled a peach, cutting a small slice for himself. "If I had any prejudices, the gift of such fruit would melt them away. I have not tasted such delicacies since I was a boy. Boys, I fancy, find all fruit good. Remember the matted currant bushes at the corner of the west wall at our home?"

Did she not? Did she not recall every weather-stain on the old stone wall, the gray and yellow lichens mapping its age, the tiny crane's-bill growing in the crevices? Shaken by the tumult of the last forty-eight hours, her entire existence a strain upon her fortitude, these casual words triggered a visceral reaction. The memory of sunny times shattered her composure. She dropped her sewing, stumbled to her feet, and fled hastily into her own chamber.
