He looked up, a sharp, bitter glint flashing in his eye before dissolving into a low, mocking laugh.

"Measter! if it's no offence, I'll ask yo' a question or two in my turn."

"You're quite welcome," Mr. Hale said, meeting the challenge.

"I reckon yo'n some way of earning your bread. Folk seldom lives i' Milton just for pleasure, if they can live anywhere else."

"You are quite right. I have some independent property, but my intention in settling in Milton was to become a private tutor."

"To teach folk. Well! I reckon they pay yo' for teaching them, dunnot they?"

"Yes," replied Mr. Hale, smiling faintly. "I teach in order to get paid."

"And them that pays yo', dun they tell yo' whatten to do, or whatten not to do wi' the money they gives you in just payment for your pains—in fair exchange like?"

"No; to be sure not!"

"They dunnot say, 'Yo' may have a brother, or a friend as dear as a brother, who wants this here brass for a purpose both yo' and he think right; but yo' mun promise not give it to him. Yo' may see a good use, as yo' think, to put yo'r money to; but we don't think it good, and so if yo' spend it a-thatens we'll just leave off dealing with yo'.' They dunnot say that, dun they?"

"No: to be sure not!"

"Would yo' stand it if they did?"

"It would be some very hard pressure that would make me even think of submitting to such dictation."

"There's not the pressure on all the broad earth that would make me," said Nicholas Higgins, his voice rising with a defiant energy. "Now yo've got it. Yo've hit the bull's eye. Hampers—that's where I worked—makes their men pledge 'emselves they'll not give a penny to help th' Union or keep turn-outs fro' clemming. They may pledge and make pledge," he continued, his contempt dripping like acid, "they nobbut make liars and hypocrites. And that's a less sin, to my mind, to make men's hearts so hard that they'll not do a kindness to them as needs it, or help on the right and just cause, though it goes again the strong hand. But I'll ne'er forswear mysel' for a' the work the king could gi'e me. I'm a member o' the Union; and I think it's the only thing to do the workmen any good. And I've been a turn-out, and known what it was to clem; so if I get a shilling, sixpence shall go to them if they axe it from me. Consequence is, I dunnot see where I'm to get a shilling."

"Is that rule about not contributing to the Union in force at all the mills?" Margaret asked, her heart tightening.

"I cannot say. It's a new regulation at ours; and I reckon they'll find that they cannot stick to it. But it's in force now. By-and-by they'll find out, tyrants makes liars."

A heavy silence settled over the room, broken only by the crackle of the dying fire. Margaret hesitated, the words forming in her mind like stones she feared to throw. She was unwilling to irritate a man already drowning in gloom. Finally, she spoke, her voice soft, her manner reluctant, hoping to avoid causing pain.

"Do you remember poor Boucher saying that the Union was a tyrant? I think he said it was the worse tyrant of all. And I remember, at the time I agreed with him."

For a long while, Higgins did not move. He rested his head on his two hands, staring down into the flames, shielding his face from her gaze.

"I'll not deny but what th' Union finds it necessary to force a man into his own good. I'll speak truth. A man leads a dree life who's not i' th' Union. But once i' th' Union, his interests are taken care on better nor he could do it for himsel', or by himsel', for that matter. It's the only way working men can get their rights, by all joining together. More the members, more chance for each one separate man having justice done him. Government takes care o' fools and madmen; and if any man is inclined to do himsel' or his neighbour a hurt, it puts a bit of a check on him, whether he likes it or no. That's all we do i' th' Union. We can't clap folk into prison; but we can make a man's life so heavy to be borne, that he's obliged to come in, and be wise and helpful in spite of himsel'. Boucher were a fool all along, and ne'er a worse fool than

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the Hale household windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt less like weather and more like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of industrial Manchester itself. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of beeswax and damp wool, a heavy perfume clinging to the silence of a home fractured by tragedy. Margaret sat at her small table, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post, but her mind was elsewhere, trapped in the suffocating memory of the Widow Boucher's house.

Yesterday's visit had been a failure, a testament to the crushing weight of urban despair. The widow, consumed by a selfish, animalistic grief, viewed her husband's suicide not as a tragedy shared, but as a personal grievance against a world that had wronged her. She spoke of her children not with maternal warmth, but as burdens, as incumbrances dragging her down. Margaret had watched, her heart bleeding silently, as the woman dragged her terrified offspring upstairs to stare upon the disfigured corpse of their father. It was a grotesque intermingling of horror and natural mourning, a violation of the sacred boundary between the living and the dead. While Margaret struggled to steer the children's thoughts toward practical duty—to clean the slatternly rooms, to imagine what their father would have wished—they remained simple, truthful mourners, their voices stammering with memories of a tenderness the mother refused to acknowledge.

The widow's mind, however, was a fortress of irrationality. She constructed an entire army of personal enemies: the masters, particularly Mr. Thornton whose mill had been attacked; the Union, represented by Higgins; the sheer number of hungry, noisy children. All of them, she insisted, were responsible for her destitution. When Margaret returned home, unable to cheer her father despite her own exhaustion, she found herself reflecting on the peculiar pathology of city life.

"It is the town life," she told Mr. Hale later, her voice steady. "Their nerves are quickened by the haste and bustle and speed of everything around them, to say nothing of the confinement in these pent-up houses, which of itself is enough to induce depression and worry of spirits. Now in the country, people live so much more out of doors, even children, and even in the winter."

"But people must live in towns," Mr. Hale countered gently. "And in the country some get such stagnant habits of mind that they are almost fatalists."

"Yes," Margaret acknowledged, her eyes drifting to the window where the grey sky pressed down on the rooftops. "Each mode of life produces its own trials. The dweller in towns must find it as difficult to be patient as the country-bred man finds it to be active. Both must find it hard to realize a future; the one because the present is so living and hurrying and close around him; the other because his life tempts him to revel in the mere sense of animal existence, not knowing of, and consequently not caring for, any pungency of pleasure attainable through planning and denial."

"And thus both the necessity for engrossment and the stupid content in the present produce the same effects. But this poor Mrs. Boucher! How little we can do for her."

"And yet we dare not leave her without our efforts, although they may seem so useless. Oh, Papa! It's a hard world to live in!"

"So it is, my child. We feel it so just now, at any rate; but we have been very happy, even in the midst of our sorrow. What a pleasure Frederick's visit was!"

"Yes, that it was," said Margaret, brightening momentarily. "It was such a charming, snatched, forbidden thing."

But the smile vanished as quickly as it appeared. She had already spoiled the remembrance of Frederick's visit to herself by her own cowardice. Of all faults, the one she most despised in others was the want of bravery; the meanness of heart which leads to untruth. And here she had been guilty of it! The thought of Mr. Thornton's knowledge of her falsehood struck her with fresh force. She wondered if she should have minded detection half so much from anyone else. She mentally rehearsed the confession with her Aunt Shaw, Edith, her father, Captain Lennox, and finally, Frederick. The thought of the last knowing what she had done, even in his own behalf, was the most painful, for the brother and sister were in the first flush of their mutual regard and love. But even any fall in Frederick's opinion was as nothing to the shame, the shrinking shame she felt at the thought of meeting Mr. Thornton again.

Yet, beneath the shame lay a desperate longing. She wanted to see him, to get it over, to understand where she stood in his opinion. Her cheeks burned as she recalled how proudly she had implied an objection to trade in the early days of their acquaintance, citing the deceit of passing off inferior goods or assuming credit for wealth not possessed. She remembered Mr. Thornton's look of calm disdain, as in few words he gave her to understand that in the great scheme of commerce, all dishonorable ways of acting were sure to prove injurious in the long run. She remembered using the word *chivalric*, and her father correcting her with the higher word, *Christian*; and so drawing the argument upon himself while she sat silent beside him with a slight feeling of contempt.

*No more contempt for her!* No more talk about the chivalric! Henceforward she must feel humiliated and disgraced in his sight. But when should she see him? Her heart leaped up in apprehension at every ring of the doorbell; and yet, when it fell down to calmness, she felt strangely saddened and sick at heart at each disappointment. It was very evident that her father expected to see him, and was surprised that he did not come. The truth was, there were points in their conversation the previous night on which they had no time then to enlarge; but it had been understood that if possible on the succeeding evening—or at least the very first evening Mr. Thornton could command—they should meet for further discussion. Mr. Hale had looked forward to this meeting ever since they had parted. He had not yet resumed the instruction to his pupils, which he had relinquished at the commencement of his wife's more serious illness, so he had fewer occupations than usual; and the great interest of the last day or so (Boucher's suicide) had driven him back with more eagerness than ever upon his speculations. He was restless all the evening. He kept saying, "I quite expected to have seen Mr. Thornton. I think the messenger who brought the book last night must have had some note, and forgot to deliver it. Do you think there has been any message left today?"

"I will go and inquire, Papa," said Margaret, after the changes on these sentences had been rung once or twice. "Stay, there's a ring!"

She sat down instantly, bending her head attentively over her work. She heard a step on the stairs, but it was only one, and she knew it was Dixon's. She lifted up her head and sighed, believing she felt glad.

"It's that Higgins, sir. He wants to see you, or else Miss Hale. Or it might be Miss Hale first, and then you, sir; for he's in a strange kind of way."

"He had better come up here, Dixon; and then he can see us both, and choose which he likes for his listener."

"Oh! Very well, sir. I've no wish to hear what he's got to say, I'm sure; only, if you could see his shoes, I'm sure you'd say the kitchen was the fitter place."

"He can wipe them, I suppose," said Mr. Hale. So Dixon flung off to bid him walk upstairs. She was a little mollified, however, when he looked at his feet with a hesitating air; and then, sitting down on the bottom stair, he took off the offending shoes, and without a word walked upstairs.

"Servant, sir!" said he, slicking his hair down when he came into the room. "If hoo'll excuse me (looking at Margaret) for being in my stockings; I've been tramping all day, and streets is none o' the cleanest."

Margaret thought that fatigue might account for the change in his manner, for he was unusually quiet and subdued; and he had evidently some difficulty in saying what he came to say. Mr. Hale's ever-ready sympathy with anything of shyness or hesitation, or want of self-possession, made him come to his aid.

"We shall have tea up directly, and then you'll take a cup with us, Mr. Higgins. I am sure you are tired, if you've been out much this wet, relaxing day. Margaret, my dear, can't you hasten tea?"

Margaret could only hasten tea by taking the preparation of it into her own hands, and so offending Dixon, who was emerging out of her sorrow for her late mistress into a very touchy, irritable state. But Martha, like all who came in contact with Margaret—even Dixon herself, in the long run—felt it a pleasure and an honor to forward any of her wishes; and her readiness, and Margaret's sweet forbearance, soon made Dixon ashamed of herself.

"Why, Master, and you must always be asking the lower classes up-stairs, since we came to Milton, I cannot understand. Folk at Helstone were never brought higher than the kitchen; and I've let one or two of them know before now that they might think it an honor to be even there."

Higgins found it easier to unburden himself to one than to two

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a city that cared nothing for the quiet suffocation of its women. Yet, inside the small, dimly lit room where she sat, the atmosphere was thick with a different kind of pressure—one born not of weather, but of the crushing weight of a single, stolen glance.

She traced the edge of the letter she held, her fingers trembling slightly. It was from Mrs. Thornton, a woman whose sharp Damascus blade she had once admired, now turned against her own heart. The ink on the page seemed to shift, accusing her of crimes she had committed only in the shadow of a dusky twilight.

"It was not merely that I spoke falsely," Margaret whispered to the empty room, echoing the thoughts that plagued her. "But that this falsehood... it pointed somewhere else."

In her mind's eye, the image burned with terrifying clarity: Mr. Thornton's face, twisted in a rage she had never witnessed before. He had not simply been angry at her deception; he had been consumed by a jealous fury that suggested she belonged to someone else. A dark, slight, elegant man. Someone who commanded her soul in a way she had never expected to feel. She remembered the look that had passed between them at the station, the attitude of familiar confidence, the implicit endearment that had made her feel safe, cherished, and utterly vulnerable.

Thornton's memory of that moment had been a physical torment to him, she realized now. He had ground his teeth, imagining the cruelty of sharing her with a mob, yet finding himself excluded from the intimacy she shared with another. He had mocked her protection of him during the riot, contrasting it with the soft, bewitching way she moved when she truly loved. He had believed her capable of lying to him, of concealing a secret attachment that violated her truthful nature.

"He thinks I am attached to him," she murmured, the realization hitting her with the force of a physical blow. "He thinks I chose him over *him*."

The irony was bitter. She had lied to protect him, to shield him from the harsh realities of the strike, to prevent bloodshed. But in doing so, she had revealed a capacity for deceit that he interpreted as evidence of a deeper betrayal. She had shown him that she could lie for a cause, and he assumed she could lie for a lover.

A knock at the door shattered her reverie. It was Mrs. Thornton.

Margaret smoothed her dress, forcing her breathing to steady. She rose, her movements graceful despite the turmoil within. As the older woman entered, Margaret's demeanor shifted instantly into a mask of gentle deference. She praised Martha, the servant, and spoke softly of Edith, creating a buffer of polite conversation designed to disarm the storm approaching her.

Mrs. Thornton stood there, her expression unreadable, a mixture of maternal concern and cold judgment. She had prepared herself all night, steeling her resolve to deliver a rebuke that would strip Margaret bare. She wanted to show herself untouched by the glamour that had once captivated her son, to prove that she could see clearly through the beauty of her victim's jet-black hair and lucid eyes.

"I have a duty to perform," Mrs. Thornton began, her voice clipped. "Your poor mother entrusted me with a promise. I would not allow you to act wrongly, or inadvertently, without remonstrating."

Margaret felt the color rise in her cheeks, hot and shameful. She braced herself for the accusation regarding the lie she had told to Mr. Thornton. She expected him to confront her directly, to demand her penitence and restore her to his good opinion. Instead, she found herself facing a stranger who wielded the authority of a moral judge.

"At first, I could hardly believe it," Mrs. Thornton continued, her tone sharpening. "That you walked about with a gentleman at the Outwood station, so far from home, at such a late hour. My son confirmed the story. It was indiscreet, Miss Hale. Many a young woman has lost her character before now."

The words struck Margaret like a physical blow. Her eyes flashed with sudden fire. This was not about the lie she had told; this was an assault on her integrity, her very essence. To suggest she had compromised her character, to imply she was a woman who could be led astray by a young man in the dusk—it was an insult she could not tolerate.

"For my mother's sake," Margaret said, her voice trembling but gaining strength, "I will bear much. But I cannot bear everything. She never meant me to be exposed to insult, I am sure."

"Insult?" Mrs. Thornton scoffed, stepping closer. "What do you know of me that should lead you to suspect—"

"Oh!" Margaret broke down, covering her face with her hands, the dam finally cracking. "I know now, Mr. Thornton has told you..."

She stopped, unable to finish the sentence. The implication hung heavy in the air: that Thornton had confessed his suspicions, that he had revealed the existence of the other man, the secret attachment that had driven her to such desperate measures. She had lied to protect him, yes, but in doing so, she had inadvertently handed him the weapon he needed to destroy her reputation.

Mrs. Thornton saw the battle spirit ignite in Margaret's eyes, the same defiance that had once drawn Thornton's admiration. The older woman's own combative nature stirred in response. She pressed on, unwilling to back down from the confrontation she had sought.

"For your mother's sake, I have thought it right to warn you against such improprieties," Mrs. Thornton declared, her voice rising. "They must degrade you in the long run in the estimation of the world, even if in fact they do not lead you to positive harm."

Margaret lowered her hands, her face pale but resolute. "For my mother's sake," she repeated, her voice steady now, "I will bear much. But I cannot bear everything. She never meant me to be exposed to insult, I am sure."

The silence that followed was absolute, broken only by the distant sound of rain continuing its relentless rhythm against the glass. In that moment, the gap between them widened, filled with the unspoken truths of love, jealousy, and the devastating cost of a single, misunderstood glance.

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a city that cared nothing for the quiet suffocation of its women. She sat at her small, slightly-built desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the empty space where Mrs. Thornton had stood moments before. Every step she took in this house echoed, a physical manifestation of her agitation, but she forced herself to remain still until she heard the sound of the latch lifting and the door closing securely behind her mistress. Only then did she rise, compelling her memory to replay the conversation, speech by speech, dissecting the words until they lost their sting.

"At any rate, her words do not touch me," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. "They fall off from me; for I am innocent of all the motives she attributes to me. But still, it is hard to think that any one—any woman—can believe all this of another so easily. It is hard and sad. Where I have done wrong, she does not accuse me—she does not know. He never told her: I might have known he would not!"

She lifted her head, a flicker of pride igniting within her at the delicacy of feeling Mr. Thornton had displayed, even in his rejection. Then, a new thought, sharp and invasive, cut through her resolve. She pressed her hands tightly together, her knuckles white.

"He, too, must take poor Frederick for some lover." The word passed through her mind, bringing a flush to her cheeks that burned hotter than the shame. "I see it now. It is not merely that he knows of my falsehood, but he believes that some one else cares for me; and that I... Oh dear!—oh dear! What shall I do? What do I mean? Why do I care what he thinks, beyond the mere loss of his good opinion as regards my telling the truth or not? I cannot tell. But I am very miserable! Oh, how unhappy this last year has been! I have passed out of childhood into old age. I have had no youth—no womanhood; the hopes of womanhood have closed for me—for I shall never marry; and I anticipate cares and sorrows just as if I were an old woman, and with the same tearful spirit. I am weary of this continual call upon me for strength. I could bear up for papa; because that is a natural, pious duty. And I think I could bear up against—at any rate, I could have the energy to resent, Mrs. Thornton's unjust, impertinent suspicions. But it is hard to feel how completely he must misunderstand me. What has happened to make me so morbid to-day? I do not know. I only know I cannot help it. I must give way sometimes. No, I will not though," she said, springing to her feet with a sudden surge of defiance. "I will not—I *will* not think of myself and my own position. I won't examine into my own feelings. It would be of no use now. Some time, if I live to be an old woman, I may sit over the fire, and looking into the embers, see the life that might have been."

All this time she was hastily putting on her things to go out, only stopping from time to time to wipe her eyes, with an impatient gesture at the tears that would come again, in spite of all her bravery.

"I dare say there's many a woman makes as sad a mistake as I have done, and only finds it out too late. And how proudly and impertinently I spoke to him that day! But I did not know then. It has come upon me little by little, and I don't know where it began. Now I won't give way. I shall find it difficult to behave in the same way to him, with this miserable consciousness upon me; but I will be very calm and very quiet, and say very little. But, to be sure, I may not see him; he keeps out of our way evidently. That would be worse than all. And yet no wonder that he avoids me, believing what he must about me."

She stepped out into the grey afternoon, going rapidly towards the country, trying to drown the rising tide of reflection in the swiftness of her motion.

As she stood on the doorstep at her return, her father came up, his voice warm with relief.

"Good girl!" said he. "You've been to Mrs. Boucher's. I was just meaning to go there, if I had time, before dinner."

"No, papa; I have not," said Margaret, reddening. "I never thought about her. But I will go directly after dinner; I will go while you are taking your nap."

Accordingly Margaret went. Mrs. Boucher was very ill; really ill—not merely ailing. The kind and sensible neighbor who had come the previous day seemed to have taken charge of everything, her presence a sturdy anchor in the chaos. Some of the children were gone to the neighbors; Mary Higgins had fetched the three youngest at dinner-time; and since then Nicholas had gone for the doctor. He had not returned yet; Mrs. Boucher was dying, and there was nothing to do but wait. Margaret thought that she should like to hear his opinion, and that she could not do better than go and see the Higginses in the meantime. She might then possibly hear whether Nicholas had been able to make his application to Mr. Thornton.

She found Nicholas busily engaged in making a penny spin on the dresser, for the amusement of three little children who clung to him in a fearless manner. He, as well as they, was smiling at a good long spin; and Margaret thought that the happy look of interest in his occupation was a good sign. When the penny finally stopped spinning, "little Johnnie" began to cry.

"Come to me," said Margaret, taking him off the dresser and holding him in her arms; she held her watch to his ear, while she asked Nicholas if he had seen Mr. Thornton.

The look on his face changed instantly, shifting from playful engagement to a grim, guarded expression.

"Ay!" said he. "I've seen and heerd too much on him."

"He refused you, then?" said Margaret, sorrowfully.

"To be sure. I knew he'd do it all along. It's no good expecting marcy at the hands o' them measters. Yo'r a stranger and a foreigner, and aren't likely to know their ways; but I knowed it."

"I am sorry I asked you. Was he angry? He did not speak to you as Hamper did, did he?"

"He weren't o'er-civil!" said Nicholas, spinning the penny again, as much for his own amusement as for that of the children. "Never yo' fret, I'm only where I was. I'll go on tramp to-morrow. I gave him as good as I got. I telled him, I'd not that good opinion on him that I'd ha' come a second time of mysel'; but yo'd advised me for to come, and I were beholden to yo'."

"You told him I sent you?"

"I dunno, if I ca'd yo' by your name. I dunnot think I did. I said a woman who knew no better had advised me for to come and see if there was a soft place in his heart."

"And he—?" asked Margaret.

"Said I were to tell yo' to mind yo'r own business.—That's the longest spin yet, my lads.—And them's civil words to what he used to me. But ne'er mind. We're but where we was; and I'll break stones on the road afore I let these little uns clem."

Margaret put the struggling Johnnie out of her arms, back into his former place on the dresser.

"I am sorry I asked you to go to Mr. Thornton's. I am disappointed in him."

There was a slight noise behind her. Both she and Nicholas turned round at the same moment, and there stood Mr. Thornton, with a look of displeased surprise upon his face. Obeying her swift impulse, Margaret passed out before him, saying not a word, only bowing low to hide the sudden paleness that she felt had come over her face. He bent equally low in return, and then closed the door after her. As she hurried to Mrs. Boucher's, she heard the clang, and it seemed to fill up the measure of her mortification. He too was annoyed to find her there.

Inside the Higgins household, the atmosphere was thick with tension. Mr. Thornton had tenderness in his heart—"a soft place," as Nicholas Higgins called it; but he had some pride in concealing it; he kept it very sacred and safe, and was jealous of every circumstance that tried to gain admission. But if he dreaded exposure of his tenderness, he was equally desirous that all men should recognize his justice; and he felt that he had been unjust in giving so scornful a hearing to anyone who had waited, with humble patience, for five hours, to speak to him. That the man had spoken saucily to him when he had the opportunity was nothing to Mr. Thornton. He rather liked him for it; and he was conscious of his own irritability of temper at the time, which probably made them both quits. It was the five hours of waiting that struck Mr. Thornton. He had not five hours to spare, himself; but one hour—two hours, of his hard penetrating intellectual, as well as

The candlelight flickered against the dust motes dancing in the air of the Hale parlor, casting long, wavering shadows that seemed to stretch toward the door where Mr. Thornton stood waiting. The atmosphere was thick with the unspoken tension of a storm held at bay, a pressure that made the very breath feel heavier. Margaret had not anticipated the joy of her godfather's arrival, viewing it merely as a courtesy to her father, yet the moment Mr. Bell stepped inside, the rigid formalities of the household dissolved. He claimed her with a familiarity that bordered on possessive, declaring her innate ability to command his affection an hereditary trait, a power she wielded effortlessly. In return, she praised his youthful vigor beneath his academic robes, a compliment he accepted with a grin that belied the weariness etched into his features.

"You are fresh and young in warmth and kindness," Mr. Bell declared, though his tone shifted instantly to a mock severity. "Though I must confess, your opinions are the oldest and mustiest I have encountered in this long journey."

"Hear this daughter of yours, Hale!" Mr. Bell continued, his voice rising in theatrical outrage. "Her residence in Milton has utterly corrupted her soul. She is a democrat, a red republican, a member of the Peace Society, a socialist—"

"Papa," Margaret interjected, her voice steady despite the turmoil brewing within her, "it is simply because I advocate for the progress of commerce. Mr. Bell would have preferred the trade remain stagnant, bartering wild-beast skins for acorns."

"No, no," Mr. Hale countered, shaking his head with a weary sigh. "I would till the earth and grow potatoes. I would shear the beast and weave broadcloth. Do not exaggerate, missy. But I am exhausted by this ceaseless bustle. Everyone rushing over one another, driven by a frantic hunger to accumulate wealth."

"It is not everyone who can recline comfortably in college rooms and watch their fortunes swell without lifting a finger," Mr. Hale observed sharply. "Surely, many here would envy your property increasing without their own toil."

"I do not believe they would," Mr. Bell retorted, his eyes gleaming with a cynical amusement. "It is the struggle, the very chaos of competition they crave. To sit still, to learn from the dead past, or to shape the future through faithful, prophetic labor? Why, pooh! I know not a single man in Milton capable of such stillness; it is a high art indeed."

"Miltonians," Mr. Hale mused, "likely believe Oxford scholars lack the capacity for motion. It would be a boon if the two worlds mingled more freely."

"It might benefit the Miltoners," Mr. Bell conceded, "though many things beneficial to them would be profoundly disagreeable to others."

"Are you not a Milton man yourself?" Margaret asked, tilting her head. "One would assume pride in one's hometown is a given."

"I confess, I see little cause for pride," Mr. Bell admitted, his expression darkening. "If you will come to Oxford, Margaret, I will show you a place worthy of glory."

"Well!" Mr. Hale interrupted, sensing the shift in the current. "Mr. Thornton arrives tonight for tea, and he is as proud of Milton as you are of Oxford. You two must endeavor to broaden each other's minds."

"I have no desire to become more liberal-minded, thank you," Mr. Bell replied dryly.

"Is Mr. Thornton coming to tea, Papa?" Margaret whispered, her voice barely audible.

"To tea, or shortly thereafter. He was uncertain. He instructed us not to wait."

In the adjacent study, Mr. Thornton wrestled with a conflict that tore at his composure. He had resolved not to ask his mother about her attempts to counsel Margaret regarding her conduct, fearing that her filtered account would only deepen his anguish. He knew she would color the narrative, twisting his actions through the lens of her own anxieties. He recoiled from the mere mention of Margaret's name, torn between blame and a jealousy that burned hotter than reason allowed. While he condemned her, while he cherished her in spite of himself, he dreamed of her approaching with outstretched arms, a vision of lightness and gaiety that simultaneously repelled and drew him in. This phantom image, stripped of her actual character as if possessed by a malevolent spirit, haunted his waking hours, blurring the line between the virtuous Una and the treacherous Duessa. His disdain for the latter inevitably marred his perception of the former. Yet, pride forbade him from acknowledging his vulnerability by fleeing her presence. He neither sought nor avoided her, lingering unnecessarily over trivial business matters to prove his self-mastery, forcing his movements into unnatural slowness until the clock struck eight.

Upon arriving at the Hales', he found Mr. Bell still seated by the fire, engaging in tedious discourse long after business concluded. Mr. Thornton remained silent, chafing under the weight of the conversation, while Mr. Bell secretly resented Thornton's brusqueness, judging him lacking in both intellect and manners. Finally, a sound from the floor above prompted them to ascend. They discovered Margaret engrossed in a letter, animatedly discussing its contents with her father. Upon the gentlemen's entry, she hastily concealed the paper, but Mr. Thornton's keen senses caught fragments of Mr. Hale's words directed at Mr. Bell.

"A letter from Henry Lennox. It fills Margaret with hope."

Mr. Bell nodded. When Mr. Thornton's gaze swept over Margaret, she flushed crimson. He felt an overwhelming urge to rise and depart, never to return to the house.

"We were reflecting," Mr. Hale said smoothly, bridging the gap, "that you and Mr. Thornton have followed Margaret's advice, attempting to convert one another during your prolonged stay in the study."

"And you believed we had reduced each other to mere opinions, akin to the Kilkenny cats' tails. Whose opinion, pray, do you think possesses the most obstinate vitality?"

Mr. Thornton feigned ignorance, dismissing the query with disdain. Mr. Hale, polite as ever, clarified.

"Mr. Thornton, we accused Mr. Bell this morning of an Oxonian medieval bigotry toward his native town. We—Margaret, I believe—suggested it would benefit him to associate more closely with Milton's manufacturers."

"I beg your pardon," Mr. Bell corrected, his tone sharpening. "Margaret thought it would benefit the Milton manufacturers to associate more with Oxford men. Was that not so, Margaret?"

"I believe I thought mutual acquaintance would benefit both sides; I did not realize it was my specific idea, any more than my father's."

"So you see, Mr. Thornton," Mr. Bell pressed, "we should have been improving each other downstairs rather than debating vanished families of Smiths and Harrisons. Nevertheless, I am willing to contribute my share now. I wonder when you Miltonians intend to truly live. Your entire existence seems devoted to gathering the raw materials for life."

"By 'living,' do you mean enjoyment?"

"Yes, enjoyment—I do not specify the type, for I trust we both recognize mere pleasure as a poor substitute for true fulfillment."

"I would prefer the nature of this enjoyment be defined."

"Well! Enjoyment or leisure—the enjoyment derived from the power and influence money affords. You all strive for money. What is its purpose?"

Mr. Thornton fell silent. Then, softly, "I truly do not know. But money is not what *I* strive for."

"What then?"

"It is a domestic matter. I shall have to submit to such a catechism, and I am unsure I am prepared."

"No!" Mr. Hale interjected quickly. "Let us not make this personal. Neither of you represents your respective groups; you are too individual for that."

"I am uncertain whether to accept that as a compliment. I aspire to represent Oxford, with its beauty, learning, and proud history. What say you, Margaret? Should I be flattered?"

"I know little of Oxford. But there is a distinction between representing a city and representing its inhabitants."

"True enough, Miss Margaret. Now I recall, you opposed me this morning, adopting a distinctly Miltonian and manufacturing stance."

Margaret sensed the startled glance Mr. Thornton cast her and bristled at the potential misinterpretation of Mr. Bell's words. Mr. Bell continued, oblivious to the underlying tension.

"Oh! I wish I could show you our High Street, our Radcliffe Square. I omit our colleges, just as I grant Mr. Thornton license to exclude his factories when describing Milton's charms. I have a right to critique my birthplace. Remember, I am a Milton man."

Mr. Thornton felt a surge of irritation disproportionate to the provocation. He was not in a mood for jest. Had the circumstances been different, he might have relished Mr. Bell's half-teasing condemnation of a town whose life clashed so violently with his habits. But now, he was goaded into defending a position never intended to be seriously challenged.

"I do not set up Milton as a model town."

"Not in architecture?" Mr. Bell asked slyly.

"No! We have been too preoccupied to attend to mere outward appearances."

"Do not say *mere* outward appearances," Mr. Hale urged gently. "They imprint themselves upon us from childhood onward, every day of our lives."

"Wait a moment," Mr. Thornton said, his voice tightening. "Remember we belong to a different stock than the Greeks, to whom beauty was paramount. Mr. Bell might speak of a life of leisure and serene enjoyment, much of which entered through their outward senses. I do not

The winter wore on, dragging its weary tail through the calendar, yet the days, though lengthening, refused to ignite with the customary brilliance of hope that usually greets a February sun. Mrs. Thornton had vanished from the household entirely, a ghost of her former presence. Mr. Thornton visited, but his footsteps were restricted to the study, his conversations directed solely toward her father. Mr. Hale observed him with a steady, unflinching gaze, noting that the scarcity of their meetings somehow intensified the weight of each encounter. From the fragments Margaret could glean, there was no trace of wounded pride or lingering resentment in Mr. Thornton's demeanor; rather, his business affairs, tangled and complex since the strike, demanded a vigilance he had neglected the previous year. Even more striking was the fact that he spoke of her—not avoiding the name, nor seeking it out, but mentioning her with a calm, consistent friendliness that cut through the frost of their estrangement.

Yet, Margaret possessed no reservoir of spirits to lift her father's burdened mind. The dreary tranquility of the present moment was shadowed by a prolonged era of anxiety and storm, a period so exhaustive that her mind had lost its elastic capacity to rebound. She threw herself into teaching the two younger Boucher children, working tirelessly at the mechanics of goodness. Hard, indeed, she labored, for her heart remained a stone, unmoved by the efficacy of her efforts. Though she performed her duties punctually and painfully, she remained as distant from cheerfulness as ever; her life continued to be a landscape of bleakness. Her only genuine triumph was an act born of unconscious piety: the silent, steadfast comforting of her father. Every mood of his found a ready sympathizer in her; every wish, however quiet and hesitant, was met with her meek spirit of obedience, striving to forecast and fulfill it.

Then came March, bearing the news of Frederick's marriage. Letters flowed between him and Dolores; hers were a hybrid of Spanish and English, natural in their mixture, while his were peppered with linguistic inversions and turns that testified to the rapid infection of his native tongue by his bride's culture. Upon receiving Henry Lennox's letter—a grim missive declaring the slim hope of clearing himself at a court-martial due to missing witnesses—Frederick had written to Margaret a vehement renunciation of England. He wished to unnative himself, declaring he would reject a pardon if offered and refuse to live in the country if permitted. At first, these words tore at Margaret, seeming so unnatural to her sensibilities that she wept bitterly. But upon reflection, she recognized the raw poignancy of a hope crushed so violently; she understood that patience was the only currency available. Soon, Frederick's subsequent letters spoke of a future so radiant that the past held no sway, and Margaret discovered within herself the patience she had been cultivating for him.

Simultaneously, the pretty, timid letters from Dolores began to captivate both Margaret and her father. The young Spaniard was evidently desperate to impress her lover's English relations, her feminine care peeking through every erasure and correction. Accompanying the announcement of the union was a magnificent black lace mantilla, selected personally by Dolores for her unseen sister-in-law, whom Frederick had described as a paragon of beauty, wisdom, and virtue. This marriage elevated Frederick's worldly station to heights they could scarcely have imagined. Barbour and Co., one of Spain's most extensive trading houses, welcomed him as a junior partner. Margaret smiled faintly, then sighed, remembering her old tirades against commerce. Here was her *preux chevalier*, her knightly brother, transformed into a merchant, a trader! Yet, she immediately recoiled from herself, protesting the confusing equation of a Spanish merchant with a Milton mill-owner. Regardless, trade or no trade, Frederick was undeniably, overwhelmingly happy. Dolores must be charming, and the mantilla was exquisite. With a final sigh, she turned her gaze back to the stark reality of her own life.

That spring, her father suffered occasional difficulties in breathing, distressing him acutely. Margaret, though less alarmed as the symptoms subsided in intervals, grew increasingly urgent that he accept Mr. Bell's invitation to visit him at Oxford in April. The invitation explicitly included Margaret, with a special letter commanding her attendance. However, she felt a profound relief in remaining quietly at home, entirely free from responsibility, allowing her mind and heart to rest in a manner denied her for over two years.

When her father finally drove off toward the railroad, Margaret felt the immense, crushing pressure of her time and spirits vanish. It was almost staggering to realize she was so utterly at liberty: no one depended on her for cheering care or positive happiness; there was no invalid to plan for or think for. She could be idle, silent, forgetful—and, most liberating of all, she could be unhappy if she chose. For months, her personal cares and troubles had been stuffed into a dark cupboard, but now she had the leisure to retrieve them, to mourn their nature, and to seek the true method of subduing them into the elements of peace. These weeks, she had been vaguely aware of their existence, hidden behind a veil of dullness. Now, she would confront them, assigning each its rightful work in her life. Sitting almost motionless in the drawing-room for hours, she reviewed the bitterness of every memory with an unwincing resolution. Once, at the stinging thought of the faithlessness that spawned that abasing falsehood, she cried aloud.

She refused to acknowledge the force of the temptation anymore; her plans for Frederick had failed, and the temptation lay there as a dead mockery, a lie so despicably foolish in the light of ensuing events that faith in truth revealed itself as infinitely wiser. In her nervous agitation, she unconsciously opened a book belonging to her father lying on the table. The words that caught her eye seemed forged specifically for her state of acute self-abasement: *"Je ne voudrois pas reprendre mon cœur en ceste sorte... mais je voudrois le corriger par voye de compassion. Or sus, mon pauvre cœur, nous voilà tombe dans la fosse... Revelons-nous, et quittons-la pour jamais, reclamons la misericorde de Dieu..."*

"The way of humility," Margaret thought, the French phrase settling in her chest like a warm stone. "Ah, that is what I have missed! But courage, little heart. We will turn back, and by God's help, we may find the lost path."

Rising abruptly, she determined to engage in work that would draw her out of herself. Passing the drawing-room door on her way upstairs, she summoned Martha, attempting to pierce the grave, respectful, servant-like manner that had crusted over the girl's individuality with a mechanical obedience. It was difficult to coax Martha into speaking of personal interests, but eventually, Margaret struck the right chord by mentioning Mrs. Thornton. Martha's face instantly brightened, and under slight encouragement, a long story spilled forth. She recounted how her father, in his early life, had been connected to Mrs. Thornton's husband, even positioned to show him kindness—a detail Martha barely recalled, occurring when she was a small child. Circumstances had separated the families until Martha was nearly grown, her father sinking lower from his original role as a warehouse clerk, and her mother dying. Without Mrs. Thornton, Martha and her sister would have been "lost"; it was she who sought them out, provided for them, and cared for them.

"I had the fever, and was but delicate," Martha whispered, her voice softening. "Mrs. Thornton and Mr. Thornton never rested till they had nursed me up in their own house and sent me to the sea. The doctor said the fever was catching, but they cared for nothing else—except Miss Fanny, who went visiting the folk she intended to marry into. So, though she was afraid at the time, it has all ended well."

"Miss Fanny going to be married!" Margaret exclaimed, surprised.

"Yes; and to a rich gentleman, too, only he's a deal older than she is. His name is Watson; and his mills are somewhere out beyond Hayleigh. It's a very good marriage, for all he's got such gray hair."

At this revelation, Margaret fell silent long enough for Martha to regain her propriety and her habitual brevity. She swept up the hearth, asked when to prepare tea, and departed with the same wooden face she had entered with. Margaret had to forcibly suppress a bad habit she had recently cultivated: imagining how every event concerning Mr. Thornton affected him, wondering if he would like or dislike it.

The following day, she taught the little Boucher children, took a long walk, and concluded with a visit to Mary Higgins. To her surprise, she found Nicholas already home; the lengthening daylight had deceived her regarding the lateness of the evening. He, too, seemed to have entered further onto the path of humility; he was quieter, less self-asserting.

"So th' oud gentleman's away on his travels, is he?" Nicholas asked. "Little 'uns told me so. Eh! but the're sharp 'uns they are; I a'most think they beat my own wenches for sharpness, though m'appen it's wrong to say so, and one on 'em in her grave. There's summut in th' weather, I reckon, as sets folk a-wandering. My measter, him at th' shop yonder, is spinning about th' world somewhere."

"Is that the reason you're so soon at home to-night?" Margaret asked innocently.

"Thou know'st nought about it, that's all," he replied, with a touch of contempt. "I

The silence that descended upon the room was not merely an absence of sound; it was a physical weight, a suffocating fog that pressed against the ribs until breathing became a labored act of defiance. Margaret lay upon the sofa, a statue carved from marble and despair, her eyes sealed shut against a world that had suddenly lost its color. The shock of her beloved voice failing her, replaced by a silence that ached like a fever, had stripped her of all capacity for speech or movement. She was prostrate, not in sleep, but in a paralysis of the soul, waiting for a music that would never undo the crushing quiet.

Mr. Bell sat by the fire, his stout limbs stiff with the rigors of travel, trapped in a limbo between duty and helplessness. He dared not summon the carriage to carry her back to Oxford; the journey would demand a strength she possessed in no measure. Instead, he watched her, a sentinel guarding a fallen queen, while the dinner prepared by Dixon below grew cold. The savory aroma of deviled chicken drifted up, but to Mr. Bell's senses, it tasted of sawdust and ash. With a tender, futile gentleness, he minced the meat, seasoning it with care, only to watch Dixon attempt to coax a bite from Margaret. The languid shake of her head was a final decree: in this state, food was poison, not nourishment.

"I cannot leave her," Mr. Bell muttered, rising with a groan that echoed the room's heaviness. "I must write to Oxford. The preparations can wait. Can Mrs. Lennox not come?"

Dixon wiped her eyes, her own grief spilling over for two souls, and delivered the blow: Mrs. Lennox was confined, too near the precipice of childbirth to undertake a journey.

"Well!" Mr. Bell coughed, clearing his throat as if to swallow the disappointment. "We must have Mrs. Shaw. She has returned to England, surely?"

"Yes, sir," Dixon replied, her voice tinged with a defensive protectiveness. "But I doubt she wishes to abandon Mrs. Lennox during such a... significant time."

"Significant time," Mr. Bell repeated, the phrase tasting bitter on his tongue. He cleared his throat again, unable to finish the thought. "She could be content to linger in Venice or Naples, or some of those Papish havens where her own 'significant times' unfolded in Corfu. But what does a prosperous woman's convenience mean compared to this? Look at her! Lying there as still as an altar-stone, a helpless, homeless, friendless creature named Margaret. Tell Mrs. Shaw to come. Secure a room for her by tomorrow night. I will ensure she arrives."

And so, the letter was written. Mrs. Shaw received it with tears streaming down her cheeks, declaring it so reminiscent of the General's pre-gout rages that she would treasure it forever. Had Mr. Bell merely requested her aid, pleading with a tone that suggested refusal was an option, her natural *vis inertiae* might have held her fast. True sympathy alone would not have moved her; it required the sharp, uncourteous command of authority to break her resistance. Once the order was absolute, she surrendered instantly, allowing her maid to pack her trunks with frantic efficiency.

As Captain Lennox escorted his mother to the carriage, Edith burst forth from the house, a whirlwind of caps, shawls, and tears. "Do not forget, Mamma! Margaret must come and live with us. Sholto departs for Oxford on Wednesday; you must instruct Mr. Bell regarding our arrival. And if you require Sholto, he can proceed directly from Oxford to Milton. Do not forget, Mamma—you are to bring Margaret back."

Inside the drawing-room, Mr. Henry Lennox sat cutting open the pages of a new review, seemingly oblivious to the storm gathering around him. "If you find Sholto's prolonged absence disagreeable, Edith," he remarked without looking up, "perhaps I should descend to Milton myself to render assistance."

"Oh, thank you," Edith replied, her voice softening. "Old Mr. Bell will manage everything; additional help may not be necessary. One rarely expects much *savoir-faire* from a resident Fellow. But dearest, darling Margaret—it will be wonderful to have her here again. You were such great allies years ago."

"Were we?" Mr. Henry asked indifferently, his gaze fixed on a passage in the journal.

"Well, perhaps not—I forget. I was so consumed with thoughts of Sholto. But isn't it providential? If Uncle is to pass, it is precisely now, when we have returned home, settled in the ancestral house, and fully prepared to receive Margaret? Poor thing! What a transformation awaits her from Milton! I shall procure new chintz for her bedroom, make it bright and cheerful, to lift her spirits."

Mrs. Shaw's journey to Milton was a tapestry woven from dread and determination. She oscillated between fearing the initial confrontation and plotting the swift removal of Margaret from "that horrid place" back to the plush comforts of Harley Street. "Oh dear!" she confided to her maid, staring out at the looming chimneys of the distant town. "Look at those chimneys! My poor sister Hale! I could not have rested in Naples had I foreseen this. I must have fetched her and Margaret away sooner." Privately, she acknowledged a truth she had long suppressed: she had always deemed her brother-in-law weak, but never so utterly diminished as he appeared now, having traded the radiant sanctuary of Helstone for this gray exile.

Within the house, Margaret remained unchanged. Pale, motionless, speechless, tearless. They informed her of her aunt's arrival, yet she offered neither surprise nor joy, nor did she register any aversion. Mr. Bell, his hunger finally returning, urged her to taste sweetbreads stewed with oysters, praising Dixon's culinary efforts. She shook her head with the same quiet obstinacy as the day before. Deprived of her company, he ate the entire dish himself, finding sustenance in the act of service.

It was Margaret who first perceived the halting of the cab arriving from the station. Her eyelids fluttered, her lips colored and trembling. Mr. Bell hastened downstairs to greet Mrs. Shaw. When they ascended, Margaret stood, fighting the vertigo that threatened to unbalance her. Upon seeing her aunt, she stepped forward into the open embrace awaiting her. First came the passionate release of tears soaking her aunt's shoulder, dissolving the numbness that had encased her heart. All the quiet habits of love, the tenderness of years, the bond forged with the dead—all melted away in that instant. An inexplicable likeness in look, tone, and gesture surged forth, reminding her with terrifying clarity of her mother, turning her frozen spirit into an overflowing river of warmth.

Mr. Bell slipped out of the room and retreated to the study, ordering a fire and attempting to distract himself by examining the books lining the shelves. Every volume whispered of his deceased friend, a constant reminder of the void left behind. Though his duties had shifted from watching Margaret to managing correspondence, his thoughts remained tethered to her. Just then, the sound of Mr. Thornton's voice reached the door. Dixon, emboldened by the arrival of Mrs. Shaw's entourage, dismissed him with cavalier brevity. The presence of the maid conjured visions of former grandeur, the Beresford lineage, and the "station"—a word she used with relish—from which her young mistress had been cast out and to which she was destined to return. These fantasies, shared complacently with Mrs. Shaw's maid, fueled Dixon's condescension toward anyone associated with Milton. Thus, despite her lingering awe of Mr. Thornton, she barred his entry with curt precision.

The discomfort of being contradicted vanished when Mr. Bell opened the study door. "Thornton! Is that you? Enter for a moment; I wish to speak with you."

Mr. Thornton complied, forcing Dixon to retreat to the kitchen, where she bolstered her ego with a prodigious tale of Sir John Beresford's coach-and-six during his tenure as High Sheriff.

"I confess, I am unsure why I summoned you," Mr. Bell admitted, pacing the room. "It is dreary enough to sit among objects that whisper of a dead friend. Yet, Margaret and her aunt must possess the drawing-room exclusively."

"Has Mrs.... has her aunt arrived?" Mr. Thornton asked.

"Arrived? Yes! Maid and all. One might have expected her to come alone at such a juncture! Now I shall be forced to vacate and navigate my way to the Clarendon."

"You must not go to the Clarendon. We have five or six vacant bedrooms at home."

"Well aired?"

"I trust my mother to ensure that."

"Then I shall merely ascend to bid that wan girl goodnight, pay my respects to her aunt, and depart with you immediately."

Mr. Bell lingered upstairs longer than anticipated. Mr. Thornton, impatient with business matters and having barely spared time to inquire after Miss Hale at Crampton, began to wonder at the delay.

Once they had set out for their walk, Mr. Bell voiced his turmoil. "I was detained by the women in the drawing-room. Mrs. Shaw is eager to return home—citing concerns for her daughter—and insists Margaret accompany her immediately. Yet she is no more fit for travel than I am for flight. Furthermore, she argues, with considerable justice, that she has acquaintances she must visit, farewells to render. Then her aunt harried her regarding old

The air in Milton hung heavy, a suffocating blanket of coal smoke and industrial grit that seemed to seep into the very marrow of the street. To Mrs. Shaw, a woman whose gentleness usually dictated her actions, this environment was an assault. The noise was deafening, the poverty of the working classes stark and repulsive, and the ostentatious display of the wealthy equally distasteful. Most grievous of all was the sight of the men—whether lord or laborer—all clad in ill-fitting garments that betrayed a lack of care, a symptom of a society unraveling under the weight of its own indifference.

"It is unbearable," Mrs. Shaw declared, her voice tight with anxiety. "Margaret cannot possibly recover her health here. The very atmosphere is hostile to her constitution. We must leave immediately."

Her urgency was born of genuine terror; she feared a recurrence of her own nervous afflictions, a shadow that had haunted her family for generations. She pressed her plea upon Margaret until the younger woman, drained by weeks of grief and exhaustion, finally capitulated. With a sigh that seemed to deflate her entire spirit, Margaret promised to depart the moment Wednesday concluded. That day was already etched in tragedy: the funeral of Mr. Hale, a man who had died far from the homes he knew, leaving behind a widow isolated among strangers. This separation weighed heavily on Margaret, who chided herself bitterly for having succumbed to a stupor during those initial days of sorrow. Had she remained lucid, she reasoned, she might have orchestrated a different outcome.

Before the mournful rites could begin, a letter arrived from Mr. Bell, bearing news that would alter the trajectory of their departure.

"My Dear Margaret," the letter began, its tone oscillating between paternal concern and the casual authority of a man who believed he held the keys to her destiny. "I intended to return to Milton on Thursday, but fate, in its capricious way, has summoned the Plymouth Fellows to a sudden duty. I cannot be absent from my post. Fortunately, Captain Lennox and Mr. Thornton are here. The former appears to be a sensible, well-intentioned soul; he has volunteered to travel to Milton to assist you in your futile search for a will—which, naturally, does not exist, unless you have ignored my previous instructions. Furthermore, the Captain insists on transporting you and his mother-in-law home. Given his wife's fragile state, I foresee no delay beyond Friday. Your steward, Dixon, is trustworthy; he can manage the household finances and the disposal of your belongings until my arrival. Should no will surface, I shall entrust the matter to my local attorney, for I suspect this 'smart' Captain lacks the acumen for business, despite his magnificent moustaches. A sale of your possessions is inevitable; therefore, select what you wish to save, or send a list later.

"There are two final points, and then I shall conclude. You are aware, or your late father certainly was, that upon my death, you inherit my fortune and goods. Do not mistake this for a morbid premonition; I mention it merely to clarify the terms of our future arrangement. The Lennoxes appear fond of you now, though sentimentality is a fickle commodity. Prudence dictates a formal agreement: you shall remit two hundred and fifty pounds annually to them, provided you find the arrangement congenial. (Do not be swindled into paying more for Dixon's services.) This stipulation ensures that if the Captain eventually desires solitude, you possess the means to relocate with your capital and dignity intact—or, heaven forbid, if I have not already claimed you to serve as my housekeeper. Regarding attire, domestic staff, and personal luxuries—including the confections that all young ladies consume until wisdom arrives with age—I intend to consult a trusted confidante to determine your allowance based on your father's estate.

"Now, Margaret, have you not already fled the page, wondering what right this elderly man possesses to dictate your affairs with such cavalier disregard? I am certain you have. Yet, he holds a rightful claim. He has cherished your father for thirty-five years, stood witness to his union, and closed his eyes in death. Moreover, he is your godfather. While his spiritual guidance may be limited by a humble awareness of your superior nature, he seeks to compensate by endowing you materially. Having no kin on earth to mourn him—who will lament the passing of Adam Bell?—his entire existence bends toward this singular purpose. And Margaret Hale is not the type to deny such devotion. Reply within the hour with a mere two lines indicating your decision. But remember: no thanks."

Margaret picked up her pen, her hand trembling violently. She scrawled, *"Margaret Hale is not the girl to say him nay."* In her weakened state, no other words formed in her mind, yet the phrasing gnawed at her conscience. Exhausted by the sheer effort of composition, she collapsed onto the bed, forcing her mind into silence.

"My dearest child," Mrs. Shaw asked softly, sensing the turmoil beneath her daughter's composure, "has this letter distressed you?"

"No!" Margaret whispered, her voice frail. "I shall be stronger once tomorrow passes."

"I am convinced, darling, that you will not find peace until we escape this foul air. I cannot comprehend how you have endured it for two years."

"Where else could I go? I cannot abandon Papa and Mama."

"Do not torment yourself further, my dear. It was undoubtedly for the best, though I lacked the insight to perceive your suffering. Even our butler's wife resides in a finer dwelling than this."

"It is occasionally lovely—in summer. One cannot judge by its current decay. I have been quite happy here," Margaret lied, closing her eyes to halt the conversation.

The house, once a sanctuary, now teemed with a forced comfort. Fires burned brightly in every chamber at Mrs. Shaw's command, and she doted on Margaret with lavish purchases of delicacies and soft linens, items she herself would have coveted. Yet, Margaret remained detached, viewing these gestures solely as evidence of her aunt's sacrifice. Restless despite her weakness, she wandered the rooms throughout the day, methodically separating objects she wished to keep. Dixon followed, ostensibly to gather instructions, but privately tasked with soothing her into rest.

"These books, Dixon, I shall retain. Send the remainder to Mr. Bell; he will appreciate them for their own sake and for my father's memory." She paused, selecting a specific volume. "Take this to Mr. Thornton after I depart. Wait; I shall draft a note." She sat abruptly, as if fearing the intrusion of thought, and wrote hurriedly:

She resumed her perusal of the house, touching familiar objects with a tender reluctance, knowing they were destined for the auction block. Yet, she spoke little. Dixon reported to Mrs. Shaw that Miss Hale appeared oblivious to her surroundings, listening to nothing while talking incessantly to distract herself. The result of such physical exertion was a crushing fatigue by evening, accompanied by a sleep more restorative than she had experienced since hearing of Mr. Hale's death.

By breakfast the following day, Margaret expressed a desire to bid farewell to a few acquaintances. Mrs. Shaw initially objected, citing propriety and the need for church attendance.

"But today is my only opportunity," Margaret insisted. "If Captain Lennox arrives this afternoon, and if we are compelled to leave tomorrow..."

"Oh, yes, we shall depart tomorrow. I am increasingly convinced this air is detrimental to your complexion. Besides, Edith awaits us, and you cannot be left alone at your age. If you insist on these calls, I shall accompany you. Dixon can arrange a carriage, I presume?"

Thus, Mrs. Shaw prepared to escort her niece, bringing Dixon along to manage the shawls and air-cushions. Margaret's face remained a mask of sorrow, refusing to soften into a smile despite the preparations. She dreaded visiting one particular location: Nicholas Higgins' cottage. She hoped her aunt would refuse to alight and traverse the narrow court, where damp laundry hung on lines stretching from house to house, threatening to slap her face with cold water with every gust of wind.

A silent conflict raged within Mrs. Shaw between maternal instinct and social convention; ultimately, convenience prevailed. With repeated warnings to avoid catching a fever, her aunt granted Margaret permission to visit places she had frequented freely in the past.

Nicholas was absent, leaving only Mary and a few of the Boucher children. Margaret reproached herself for her poor timing. Mary possessed a blunt intellect, though her heart was warm; upon understanding Margaret's purpose, she broke down into unrestrained weeping. Confronted with such raw emotion, Margaret found her carefully crafted pleasantries useless. She could only offer vague hopes of reunion and urge Mary to convey her message to her father: that she wished, if possible, for him to visit her after his evening labors.

As she departed, Margaret paused, looking around the modest interior. "I would like a small token to remind me of Bessy."

Mary's generosity flared instantly. "What could we give?" she cried. When Margaret pointed to a simple drinking cup, the one always placed beside Bessy's feverish lips, Mary protested, "Oh, take something better! That only cost fourpence!"

"That will suffice, thank you," Margaret replied, hastening away. The faint glow of satisfaction lingered on Mary's face, a fleeting warmth in the gray day.

"Now to Mrs. Thornton's," Margaret thought, steel

The silence of the Harley Street house was not peace; it was a heavy, suffocating blanket woven from the threads of Edith's recovery and the sheer absence of struggle. For Margaret, this extreme quietude was a double-edged sword. While it granted her the natural rest her delicate constitution craved, it also stripped away the familiar friction of life, leaving her adrift in a sea of luxurious stagnation. Within the span of two months, the world had shifted beneath her feet. She was no longer the vicar's daughter of Helstone, nor the humble resident of the Milton cottage where anxiety and poverty were constant companions. Here, the machinery of domesticity ran on silent, oiled gears. Troubles were unknown quantities; cares were non-existent variables.

Mrs. Shaw and Edith treated Margaret with a cloying affection that bordered on infantilization, persistently referring to this grand estate as *her* home. Yet, a bitter seed took root in Margaret's chest. Was it truly ungrateful to harbor a secret longing for the chaotic warmth of Milton? There, amidst her father's worried brow and her mother's frailty, life had a texture, a grit that defined the soul. Now, Edith, eager to reclaim her health, rushed to transform Margaret's bedroom into a mirror of her own vanity, flooding the space with soft comforts and pretty knick-knacks. Mrs. Shaw and her maid labored tirelessly to restore Margaret's wardrobe to a state of elegant variety, while Captain Lennox moved through the days with a gentle, detached competence. He spent hours in his wife's dressing room, playing briefly with his son, and then retreating to his club or dining out, leaving the atmosphere of the house to fester in his wake.

Just as Margaret began to feel the crushing weight of this eventless ease, Edith descended the stairs, resuming her role as the center of the social universe. Margaret, ever dutiful, slipped back into the shadow of service, managing the semblance of duty for her cousin. She answered notes, reminded Edith of engagements, and tended to her when the gaiety faded, all while cultivating a faint illness that allowed her to remain close without demanding energy. But when the rest of the family plunged into the frenetic business of the London Season, Margaret was left alone in the vast rooms. Her thoughts drifted inevitably to Milton, haunted by a strange, hollow vacuum in her heart. She feared she was becoming sleepily deadened, forgetting everything beyond the lapping luxury of her surroundings. The servants, she observed, lived in an underground realm of their own, appearing only to serve a whim or a want, their hopes and fears invisible to her.

One evening, sitting alone in the Lennox drawing room, Margaret held Dixon's letters in her hand but refused to open them. The cessation of news from Milton felt like a sudden famine. Occasionally, Dixon's business-like correspondence quoted Mr. Thornton regarding furniture or landlords, but the name rarely surfaced, and the connection to her old life remained severed. She wondered if anyone in that whirlwind missed her, if Higgins remembered her, when suddenly the door opened and Mr. Bell was announced. Margaret hurried the letters into her workbasket, rising with a blush that betrayed her guilt.

"Oh, Mr. Bell! I never thought of seeing you!"

"You give me a welcome, I hope, as well as that very pretty start of surprise," Mr. Bell replied, his eyes twinkling.

"Have you dined? How did you come? Let me order you some dinner."

"If you're going to have any. Otherwise, there is no one who cares less for eating than I do. But where are the others? Gone out to dinner? Left you alone?"

"Oh yes! and it is such a rest. I was just thinking—"

"But will you run the risk of dinner? I don't know if there is anything in the house."

"Why, to tell you the truth, I dined at my club. Only they don't cook as well as they did, so I thought, if you were going to dine, I might try and make out my dinner. But never mind! There aren't ten cooks in England to be trusted at impromptu dinners. If their skill and their fires will stand it, their tempers won't. You shall make me some tea, Margaret. And now, what were you thinking of? Whose letters were those, god-daughter, that you hid away so speedily?"

"Only Dixon's," Margaret replied, her face flushing deeper.

"Whew! Is that all? Who do you think came up in the train with me?"

"I don't know," Margaret said, resolving against guessing.

"Your what d'ye call him? What's the right name for a cousin-in-law's brother?"

"Mr. Henry Lennox?"

"Yes," Mr. Bell confirmed. "You knew him formerly, didn't you? What sort of a person is he, Margaret?"

"I liked him long ago," Margaret admitted, glancing down. Then she looked up, her expression hardening slightly. "You know we have been corresponding about Frederick since; but I have not seen him for nearly three years, and he may be changed. What did you think of him?"

"I don't know. He was so busy trying to find out who I was, in the first instance, and what I was in the second, that he never let out what he was; unless indeed that veiled curiosity of his as to what manner of man he had to talk to was not a good piece, and a fair indication of his character. Do you call him good-looking, Margaret?"

"No! certainly not. Do you?"

"Not I. But I thought perhaps you might. Is he a great deal here?"

"I fancy he is when he is in town. He has been on circuit now since I came. But—Mr. Bell—have you come from Oxford or from Milton?"

"From Milton. Don't you see I'm smoke-dried?"

"Certainly. But I thought that it might be the effect of the antiquities of Oxford."

"Come now, be a sensible woman! In Oxford, I could have managed all the landlords in the place, and had my own way, with half the trouble your Milton landlord has given me, and defeated me after all. He won't take the house off our hands till next June twelve-month. Luckily, Mr. Thornton found a tenant for it. Why don't you ask after Mr. Thornton, Margaret? He has proved himself a very active friend of yours, I can tell you. Taken more than half the trouble off my hands."

"And how is he? How is Mrs. Thornton?" Margaret asked hurriedly, her voice dropping below her normal pitch despite her effort to sound casual.

"I suppose they're well. I've been staying at their house till I was driven out of it by the perpetual clack about that Thornton girl's marriage. It was too much for Thornton himself, though she was his sister. He used to go and sit in his own room perpetually. He's getting past the age for caring for such things, either as principal or accessory. I was surprised to find the old lady falling into the current, and carried away by her daughter's enthusiasm for orange-blossoms and lace. I thought Mrs. Thornton had been made of sterner stuff."

"She would put on any assumption of feeling to veil her daughter's weakness," Margaret whispered.

"Perhaps so. You've studied her, have you? She doesn't seem over fond of you, Margaret."

"I know it," Margaret said. "Oh, here is tea, at last!" she exclaimed, as if relieved by the interruption.

With the arrival of tea came Mr. Henry Lennox. He had walked up to Harley Street after a late dinner, expecting to find his brother and sister-in-law at home. Margaret suspected him of being as thankful as she was for the presence of a third party, especially considering their first meeting since the memorable day of his offer and her refusal at Helstone. She could hardly find words, grateful for the trivial occupations of the tea table that provided an excuse for silence and allowed him to recover his composure. Indeed, he had forced himself to Harley Street tonight, hoping to navigate an awkward encounter that was already difficult in the presence of Captain Lennox and Edith, and now doubly so with her standing alone as the primary conversational partner.

She was the first to regain her self-possession.

"Mr. Lennox, I have been so much obliged to you for all you have done about Frederick."

"I am only sorry it has been so unsuccessful," he replied, casting a quick, assessing glance toward Mr. Bell as if gauging how far he could push the conversation before hitting a wall. Margaret, sensing his hesitation, immediately pivoted to address Mr. Bell, effectively including him in the dialogue and implying his awareness of the efforts made to clear Frederick's name.

"That Horrocks—that very last witness of all—has proved as unavailing as all the others. Mr. Lennox has discovered that he sailed for Australia only last August; only two months before Frederick was in England, and gave us the names of—"

"Frederick in England! You never told me that!" Mr. Bell exclaimed in genuine surprise.

"I thought you knew. I never doubted you had been told. Of course, it was a great secret, and perhaps I should not have named it now," Margaret said, a flicker of dismay crossing her features.

"I have never named it to either my brother or your cousin," Mr. Lennox stated, his tone carrying a professional dryness that felt like an implied reproach.

"Never mind, Margaret. I am not living in a talking, babbling world

The echo of Landor's verse hung heavy in the air, a ghostly refrain that haunted Mr. Bell's waking consciousness and tore through the fabric of his sleep. For hours, his mind had wandered the verdant, impossible landscapes of Helstone, re-enacting the role of a tutor in a college where he once held the rank of Fellow. In this fever-dream, time and space dissolved into a fluid suspension; he leaped across babbling brooks that defied gravity, soaring through an autumnal haze where the air smelled of sweet herbs and blooming flowers. Here, amidst the gorgeous decay of falling leaves, stood his newly married friend, the proud Vicar, and beside him, the young wife whose expression was a complex tapestry of resentment toward her new wealth and fierce devotion to her husband—a dynamic Mr. Bell had observed with keen clarity forty years prior. The dream was so vividly textured with the sensory richness of life that upon awakening, the stark reality of his current existence felt like a cold, distant nightmare.

He found himself in the stifling, ornately furnished chamber of a London hotel, the silence pressing against his ears. Those who had spoken moments before, who had brushed against him, were gone—dead, buried, erased from the continuum of earth's fleeting forever. He was an old man, stripped of the exultant vitality of his youth, consumed by an utter loneliness that rendered thought unbearable. With frantic haste, he dressed, attempting to drown the memory of loss in the mundane rituals of preparing for breakfast in Harley Street.

Across the room, the atmosphere was equally fraught. Margaret watched as the lawyer's documents unfolded a grim narrative. Her eyes widened, pupils dilating until the irises seemed swallowed by darkness, while her lips grew pale as white wax. Each detail Mr. Lennox read acted as a stone dropped into a well, extinguishing the last flickers of hope regarding Frederick's exoneration. Evidence that should have cleared his name crumbled away, vanishing beneath the weight of fate's decree. Even Mr. Lennox, usually a man of precise, regulated tones, softened his voice to a tender murmur as he approached the precipice of finality.

"It was a foolish proposal of mine," Lennox admitted, his concern etched into every syllable. "Lieutenant Hale..." He paused, granting the title of the service from which the young man had been so cruelly cast aside, a small act of kindness that momentarily soothed Margaret's anguish. "Lieutenant Hale is happy now; more secure in fortune and future prospects than he could ever have been in the navy; and has, doubtless, adopted his wife's country as his own."

"That is it," Margaret whispered, forcing a brittle smile. "It seems so selfish in me to regret it... and yet he is lost to me, and I am so lonely."

Lennox turned his page, silently wishing for the prosperity he believed awaited him someday, while Mr. Bell blew his nose, maintaining a stoic silence. Within minutes, Margaret composed herself, masking her devastation behind a veneer of polite gratitude. She was acutely aware that her visible grief might seem ungrateful to the man who had spared her pain, yet she knew she would not have endured the ordeal without it.

Mr. Bell rose to bid her farewell, his movements stiff with age. "Margaret!" he called out, fumbling with his gloves. "I am going down to Helstone tomorrow to inspect the old place. Would you like to come with me? Or would it give you too much pain? Speak out, don't be afraid."

"Oh, Mr. Bell," she began, her voice breaking, unable to find the words beyond the initial plea. Instead, she reached out, grasping his gnarled, gout-ridden hand and pressing a kiss to it.

"Come, come; that's enough," he said, flushing with awkward embarrassment. "I suppose your aunt Shaw will trust you with me. We'll start tomorrow morning, arriving around two. We'll take a snack, order dinner at the Lennard Arms—that's where it used to be—and walk into the forest to whet our appetites. Can you manage it, Margaret? I know it will be a trial for both of us, but it will be a pleasure to me, at least. We'll dine on doe-venison, if we can procure any, and then I'll retire for a nap while you visit old friends.
