I'll return you safely, barring railway accidents, and I'll insure your life for a thousand pounds before we depart, which may offer some solace to your relations. Otherwise, I promise to bring you back to Mrs. Shaw by lunchtime on Friday. So, if you consent, I shall ascend to propose the terms formally."

"It's no use my trying to explain how much I shall like it," Margaret replied, her voice trembling through fresh tears.

"Well, then, prove your gratitude by keeping those fountains of yours dry for the next two days," Mr. Bell countered gently. "If you don't, I shall feel quite queasy about my own lachrymal ducts, and I dislike that sensation."

"I won't cry a drop," she vowed, winking rapidly to shed the moisture from her lashes and forcing a radiant, if strained, smile onto her face.

"There's my good girl," he said, satisfied. "Then we shall go upstairs and finalize everything."

Margaret stood in a state of almost trembling eagerness as Mr. Bell conferred with Aunt Shaw. Initially startled, the older woman became doubtful and perplexed, her mind racing through the proprieties and dangers of the journey. Yet, eventually, she yielded—not necessarily to her own conviction, but to the rough, persuasive force of Mr. Bell's confidence. Until Margaret's safe return confirmed the success of the endeavor, she could not fully reconcile the morality of the trip. Finally, the prospect of the young woman's happiness settled her doubts. "She was sure it had been a very kind thought of Mr. Bell's," she declared, her voice steady. "And just what she herself had been wishing for Margaret, providing the very change of scenery she required after all this anxious waiting."

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a city that had forgotten the weight of grief. Yet here, in the carriage, the world outside was a study in stark contrasts. The train whirled away from the grey slate roofs of the North, plunging into a landscape bathed in a ruddy, golden light that seemed to demand joy from the very soil. Pigeons, brooding in the warm air, settled upon the quaint, peaked gables of the South Country, their soft feathers ruffled by a sun that felt dangerously intimate. It was a landscape of *German Idylls*—Herman and Dorothea, Evangeline—where nature remained eternally young, oblivious to the human tragedy unfolding within the carriage.

Margaret sat opposite Mr. Bell, her face a mask of practiced brightness, though her heart was a hollow chamber echoing with the absence of her parents. She had cried quietly in the privacy of her own mind, a silent storm that no one else could witness, but now, as the miles rolled by, the scenery itself became a torture. Every familiar tree, every turn of the road flooded in sunlight, screamed of "days that are no more." She was an orphan now, drifting through a world that had moved on, while her parents had vanished from the face of the earth, leaving her stranded in a memory of a gloomy departure that felt like a lifetime ago.

Mr. Bell, perceptive and kind, wisely held his tongue, allowing her grief to ride alongside the train in comfortable silence. Soon, they approached Helstone, the destination that sharpened her sorrow into a physical ache. The carriage drew up to the Lennard Arms, a half-farmhouse, half-inn that stood slightly apart from the road, asserting a dignity that refused to court travelers with obtrusive hospitality. Its front faced the village green, dominated by an immemorial lime-tree, its leafy depths hiding the grim escutcheon of the Lennards.

Inside, the atmosphere was heavy with the heat of hay-making. The landlady, Mrs. Purkis, emerged not with the hurried efficiency of a commercial hostess, but with the weary grace of someone buried in domestic labor. She apologized profusely for her delay, citing the demands of the fields and the soft turf that had masked the sound of their wheels. Then, her eyes caught a glimpse of Margaret's face in the dappled shade of the parlour.

"Why, bless me!" she exclaimed, rushing forward. "It's Miss Hale, Jenny! Come here, come directly, it's Miss Hale!"

Mrs. Purkis greeted her with a motherly fondness that felt like a balm, asking immediately about the Vicar and Miss Dixon. When Margaret faltered, choking on the single word "Papa," the landlady's demeanor shifted instantly. The news of Mr. Hale's sudden death at Oxford struck her with the force of a physical blow. She turned to Mr. Bell, her voice trembling with disbelief, recounting rumors of a gentleman who had spoken of Mrs. Hale's passing months prior, yet never mentioning the Vicar's illness.

"It is so, however," Mr. Bell confirmed gently, explaining the tragic brevity of the clergyman's life. He introduced himself as a friend of the deceased and a godfather to Margaret, inviting her to rest and dine. Mrs. Purkis, momentarily distracted by the sheer magnitude of the loss, spoke of the new Vicar—a teetotaler, a magistrate, a man of "receipts for economical cooking" who built nurseries and installed plate-glass windows. She described him and his wife as "stirring people" who talked so much they knocked one down, replacing the old, comforting chaos of the parish with a rigid, noisy order. She lamented the loss of the old ways, where mothers sent good malt liquor to haymakers and relied on salts and senna, contrasting it sharply with the new era of comfits and unnatural bread-making.

After attending to Margaret's needs—showing her to a room scented with fresh roses and spring water—Mrs. Purkis departed, leaving the two men and the orphan to prepare for the evening. Mr. Bell had arranged a rustic luncheon of strawberries, cream, brown bread, and Stilton, a meal designed to ground them in the present moment.

They set out for a walk, navigating a landscape where time seemed to be erasing the past. Old trees felled the previous autumn lay bare; squatters' cottages, rough and decaying, had been replaced by tidy, respectable structures. At the spot where Margaret and Mr. Lennox had once sketched, the venerable beech tree, lightning-scarred and beloved, was gone, replaced by a small garden. The old man who had inhabited the ruinous cottage nearby was dead.

"I did not think I had been so old," Margaret whispered, turning away with a sigh that carried the weight of decades compressed into a single afternoon.

"Yes!" Mr. Bell agreed. "It is the first changes among familiar things that make such a mystery of time to the young. Afterwards, we lose the sense of the mysterious. I take changes in all I see as a matter of course. The instability of all human things is familiar to me, to you it is new and oppressive."

Guided by a lingering sense of duty, they sought out little Susan, the child who had waited for Margaret's farewell. Finding the girl absent, they learned she had been sent to the parochial school. The mother, a widow, confessed her sadness, admitting that while Susan was becoming a "handy girl," she was losing the intimacy of nightly lessons in the Bible. Mr. Bell, horrified by the prospect of formal schooling, argued passionately for the simplicity of home-based education, fearing the corruption of the child's soul.

But the conversation soon drifted to darker waters. Margaret asked about old Betty Barnes, a neighbor who had once been a peacemaker. The answer was curt: "She stole my cat."

When pressed, the woman revealed the horrific truth. Betty, terrified by a gypsy fortune-teller's prophecy regarding her husband's missing Sunday clothes, had resorted to a savage country superstition. Believing that the agonized cries of a cat boiling or roasting alive could compel the powers of darkness to fulfill a wish, she had sacrificed her pet. The woman, utterly convinced of the charm's efficacy, expressed only indignation that her specific cat had been chosen for the sacrifice, unable to comprehend the cruelty of the act itself.

Margaret listened in horror, her mind reeling at the utter lack of imagination and sympathy required to commit such an atrocity. She tried to explain the logic, the moral bankruptcy of the belief system, but the woman remained trapped in her own reality, repeating that while it was cruel, it was effective. Margaret walked away sick at heart, the image of the burning cat searing her memory.

"You are a good girl not to triumph over me," Mr. Bell observed softly.

"How?"

"I own I am wrong about schooling. Anything rather than have that child brought up in such practical paganism."

Determined to shield Susan from such influences, they resolved to visit the school. As they threaded their way through the bosky dells, the soft green influence of the countryside failed to charm away the shock and pain of the tale they had just witnessed. The cruelty of the woman, the indifference to suffering, and the erosion of the old, humane world weighed heavily on Margaret. Yet, amidst the decay and the strange new orders imposed upon the village, she clung to the hope that somewhere, in the quiet corners of this changing world, innocence still survived.

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the persistent beating of a heart she could no longer control. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Mr. Bell, his script sprawling and confident, yet suffocating under the weight of a melancholy she found increasingly difficult to bear.

Dixon stood nearby, a conduit for the trivialities of Milton, her voice carrying the hollow echo of gossip that filled the silence between the thunderclaps. She recounted how Martha had retreated to Miss Thornton's household following the marriage, detailing the bridesmaids' gowns and the breakfasts that followed the ceremony. She spoke of the public's judgment—that Mr. Thornton had thrown away too much dignity on a grand wedding while bleeding financially from the strike, paying exorbitant sums for failed contracts. Then came the auction: the pathetic prices fetched for Dixon's own cherished furniture, a shameful testament to the disparity between the rich and the struggling. Dixon noted how Mrs. Thornton had secured bargains, only for Mr. Thornton to bid against himself the next day, driven by a desperate need to possess something, much to the amusement of the bystanders. "If Mrs. Thornton pays too little," Dixon observed with a shrug, "Mr. Thornton pays too much."

It was a cycle of petty economics, devoid of the gravity of the world outside. Mr. Bell's instructions regarding the books were equally opaque; he was a man of impossible particularity, his letters a labyrinth of confusion that would have been clear if he had simply walked through the door. As for the Higginses, Dixon's memory betrayed her, skewed by an aristocratic bias that rendered the lives of those beneath her status as vague, treacherous dreams. Nicholas was well, she claimed, visiting often to inquire after Miss Margaret—a rarity shared only once by Mr. Thornton. Mary, however, was a "great, stout, slatternly thing," a figure of indeterminate existence. Dixon whispered a rumor, perhaps a dream, that Mary had taken work at Mr. Thornton's mill to learn the arts of cooking, a notion so absurd it bordered on the surreal. Margaret nodded, agreeing that the story was incoherent enough to be a fever dream, yet grateful for someone with whom she could speak of Milton, even if Dixon preferred to dwell on Mr. Bell's speeches, interpreting them as a veiled proposal to make her his heiress.

But Margaret received no encouragement. Her young lady remained silent, refusing to gratify her insinuations, however disguised. Beneath this domestic stagnation lay a strange, undefined longing: the hope that Mr. Bell would finally travel to Milton. At Helstone, they had agreed that any explanation she craved must come from him, delivered orally, never forced. Yet his letters grew shorter, sharper, tinged with a bitter weariness that unsettled her. He spoke of an "old-fashioned complaint called the spleen," a condition he left her to diagnose as either mental or physical, granting himself permission to grumble without sending bulletins. In response, Margaret ceased her inquiries, fearing the truth behind his silence.

One day, Edith dropped a fragment of a conversation, planting a seed that sprouted rapidly in Margaret's mind: Mr. Bell intended to take her to Cadiz to meet her new sister-in-law, to witness the aftermath of the mutiny firsthand. Edith, weary of the questioning, admitted that Mr. Bell had only half-thought of it, suggesting it as a suitable destination for the long vacation. "Why not Spain?" Edith had asked, as if geography held no significance beyond convenience.

Edith's reaction was immediate and defensive. Any pleasure Margaret derived from afar was interpreted as a tacit affront, a proof of indifference. "I know I care much more for Margaret than Margaret does for me," Edith cried, tears welling up. Margaret comforted her, but the distance between them widened with every word. Edith viewed the world through a lens of scarcity, where joy shared with others diminished its value. So Margaret kept her delight hidden, venting it only to Dixon when dressing for dinner.

"She's a Papist, Miss, isn't she?" Dixon asked, her eyes wide with apprehension.
"I believe—oh yes, certainly!" Margaret replied, a flicker of dread dimming her spirits instantly.
"And they live in a Popish country?"
"Yes."
"Then I'm afraid I must say, that my soul is dearer to me than even Master Frederick, his own dear self. I should be in a perpetual terror, Miss, lest I should be converted."

"Oh," said Margaret, trying to steady her voice. "I do not know that I am going; and if I go, I am not such a fine lady as to be unable to travel without you. No! dear old Dixon, you shall have a long holiday, if we go. But I'm afraid it is a long 'if.'"

Dixon recoiled. First, she disliked the diminutive "dear old Dixon," a term of endearment Margaret used freely for those she favored, yet which made Dixon wince, reminding her of her age despite being barely past fifty. Second, she harbored a secret curiosity about Spain, the Inquisition, and the mysteries of the faith. Clearing her throat, she feigned reluctance before probing further. "If you take care never to see a priest, or enter into one of their churches, there would be so very much danger of your being converted?"

Master Frederick, of course, had succumbed to conversion unaccountably. "I fancy it was love that first predisposed him to conversion," Margaret sighed, the words escaping before she could check them.
"Indeed, Miss!" Dixon exclaimed, seizing the logic. "Well! I can preserve myself from priests, and from churches; but love steals in unawares! I think it's as well I should not go."

Margaret feared indulging in this Spanish fantasy, for it served only to distract her from the agonizing impatience of waiting for Mr. Thornton. Mr. Bell remained stationary at Oxford, bound by some secret restraint that prevented him from coming to Milton. Nor could she bring herself to mention Edith's revelation of the potential trip to Spain; it might have been a fleeting fancy, born of a sunny day at Helstone, yet if true, it promised a bright outlet from the monotony that was slowly crushing her life.

A small solace existed in Edith's son. He was the pride and plaything of the household, provided he behaved. But his will was strong, and when he erupted into stormy passions, Edith collapsed into despair, sighing, "Oh dear, what shall I do with him! Do, Margaret, please ring the bell for Hanley." Paradoxically, Margaret found the child more endearing in these moments of turbulence than in his compliant, blue-sashed moods. She would carry him into a private room, engaging in a fierce battle of wills. With a firm power that subdued him into peace, she countered his charms and wiles with arguments for righteousness, until he would rub his hot, tear-smeared face against hers, kissing and caressing until sleep claimed him in her arms. These were Margaret's sweetest moments, brief tastes of a maternal warmth she believed was forever denied to her.

Into this dynamic entered Mr. Henry Lennox, adding a cold brilliance to the household. Margaret found him sharper, less warm than before, yet enriched by strong intellectual tastes and varied knowledge that seasoned the otherwise insipid conversations. She caught glimpses of a slight contempt in his eyes for his brother and sister-in-law, viewing their lives as frivolous and purposeless. Once, in her presence, he queried his brother sharply about abandoning his profession. When Captain Lennox replied that he had sufficient means to live upon, Mr. Lennox curled his lip with disdain. "And is that all you live for?"

Yet the brothers remained attached, bound by the familiar dynamic of the clever leading and the patient being led. Mr. Lennox pushed forward in his career, calculatingly cultivating connections for future advantage. Keen-sighted, far-seeing, intelligent, sarcastic, and proud, he was a force of nature in the room. Since their initial conversation regarding Frederick's affairs, their interactions had been limited, yet enough to erode the shyness on Margaret's side and the mortified pride on his. They met continually, though she sensed he avoided solitude with her. Both perceived that they had drifted strangely apart from their former anchorage, separated by divergent opinions and tastes.

Still, when he spoke with remarkable epigrammatic point, or displayed unusual eloquence, Margaret felt his eye seek the expression of her countenance first, if only for an instant. In the constant family intercourse that threw them together, her opinion was the one to which he listened with a deference that was reluctant, concealed, and painfully complete.

The dinner parties hosted by Mrs. Lennox were spectacles of cultivated brilliance, meticulously engineered to dazzle. Beauty was supplied by her guests; Captain Lennox provided the effortless command of current affairs; and Mr. Henry Lennox, accompanied by a scattering of ambitious young men, furnished the wit and the razor-sharp intellect that flowed so naturally they appeared unburdened by effort. Yet, amidst this glittering display, Margaret's dissatisfaction remained a silent, corrosive undercurrent. She watched as every facet of human capability—every talent, every sentiment, even every inclination toward virtue—was consumed as fuel for fireworks. The sacred, hidden flame burned itself out in a shower of sparks and crackles, leaving nothing but embers. Art was discussed only in terms of its sensual surface, its external allure, devoid of any attempt to grasp its deeper teachings. High-minded subjects were lashed into enthusiastic rhetoric within the confines of the dining room, only to be abandoned the moment the doors closed and solitude returned. Their capacity for genuine appreciation was squandered, distilled down to a mere stream of socially appropriate phrases.

One evening, after the gentlemen had retreated to the drawing-room, Mr. Lennox approached Margaret. It was nearly the first voluntary word he had spoken to her since her return to Harley Street. "You did not seem pleased by what Shirley was saying at dinner," he observed.

"Didn't I?" Margaret replied, a faint smile touching her lips. "My face must be remarkably expressive. It seems to have retained the knack of eloquence."

"I did not like," she said quickly, "his manner of advocating what he knew to be wrong—so glaringly wrong, even in jest."

"But it was exceedingly clever. How each word landed! Did you recall the felicitous epithets?"

"Yes."

"And yet, you would likely despise them if you considered them seriously. Do not hesitate to tell me so, despite the fact that he is my friend."

"There!" she stopped abruptly, realizing the trap. "That is precisely the tone in you, that—"

He paused, listening intently to see if she would complete her thought. She flushed crimson and turned away. As she moved, he spoke in a voice low and clear, cutting through the noise of the room. "If my tones, or my modes of thought, are what you dislike, will you grant me the justice of telling me so directly? And thus give me the opportunity to learn how to please you."

For weeks thereafter, there was no word from Mr. Bell regarding his departure for Milton. He had hinted at the journey at Helstone as a possibility arising soon, yet he must have managed his affairs through correspondence. Margaret reasoned that if he possessed the ability, he would certainly avoid traveling to a place he disliked, especially one he understood so little. She knew he recognized the necessity of the meeting, yet the timing mattered less to her than the medium; the explanation required the intimacy of speech, not ink. Whether it was summer, autumn, or winter, the season held little significance compared to the delay. It was now August, and the Spanish trip he had casually mentioned to Edith remained unmentioned. Margaret struggled to reconcile herself to the fading of this illusion.

Then, one morning, a letter arrived. Next week, he intended to come to town. He wished to consult her on a plan forming in his mind. Furthermore, he planned to seek medical attention, having finally conceded to her view that it was preferable to attribute his irritability and crossness to failing health rather than to his own stubbornness. Reading the letter later, Margaret noted a tone of forced cheerfulness, but at the time, her attention was captured by Edith's frantic exclamations.

"Coming up to town! Oh dear! And I am so utterly drained by the heat that I doubt I possess the strength for another dinner. Besides, everyone has departed save our dear, stupid selves, who cannot decide where to go. There will be no one to meet him."

"I am certain he would far prefer dining with us in private company rather than among the most agreeable strangers you could possibly assemble. Moreover, if he is unwell, he will not desire invitations. I am glad he has admitted it at last. I was convinced he was ill from the entire tenor of his letters, yet he refused to answer my inquiries, leaving me with no confidant to relay news."

"Oh! He is not severely ill, or he would not entertain thoughts of Spain."

"He never mentions Spain."

"No! But the plan he intends to propose clearly concerns it. However, would you truly travel in weather such as this?"

"Oh, it will cool daily. Yes! Consider it. I am only afraid I have indulged in thinking and wishing too much—in that absorbing, willful fashion that inevitably leads either to disappointment or to a gratification that feels hollow in spirit."

"That is superstitious, surely, Margaret."

"No, I do not believe it is. Only it ought to serve as a warning, checking me from yielding to such passionate desires. It is akin to the cry, 'Give me children, or else I die.' I fear my own cry is, 'Let me go to Cadiz, or else I die.'"

"My dear Margaret! You will be persuaded to stay, and then what shall I do? Oh! I wish I could find someone for you to marry here, someone whose presence I could guarantee!"

"I shall never marry."

"Nonsense, and double nonsense! Why, as Sholto remarks, you are such an attraction to the house that he knows countless men who would gladly visit next year solely for your sake."

Margaret drew herself up with haughty dignity. "Do you know, Edith, I sometimes think your life on Corfu has taught you..."

"Well!"

"Just a shade or two of coarseness."

Edith began to sob with such bitter vehemence, declaring that Margaret had lost all affection for her and ceased to regard her as a friend, that Margaret realized she had voiced an opinion too harsh for the relief of her own wounded pride. By the end of the day, she had become Edith's devoted slave, while the little lady, overwhelmed by hurt feelings, lay like a sacrificial victim upon the sofa, heaving profound sighs until sleep claimed her.

Mr. Bell failed to appear even on the day he had twice postponed his visit. The following morning, a letter from Wallis, his servant, arrived. His master had not been feeling well for some time—the true cause of the delayed journey—and at the precise moment he was scheduled to depart for London, he had suffered an apoplectic fit. Wallis added, citing the opinion of the physicians, that he could not survive the night; it was more than probable that by the time Miss Hale received this missive, her poor master would already be gone.

Margaret received the letter at breakfast. She turned pale as she read it, then silently handed it to Edith and withdrew from the room. Edith was profoundly shocked, crying in a sobbing, frightened, childlike manner that distressed her husband greatly. Mrs. Shaw was eating breakfast in her own chamber, and upon her husband fell the daunting task of reconciling his wife to the terrifying proximity of death, a concept seemingly foreign to her experience. Here was a man destined to dine with them today, now lying dead or dying. It took considerable time before she could summon the resolve to think of Margaret. Then she sprang up and followed her upstairs into her room.

Dixon was packing a few toiletry items, and Margaret was hastily donning her bonnet, weeping continuously, her hands trembling so violently she could barely tie the strings.

"Oh, dear Margaret! How shocking! What are you doing? Are you going out? Sholto would telegraph or do anything you wish."

"I am going to Oxford. A train departs in half an hour. Dixon has offered to accompany me, but I could have traveled alone. I must see him once more. Besides, he may be recovering and require care. He has been like a father to me. Do not stop me, Edith."

"But I must. Mother will not approve. Come and ask her, Margaret. You do not know where you are going. I would not mind if he had his own house; but in his Fellow's rooms! Come to Mother and ask her permission before you leave. It will not take a minute."

Margaret yielded, missing her train. In the suddenness of the crisis, Mrs. Shaw became bewildered and hysterical, causing the precious moments to slip away. However, another train departed in a couple of hours. After various debates concerning propriety and impropriety, it was decided that Captain Lennox should accompany Margaret, for the one constant in her nature was her resolution to proceed, alone or otherwise, by the next available train, regardless of what others might say about the correctness of the step.

Her father's friend, her own friend, lay at the point of death. The vividness of this thought struck her with such force that she was surprised by the firmness with which she asserted her right to independent action. Five minutes before departure, she found herself seated in a railway carriage opposite Captain Lennox. It was always a comfort to her to believe she had acted, even if only to confirm that he had passed away during the night. She visualized the rooms he had occupied, associating them forevermore with the fondest memories of her father and his single, cherished, faithful friend.

They had promised Edith before departing that, if their fears proved true, they would return for dinner. Thus, the long, lingering gaze around the room where her father had died had to be interrupted, replaced by a quiet farewell to the kindly old face that had so often offered pleasant words, merry

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Mr. Henry Lennox, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest conquests in the realm of law and literature.

Margaret traced the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener, her movements precise despite the turmoil within. Lennox was a man of undeniable erudition, yet his correspondence was suffocated by the very patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of Virgil, of modern English poetry, of Fielding and Rochefoucauld, positioning himself as the benevolent instructor to her "undefeated resolution to admire only the right things."

"The right things," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned to his pages on Fielding. Lennox excused the novelist's coarse morality, arguing that men must be pardoned the "vices of young blood" so long as they were brave and generous. He painted a nostalgic picture of a "lusty old militant world," entirely blind to the fact that this merry England was built upon the bruised backs of women and the indentured silence of the poor. He dismissed the systemic inequalities that left women like Sophia Western with no recourse but to forgive the betrayals of their husbands.

Yet, beneath this veneer of intellectual superiority, there was a hunger. He spoke of her fortune as if it were merely a stepping stone, a "first advance in wealth" that he owed to her, promising to repay it with interest through future honors. He viewed her not as a soul to be understood, but as a project to be completed, a Cleopatra waiting to be tamed from her "Zenobia ways" into something more palatable for the court of society.

Margaret closed the letter, the paper crinkling under her thumb. She thought of the sea at Cromer, where she had spent hours staring at the waves chafing against the pebbly shore, listening to the eternal psalm of the tide. Those hours had not been wasted; they had allowed her to rearrange the fragments of her shattered life, to distinguish between the obedience demanded by authority and the freedom required for her own spirit. She had learned that she must answer for her own life, settling the difficult problem of how much of herself could be merged into duty and how much reserved for her own agency.

Now, she faced the new reality of her inheritance. Two thousand pounds in legacies, forty thousand in property value—it was a sum that would transform her from a dependent girl into a woman of means. But money could not heal the silence of Mr. Bell's death, nor could it explain the tragedy of Leonard's passing to the man she had once trusted. She resolved to stop hoping for explanations that would never come and instead focus on the life immediately before her.

When her aunt Shaw and Edith Lennox arrived, bringing with them the usual chatter of social maneuvering, Margaret listened with a calm detachment. Her aunt, good-natured but oblivious, urged her to accept a footman, fearing the burden of independence. Edith, equally eager to maintain the status quo, pleaded with her not to become "strong-minded," warning that such a trait was dangerous for a woman.

"Don't be afraid, Edith," Margaret replied, her voice steady. "I'll faint on your hands at the servants' dinner-time, the very first opportunity; and then, what with Sholto playing with the fire, and the baby crying, you'll begin to wish for a strong-minded woman, equal to any emergency."

"And you'll not grow too good to joke and be merry?" Edith pressed, trying to catch her off guard.

"Not I. I shall be merrier than I have ever been, now I have got my own way."

"I'm glad you're going to keep one or two vanities, just by way of specimens of the old Adam," Edith conceded, relieved that Margaret wouldn't retreat entirely into austerity.

"But I am going to be just the same, Edith," Margaret insisted, her eyes bright with a newfound clarity. "If you and my aunt could but fancy so. Only as I have neither husband nor child to give me natural duties, I must make myself some, in addition to ordering my gowns."

The family conclave, comprising Edith, her mother, and her husband, quickly concluded that these plans would only serve to cement Margaret's attachment to Henry Lennox. They kept her out of the way of other eligible bachelors, unaware that her unconscious disdain was already sweeping away the lesser admirers attracted by her appearance or her sudden wealth. Henry Lennox, meanwhile, watched her with the keen eye of a lawyer assessing assets. He saw the latent sweep of her mind, convinced it could be guided to embrace all the objects on which he had set his heart. He appreciated Milton's energy and its inhabitants, captivated by their struggle, though he failed to perceive the selfishness that tainted their noble endeavors until Margaret pointed it out with candid honesty.

As the days passed, the distance between them narrowed, not through submission, but through a shared, albeit unequal, recognition of each other's complexity. Neither was prepared to brook the slightest interference in their growing intimacy, though the path ahead remained fraught with the unspoken tensions of a world that valued possession over understanding. Margaret sat once more by the window, watching the rain blur the city lights, feeling a strange tranquility settle over her. She was breathing freely, no longer defined by the shadow of the past, but forging a future where her own will was the compass.

The industrial heart of Milton hammered in a rhythm that defied the natural order, a ceaseless roar of steam and iron struggling perpetually against the sky. Chimneys belched black plumes, and the machinery whirred with a dazzling, senseless fury, driven by crowds whose movements were as mechanical as the gears they tended. Yet, unlike the wood and iron, these people possessed a terrifying vitality; they moved with a frantic, purposeless energy, driven by a hunger for news that tasted like ash in their mouths. There were no loiterers in the streets, no strollers seeking mere pleasure. Every face was etched with the sharp lines of anxiety or avarice, eyes darting between the Mart and the Exchange with the fierce avidity of predators sensing a shift in the wind.

A heavy gloom settled over the town, thicker than the coal dust coating the cobblestones. Trade had faltered; buyers were sparse, and the few who appeared were scrutinized with suspicion by the sellers. Credit was a fragile thing, easily shattered by the tremors rippling from the great neighboring ports, where shipping houses were collapsing like dominoes. Though Milton had hitherto remained untouched by failure, the shadow of American speculation hung low, casting long doubts over local businesses. Whispers circulated in the crowded thoroughfares, a dangerous currency traded in hushed tones. Men avoided naming the doomed, fearing that idle breath might drag the solvent down with the insolvent. *"Thornton is safe,"* they muttered, praising his vast enterprise and his prudent daring. But behind the praise, a darker current flowed. A man would draw another aside, leaning close, whispering that while Thornton's business was large, he had squandered his profits on expansion, leaving him with no capital reserve. His machinery, gleaming and new, was a liability costing a fortune, a gamble that could leave him exposed.

Mr. Harrison, a man of inherited wealth and fearful caution, watched these rumors with a sneer, grudging every penny earned by the bold. But the truth was far more precarious for Mr. Thornton himself. He felt the pressure acutely, not in his pocket, but in the vulnerable fortress of his pride. He viewed himself as the architect of his own destiny, believing that commerce granted every brave, honest man the power to rise above the mundane and command the great game of worldly success. From afar, his name was to be respected, his wishes fulfilled, his word passing like gold. This was the merchant's dream, the ideal his mother had once recited as a trumpet-call to battle: *"Her merchants be like princes."* He had sought to build a legacy, a firm known for generations, alive to distant horizons and dead to immediate needs. It had taken him years of silent toil to glimpse the reality of his station today, standing amidst his own people, in his own town.

For too long, he and his workers had lived parallel lives, close yet never touching, separated by the invisible wall of master and servant. That barrier had begun to crack only recently, sparked by the accidental intimacy of knowing Higgins. Face to face, stripped of titles, they had recognized the shared humanity beneath the skin, that single human heart beating in both chests. Until now, the threat of losing this fragile connection, of seeing his carefully nurtured plans for mutual understanding nipped in the bud, had filled him with a poignant terror. He had never realized how deeply he cared for his role as manufacturer, precisely because it afforded him such intimate contact with a race of people shrewd, ignorant, yet undeniably full of character and strong feeling.

Now, the tide had turned. Bad trade had swept through the market, halving the value of his stock. Orders vanished, locking his capital in idle machinery while expenses drained his reserves relentlessly. Bills for cotton piled up, forcing him to borrow at exorbitant rates, unable to liquidate his assets. Yet, he refused to despair. Day and night, he calculated, foresaw, and prepared, maintaining a calm gentleness at home and a stoic silence in the mill. The men sensed the weight pressing upon him; instead of hostility, they offered a sympathetic silence. Higgins noted the master's suppressed sighs and the way he sat, looking as pale and stiff as a piece of grey calico. Higgins and another worker stayed late, unseen, to finish a neglected task, ensuring the overseer credited the effort to himself.

One evening, as Higgins approached Mr. Thornton in Marlborough Street, he paused, his eyes scanning the employer's weary countenance. *"Eh! I reckon I know who'd ha' been sorry for to see our measter sitting so like a piece o' grey calico!"* he thought, recalling the woeful look on the master's face. He stepped forward, interrupting the hurried stride of his employer.

"Measter," he called out, causing Thornton to stop with a startled annoyance, as if pulled from a trance. "Have yo' heerd aught of Miss Marget lately?"

"Miss—who?" Thornton replied, his brow furrowing.

"Miss Marget—Miss Hale—th' oud parson's daughter," Higgins persisted, his tone respectful despite the familiarity. "Yo' known who I mean well enough—if yo'll only think a bit—"

"Oh yes!" Suddenly, the wintry frost that had bound Thornton's features melted, replaced by a soft, summer-like clarity. Anxiety evaporated, though his lips remained compressed; his eyes smiled benignly. "She's my landlord now, you know, Higgins. I hear of her through her agent here, every now and then. She's well and among friends—thank you, Higgins." The "thank you" lingered, warm and genuine, sending a ripple of hope through the astute observer.

"And she's not getten married, measter?"

"Not yet." The clouds returned instantly. "There is some talk of it, as I understand, with a connection of the family."

"Then she'll not be for coming to Milton again, I reckon."

"No!"

"Stop a minute, measter." Higgins stepped closer, lowering his voice conspiratorially. "Is th' young gentleman cleared?" He emphasized the query with a wink that added layers of mystery to the exchange.

"Th' young gentleman, I mean—Master Frederick, they ca'ed him—her brother as was over here, yo' known."

"Over here."

"Ay, to be sure, at th' missus's death. Yo' need na be feared of my telling; for Mary and me, we knowed it all along, only we held our peace, for we got it through Mary working in th' house."

"And he was over. It was her brother."

"Sure enough, and I reckoned yo' knowed it, or I'd never ha' let on. Yo' knowed she had a brother?"

"Yes, I know all about him. And he was over at Mrs. Hale's death?"

"Nay! I'm not going for to tell more. I've maybe getten them into mischief already, for they kept it very close. I nobbut wanted to know if they'd getten him cleared?"

"Not that I know of. I know nothing. I only hear of Miss Hale, now, as my landlord, and through her lawyer."

Thornton broke away, resuming his business, leaving Higgins baffled. Alone, Thornton reflected, *"It was her brother,"* he whispered to himself. *"I am glad. I may never see her again; but it is a comfort—a relief—to know that much. I knew she could not be unmaidenly; and yet I yearned for conviction. Now I am glad!"*

This small piece of knowledge was a golden thread weaving through the darkening web of his fortunes, which grew ever more ominous. His agent had trusted a failing American house, and the collapse had triggered a chain reaction of failures, toppling others like a house of cards. Could he stand?

Night after night, Thornton retreated to his private room, surrounded by books and papers, staying awake long after his family slept. He believed no one knew of his vigilance. One morning, as dawn filtered through the cracks of his shutters, he sat there, exhausted, resigned to skipping the necessary rest before the day's labor began. The door creaked open, and his mother stood there, dressed exactly as she had been the previous day. Like him, she had refused sleep.

"Mother! why are you not in bed?"

"Son John," she said softly, "do you think I can sleep with an easy mind, while you keep awake full of care? You have not told me what your trouble is; but sore trouble you have had these many days past."

"Trade is bad."

"And you dread——"

"I dread nothing," he replied, lifting his head with rigid determination. "I know that no man will suffer by me. That was my anxiety."

"But how do you stand? Shall you—will it be a failure?" Her voice trembled, breaking its usual steadiness.

"Not a failure. I must give up business, but I pay all men. I might redeem myself—I am sorely tempted—"

"How? Oh, John! keep up your name—try all risks for that. How redeem it?"

"By a speculation offered to me, but full of risk; but, if successful, placing me high above water-mark, so that no one need ever know the strait I am in. Still, if it fails—"

"And if it fails," she interrupted, stepping forward to lay her hand on his arm, her eyes blazing with urgent light. She held her breath, waiting for the conclusion.

"Honest men are ruined by a rogue," he

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the dining hall windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Margaret Hale, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of beeswax, damp wool, and the cloying sweetness of pomegranate blossoms arranged with brutal disregard for color theory.

Edith stood amidst the chaos of preparation, her movements sharp with impatience. She had already rejected Dixon's suggestion of blue flowers against the "dead gold"—a term Edith insisted on using despite the fabric being clearly straw-colored. Now, she held a cluster of brilliant scarlet blooms, forcing them into the neckline of Margaret's gown with a petulant energy that betrayed her own anxiety.

"Oh, Dixon! Not those horrid blue flowers to that dead gold-coloured gown. What taste!" Edith snapped, her voice cutting through the hum of the servants. "Wait a minute, and I will bring you some pomegranate blossoms."

"It's not a dead gold-colour, ma'am. It's a straw-colour. And blue always goes with straw-colour," Dixon replied, her voice tight with suppressed indignation.

But Edith had already secured the crimson garniture. When she stepped back to assess the effect, her gaze darted frantically around the empty room. "Where is Miss Hale?" she demanded, her composure fracturing into a whine. "I can't think how my aunt allowed her to get into such rambling habits in Milton! I'm sure I'm always expecting to hear of her having met with something horrible among all those wretched places she pokes herself into. I should never dare to go down some of those streets without a servant. They're not fit for ladies."

Dixon, still seething over her despised floral choices, offered a curt retort that only fueled Edith's hysteria. "It's no wonder to my mind, when I hear ladies talk such a deal about ladies—and when they're such fearful, delicate, dainty ladies too—I say it's no wonder to me that there are no longer any saints on earth—"

"Oh, Margaret! Here you are!" Edith gasped, rushing forward as if to catch a falling star. "I have been so wanting you. But how your cheeks are flushed with the heat, poor child! But only think what that tiresome Henry has done; really, he exceeds brother-in-law's limits. Just when my party was made up so beautifully—fitted in so precisely for Mr. Colthurst—there has Henry come, with an apology it is true, and making use of your name for an excuse, and asked me if he may bring that Mr. Thornton of Milton—your tenant, you know—who is in London about some law business. It will spoil my number, quite."

"I don't mind dinner. I don't want any," Margaret said, her voice low and steady, though her hands trembled slightly at her sides. "Dixon can get me a cup of tea here, and I will be in the drawing-room by the time you come up. I shall really be glad to lie down."

"No, no! That will never do. You do look wretchedly white, to be sure; but that is just the heat, and we can't do without you possibly." Edith adjusted the flowers, lowering them slightly. "Those flowers a little lower, Dixon. They look glorious flames, Margaret, in your black hair. You know we planned you to talk about Milton to Mr. Colthurst. Oh! To be sure! And this man comes from Milton. I believe it will be capital, after all. Mr. Colthurst can pump him well on all the subjects in which he is interested, and it will be great fun to trace out your experiences, and this Mr. Thornton's wisdom, in Mr. Colthurst's next speech in the House. Really, I think it is a happy hit of Henry's. I asked him if he was a man one would be ashamed of; and he replied, 'Not if you've any sense in you, my little sister.' So I suppose he is able to sound his h's, which is not a common Darkshire accomplishment—eh, Margaret?"

"Mr. Lennox did not say why Mr. Thornton was come up to town? Was it law business connected with the property?" Margaret asked, her voice constrained, pulling her thoughts inward.

"Oh! He's failed, or something of the kind," Edith waved a dismissive hand, her eyes drifting upward. "That Henry told you of that day you had such a headache—what was it? (There, that's capital, Dixon. Miss Hale does us credit, does she not?) I wish I was as tall as a queen, and as brown as a gipsy, Margaret."

"But about Mr. Thornton?"

"Oh! I really have such a terrible head for law business. Henry will like nothing better than to tell you all about it. I know the impression he made upon me was, that Mr. Thornton is very badly off, and a very respectable man, and that I'm to be very civil to him: and as I did not know how, I came to you to ask you to help me. And now come down with me, and rest on the sofa for a quarter of an hour."

The privileged brother-in-law arrived early, bringing with him the weight of social obligation. Margaret, reddening as she spoke, began to ask him the questions she desperately needed answered regarding Mr. Thornton.

"He came up about this sub-letting the property—Marlborough Mills, and the house and premises adjoining, I mean. He is unable to keep it on; and there are deeds and leases to be looked over and agreements to be drawn up. I hope Edith will receive him properly; but she was rather put out, as I could see, by the liberty I had taken in begging for an invitation for him. But I thought you would like to have some attention shown him: and one would be particularly scrupulous in paying every respect to a man who is going down in the world."

He lowered his voice as he addressed Margaret directly, sitting close enough that the intimacy of the moment felt like a physical pressure. But as he finished, he sprang up, introducing Mr. Thornton, who had entered silently at that precise moment, to Edith and Captain Lennox.

Margaret watched him with an anxious intensity. It had been considerably more than a year since she had last seen him, and the passage of time had carved him anew. His fine figure still bore him above the common height of men, granting him a distinguished presence born of natural ease of motion. Yet his face looked older, etched with lines of care that no amount of grooming could erase. Still, a noble composure sat upon his features, impressing those who had just heard of his fallen status with a sense of inherent dignity and manly strength.

He was aware, from the first glance he cast around the room, that Margaret was present. He had seen her intent look of occupation as she listened to Mr. Henry Lennox, and he moved toward her with the perfectly regulated manner of an old friend. With his first calm words, a vivid color flashed into her cheeks, burning there permanently throughout the evening. She seemed to possess little to say to him. She disappointed him by asking, in a quiet monotone, what appeared to him to be merely necessary questions concerning her old acquaintances in Milton. Others arrived—more intimate within the household than he—and he drifted into the background, where he and Mr. Lennox exchanged occasional glances and whispers.

"You think Miss Hale looking well," said Mr. Lennox, leaning casually against the mantelpiece, "don't you? Milton didn't agree with her, I imagine; for when she first came to London, I thought I had never seen anyone so much changed. To-night she is looking radiant. But she is much stronger. Last autumn she was fatigued with a walk of a couple of miles. On Friday evening we walked up to Hampstead and back. Yet on Saturday she looked as well as she does now."

"We!" Who? They two alone?

Mr. Colthurst, a very clever man and a rising member of Parliament, possessed a quick eye for discerning character. He was struck by a remark Mr. Thornton made at dinner-time. He enquired from Edith who that gentleman was; and, to her surprise, she found, from the tone of his "Indeed!", that Mr. Thornton of Milton was not such an unknown name to him as she had imagined it would be.

Her dinner proceeded well. Henry was in good humor, deploying his dry, caustic wit with admirable precision. Mr. Thornton and Mr. Colthurst discovered one or two mutual subjects of interest, touching briefly upon them before reserving deeper discussion for private after-dinner talk. Margaret looked beautiful in the pomegranate flowers; and though she leaned back in her chair and spoke but little, Edith was not annoyed, for the conversation flowed on smoothly without her.

Margaret was watching Mr. Thornton's face. He never looked at her; so she might study him unobserved, noting the subtle shifts that even this short time had wrought in him. Only at some unexpected word from Mr. Lennox did his face flash out into the old look of intense enjoyment: the merry brightness returned to his eyes, the lips just parted to suggest the brilliant smile of former days; and for an instant, his glance instinctively sought hers, as if he wanted her sympathy. But when their eyes met, his whole countenance changed; he became grave and anxious once more; and he resolutely avoided even looking near her again during dinner.

There

The morning air hung heavy with unspoken tension, a silence so profound that Edith moved through the hallway on tiptoe, her footsteps swallowed by the dust motes dancing in the shafts of light. She intercepted Sholto before he could raise his voice, checking every sudden noise as if the slightest disturbance might shatter the fragile conference brewing within the drawing-room. By two o'clock, the clock's tick echoed louder than the thunder rolling overhead; the doors remained shut, sealing the two figures inside in a private orbit of anxiety.

Finally, the rhythm of the house shifted. A man's footstep, urgent and descending, raced down the stairs. Edith peeked out, her eyes wide with anticipation. "Well, Henry?" she asked, her gaze interrogating the space between them.

"Well!" he replied, the word clipped, carrying the weight of exhaustion. "Come in to lunch!"

"No, thank you, I can't. I've lost too much time here already."

"Then it's not all settled?" Edith's shoulders slumped, despair coloring her tone.

"Not at all," Henry answered, shaking his head with finality. "It never *will* be settled, if the 'it' is what I conjecture you mean. That will never be, Edith, so give up thinking about it."

"But it would be so nice for us all," she pleaded, the image of domestic harmony flickering in her mind. "I should always feel comfortable about the children, if I had Margaret settled down near to me. As it is, I am always afraid of her going off to Cadiz."

"I will try, when I marry, to look out for a young lady who has a knowledge of the management of children," Henry said, his voice hollow. "That is all I can do. Miss Hale will not have me. And I shall not ask her."

Edith blinked, confused. "Then what have you been talking about?"

"A thousand things you would not understand: investments, leases, the value of land."

"Oh, go away if that's all," Edith dismissed, waving a hand as if brushing away cobwebs. "You and she will be unbearably stupid, if you've been talking all this time about such weary things."

"Very well. I'm coming again tomorrow, and bringing Mr. Thornton with me, to have some more talk with Miss Hale."

"Mr. Thornton! What has he to do with it?"

"He is Miss Hale's tenant," Mr. Lennox said, turning away, his posture stiff with professional detachment. "And he wishes to give up his lease."

"Oh! Very well. I can't understand details, so don't give them to me."

"The only detail I want you to understand is to let us have the back drawing-room undisturbed, as it was today. In general, the children and servants are so in and out that I can never get any business satisfactorily explained; and the arrangements we have to make tomorrow are of importance."

Yet, fate intervened. No one ever knew why Mr. Lennox failed to keep his appointment the following day. True to the hour, Mr. Thornton arrived, but after keeping him waiting for nearly an hour, Margaret emerged. She looked pale, her complexion washed out by anxiety, her movements jerky with nervous energy.

"I am so sorry Mr. Lennox is not here," she began hurriedly, her voice trembling. "He could have done it so much better than I can. He is my adviser in this—"

"I am sorry that I came, if it troubles you. Shall I go to Mr. Lennox's chambers and try and find him?"

"No, thank you. I wanted to tell you how grieved I was to find that I am to lose you as a tenant. But, Mr. Lennox says, things are sure to brighten—"

"Mr. Lennox knows little about it," Mr. Thornton interrupted, his voice quiet but cutting through the fog of her worry. "Happy and fortunate in all a man cares for, he does not understand what it is to find oneself no longer young—yet thrown back to the starting-point which requires the hopeful energy of youth—to feel one half of life gone, and nothing done. Nothing remaining of wasted opportunity, but the bitter recollection that it has been."

He stepped closer, his presence commanding the room. "Miss Hale, I would rather not hear Mr. Lennox's opinion of my affairs. Those who are happy and successful themselves are too apt to make light of the misfortunes of others."

"You are unjust," Margaret said gently, her eyes searching his face. "Mr. Lennox has only spoken of the great probability which he believes there to be of your redeeming—your more than redeeming what you have lost—don't speak till I have ended—pray don't."

She collected herself, forcing her trembling hands to turn over law papers and statements of account with frantic speed. "Oh! Here it is! And—he drew me out a proposal. I wish he was here to explain it. Showing that if you would take some money of mine, eighteen hundred and fifty-seven pounds, lying just at this moment unused in the bank, and bringing me in only two and a half per cent—you could pay me much better interest, and might go on working Marlborough Mills."

Her voice had cleared, steadying into the cadence of commerce. Mr. Thornton said nothing, watching her intently. She continued scanning the documents, desperate to frame this transaction purely as a business arrangement, where the principal advantage lay squarely on her side. Yet, as she searched for the specific paper outlining the terms of security, her heart seized. The air in the room changed, charged with a static electricity that made the hair on her arms stand up.

His voice broke the silence, hoarse and trembling with a tenderness that terrified her.

"Margaret!"

For an instant, she looked up, her luminous eyes meeting his, before she instinctively sought to veil them, dropping her forehead onto her hands. Again, he stepped nearer, his desperation manifesting in another tremulous call.

"Margaret!"

Lower still went her head; more completely hidden became her face, almost resting on the table before her. He came close, invading her personal space until there was no escape. He knelt by her side, lowering his face to a level with her ear, whispering words that seemed to vibrate through the floorboards:

"Take care.—If you do not speak—I shall claim you as my own in some strange presumptuous way.—Send me away at once, if I must go;—Margaret!"

At that third call, she turned her face, still covered with her small white hands, towards him. She laid it on his shoulder, hiding it there, burying herself in the warmth of his coat. It was too delicious to feel her soft cheek against his for him to wish to see either deep blushes or loving eyes. He clasped her close, holding her as if she were the only solid thing in a dissolving world. But they both kept silence, the only sound the ragged breathing of two souls colliding.

At length, she murmured in a broken voice, pulling back slightly. "Oh, Mr. Thornton, I am not good enough!"

"Not good enough! Don't mock my own deep feeling of unworthiness."

After a minute or two, he gently disengaged her hands from her face, laying her arms as they had once before been placed to protect him from the rioters.

"Do you remember, love?" he murmured, his voice rough with emotion. "And how I requited you with my insolence the next day?"

"I remember how wrongly I spoke to you,—that is all."

"Look here! Lift up your head. I have something to show you!"

She slowly faced him, glowing with a beautiful, burning shame.

"Do you know these roses?" he said, drawing out his pocket-book, in which were treasured up some dried, faded flowers.

"No!" she replied, with innocent curiosity.

"Did I give them to you?"

"No! Vanity; you did not. You may have worn sister roses very probably."

She looked at them, wondering for a minute, then she smiled a little as she said—

"They are from Helstone, are they not? I know the deep indentations round the leaves. Oh! Have you been there? When were you there?"

"I wanted to see the place where Margaret grew to what she is, even at the worst time of all, when I had no hope of ever calling her mine. I went there on my return from Havre."

"You must give them to me," she said, trying to take them out of his hand with gentle violence.

"Very well. Only you must pay me for them!"

"How shall I ever tell Aunt Shaw?" she whispered, after some time of delicious silence.

"Let me speak to her."

"Oh, no! I owe to her,—but what will she say?"

"I can guess. Her first exclamation will be, 'That man!'"

"Hush!" said Margaret, leaning in close, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. "Or I shall try and show you your mother's indignant tones as she says, 'That woman!'"
